M^{\{I_{1}\cdots I_{2S}\}}=\lambda_{\alpha_{1}}^{I_{1}}\cdots\lambda_{\alpha_{2S}}^{I_{2S}}M^{\{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{2S}\}}

(4.2)

where
M^{\{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{2S}\}}
is totally symmetric in the
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
indices.

4.1
Massless Three-Particle Amplitudes

4.1.1
Complex Momenta

The discussion of three-point amplitudes in the on-shell formalism is quite delicate. Consider a three-point scattering amplitude involving only massless particle. For simplicity we will consider all particles to be incoming.
By kinematic considerations, this configuration should be impossible:

p_{1}^{\mu}+p_{2}^{\mu}+p_{3}^{\mu}=0,\quad p_{1}^{2}=p_{2}^{2}=p_{3}^{2}=0\Rightarrow s_{12}=(p_{1}+p_{2})^{2}=p_{3}^{2}=0,\,s_{13}=0,\,s_{23}=0

(4.3)

Accordingly,
\langle ij\rangle=[ij]=0
with
i,j=1,2,3
.
Therefore any possible three-point amplitude is seemingly null.

This results follows from the assumption that momenta are real.
If we allow momenta to be complex there is a way out: for complex momenta,
\tilde{\lambda}_{\dot{\alpha}}
is not the hermitian conjugate of
\lambda_{\alpha}
, accordingly,
[pk]
is not the complex conjugate of
\langle kp\rangle
, although
s_{pk}=2p\cdot k=\langle pk\rangle[kp]
still holds. Let us formalize this intuition.

For a massless three-particle scattering, momentum conservation reads
p_{1}^{\mu}+p_{2}^{\mu}+p_{3}^{\mu}=0
,
i.e.

|1\rangle[1|+|2\rangle[2|+|3\rangle[3|=0\,.

(4.4)

With complex momenta, two chirally conjugate solutions exist, obtained by multiplying Eq. (
4.4
) by either
\langle 1|
or
\langle 2|
:

\langle 12\rangle[2|+\langle 13\rangle[3|=0\,,\qquad\langle 21\rangle[1|+\langle 23\rangle[3|=0\,;

(4.5)

then for
\langle ij\rangle\neq 0
we obtain

[2|=-\frac{\langle 13\rangle}{\langle 12\rangle}[3|\,,\qquad[3|=-\frac{\langle 21\rangle}{\langle 23\rangle}[1|\,,

(4.6)

hence
[1|\propto[2|\propto[3|
.
This result implies that
[12]=[13]=[23]=0
.

To recap, if we work with complex momenta and assume
\langle ij\rangle\neq 0
for
i,j=1,2,3
,
[1|\propto[2|\propto[3|
,
thus
[ij]=0
and
s_{ij}=0
. Similarly, if we assume
[ij]\neq 0
for
i,j=1,2,3
,
\langle 1|\propto\langle 2|\propto\langle 3|
,
thus
\langle ij\rangle=0
and
s_{ij}=0
.

Therefore, if we work with complex momenta, a solution exists but it must be constructed out of either only right-handed spinors or only left-handed ones, but not both.

4.1.2
Massless Three-Particle Amplitudes

We can now compute the desired three-point amplitudes for massless particles of any spin using just the little group scaling and the above results.
We recall that under little group scaling,

\displaystyle|p\rangle\rightarrow w^{-1}|p\rangle\,,\quad\langle p|\rightarrow w^{-1}\langle p|\,,\qquad|p]\rightarrow w|p]\,,\quad[p|\rightarrow w[p|\,,

(4.7)

with
w\text{in}U(1)
; and the polarization vectors scale as

\varepsilon_{+}^{\mu}(p,k)\equiv\frac{\langle k\sigma^{\mu}p]}{\sqrt{2}\langle kp\rangle}\rightarrow w^{2}\varepsilon_{+}^{\mu}(p,k)\,,\quad\varepsilon_{-}^{\mu}(p,k)\equiv\frac{[k\sigma^{\mu}p\rangle}{\sqrt{2}[kp]}\rightarrow w^{-2}\varepsilon_{+}^{\mu}(p,k).

(4.8)

The polarization tensor of any massless particles is built out of the outer product of such polarization vectors, therefore Eq. (
4.8
) can be generalized to

\varepsilon_{\pm}^{\mu}(p,k)\rightarrow w^{\pm 2h}\varepsilon_{\pm}^{\mu}(p,k)\,,

(4.9)

where
h
is the helicity of the desired particle.

We now consider a massless three-point amplitude. After applying a little group transformation to all three particles, the amplitude must scale as

M(1,2,3)\rightarrow\Bigl(\prod_{i=1}^{3}w^{2h_{i}}\Bigr)M(1,2,3)\,,

(4.10)

where
h_{i}
is the helicity of the i-th massless particle.

We recall that three-point amplitudes, in order not to vanish, must be functions of either only
\langle ij\rangle
or
[ij]
, so we can parametrize them as

M_{3}(1^{h_{1}},2^{h_{2}},3^{h_{3}})=M_{\langle\cdot\rangle}(1^{h_{1}},2^{h_{2}},3^{h_{3}})+M_{[\cdot]}(1^{h_{1}},2^{h_{2}},3^{h_{3}})\,,

(4.11)

where
M_{\langle\cdot\rangle}
and
M_{[\cdot]}
are functions of the spinor products of the form:

\begin{cases}M_{\langle\cdot\rangle}=A_{abc}\langle 12\rangle^{x_{12}}\langle 23\rangle^{x_{23}}\langle 13\rangle^{x_{13}}\\
M_{[\cdot]}=B_{abc}[12]^{y_{12}}[23]^{y_{23}}[13]^{y_{13}}\\
\end{cases}

(4.12)

and where
A_{abc}
and
B_{abc}
are couplings, eventually dimensionful, in which we include internal degrees of freedom (
e.g.
colour for QCD).

We now apply little group transformation individually for each particle and impose Eq. (
4.10
) and Eq. (
4.11
).
We obtain the following system of equations:

\begin{cases}y_{12}+y_{13}=2h_{1}\\
y_{12}+y_{23}=2h_{2}\\
y_{13}+y_{23}=2h_{3}\end{cases}\Rightarrow\begin{cases}y_{12}=h_{1}+h_{2}-h_{3}\\
y_{13}=h_{1}-h_{2}+h_{3}\\
y_{23}=-h_{1}+h_{2}+h_{3}\\
\end{cases}

(4.13)

Likewise,

\begin{cases}x_{12}+x_{13}=-2h_{1}\\
x_{12}+x_{23}=-2h_{2}\\
x_{13}+x_{23}=-2h_{3}\end{cases}\Rightarrow\begin{cases}x_{12}=-y_{12}=-h_{1}-h_{2}+h_{3}\\
x_{13}=-y_{13}=-h_{1}+h_{2}-h_{3}\\
x_{23}=-y_{23}=h_{1}-h_{2}-h_{3}\\
\end{cases}

(4.14)

Thus, little group scaling fixes uniquely the dependence of
M_{\langle\cdot\rangle}
and
M_{[\cdot]}
on the spinor products:

\begin{cases}M_{\langle\cdot\rangle}=A_{abc}\langle 12\rangle^{-h_{1}-h_{2}+h_{3}}\langle 23\rangle^{h_{1}-h_{2}-h_{3}}\langle 13\rangle^{-h_{1}+h_{2}-h_{3}}\\
M_{[\cdot]}=B_{abc}[12]^{h_{1}+h_{2}-h_{3}}[23]^{-h_{1}+h_{2}+h_{3}}[13]^{h_{1}-h_{2}+h_{3}}\\
\end{cases}

(4.15)

Now, the three-point amplitude
M_{3}
must have the correct physical behaviour for real momenta,
i.e.
it must vanish when both
\langle ij\rangle
and
[ij]
go to zero.

Since
x_{12}+x_{13}+x_{23}=-(y_{12}+y_{13}+y_{23})=-(h_{1}+h_{2}+h_{3})
, for
h_{1}+h_{2}+h_{3}>0
,
A_{abc}
must be zero in order to have a sensible result, otherwise
M_{\langle\cdot\rangle}
would blow up. Conversely, for
h_{1}+h_{2}+h_{3}<0
,
B_{abc}
must vanish, otherwise
M_{[\cdot]}
would blow up.

So we can write in general:

M^{h_{1}h_{2}h_{3}}=\begin{cases}B_{abc}[12]^{h_{1}+h_{2}-h_{3}}[23]^{h_{2}+h_{3}-h_{1}}[31]^{h_{3}+h_{1}-h_{2}}\qquad{\rm for}\,h_{1}+h_{2}+h_{3}>0\\
A_{abc}\langle 12\rangle^{h_{3}-h_{1}-h_{2}}\langle 23\rangle^{h_{1}-h_{2}-h_{3}}\langle 31\rangle^{h_{2}-h_{3}-h_{1}}\!\!\!\!\qquad{\rm for}\,h_{1}+h_{2}+h_{3}<0\end{cases}

(4.16)

Notice that this treatment is not suitable to describe the case
h_{1}+h_{2}+h_{3}=0
, which require a specific treatment depending on the individual process.

Finally, using Bose symmetry it is possible to show that for odd-(integer)-spin particles
A_{abc}
and
B_{abc}
must be completely antisymmetric, mimicking the role of the structure constants
f_{abc}
. For even-(integer)-spin particles we do not have such requirement.

If we now consider a theory of a single self-interacting particle of spin-s, the only possible three-point amplitudes are:

\displaystyle M_{3}(1_{a}^{-s},2_{b}^{-s},3_{c}^{+s})=A_{abc}\biggl(\frac{\langle 12\rangle^{3}}{\langle 23\rangle\langle 31\rangle}\biggr)^{s}\,,\quad M_{3}(1_{a}^{+s},2_{b}^{+s},3_{c}^{-s})=B_{abc}\biggl(\frac{[12]^{3}}{[23][31]}\biggr)^{s}\,,

(4.17)

\displaystyle M_{3}(1_{a}^{-s},2_{b}^{-s},3_{c}^{-s})=A_{abc}\bigl(\langle 12\rangle\langle 23\rangle\langle 31\rangle\bigr)^{s}\,,\quad M_{3}(1_{a}^{+s},2_{b}^{+s},3_{c}^{+s})=B_{abc}\bigl([12][23][31]\bigr)^{s}\,.

Furthermore, for parity invariant theories, such as gravity, parity symmetry requires
A_{abc}=B_{abc}
.

We finally focus on pure gravity amplitudes. In D=4 dimensions,
[M_{n}]=4-n
, the gravitational coupling is
(M_{PL})^{-1}=\sqrt{32\pi G}=\kappa/2
, so it has mass dimensions
-1
.
Since the spinor products have mass dimension 1, then
[M_{3}(1^{\mp s},2^{\mp s},3^{\pm s})]=1
implies that
[A_{abc}]=[B_{abc}]=-1
, which is precisely the dimension of the gravitational coupling.
On the other hand,
[M_{3}(1^{\pm s},2^{\pm s},3^{\pm s})]=1
implies that
[A_{abc}]=[B_{abc}]=-5
. Even taking into account the dimensionful coupling, we need at least four momenta to justify this scaling. In a renormalization picture, this suggests that such amplitudes are not really “elementary” but are rather the result of composite operators, hence we can neglect them on the basis of dimensional analysis.

To sum up the only relevant amplitudes in pure gravity are:

M_{3}(1^{-2},2^{-2},3^{+2})=\frac{\kappa}{2}\frac{\langle 12\rangle^{6}}{\langle 23\rangle^{2}\langle 31\rangle^{2}}\,,\quad M_{3}(1^{+2},2^{+2},3^{-2})=\frac{\kappa}{2}\frac{[12]^{6}}{[23]^{2}[31]^{2}}\,,.\\

(4.18)

4.2
Three-Particle Amplitudes Involving Massive Particles

I will now study the case of amplitudes involving massive particles.

As discussed in Sec.
4
, the amplitude will be labeled by the spin-S representation of the SU(2) little group for massive legs and helicities for the massless legs.

We recall that, as the Little group is defined for each individual momenta separately, only the spinor variables of a given leg can carry its Little group index.
For amplitudes involving massive legs, it will be convenient to expand in terms of only
\lambda^{I}_{\alpha}
, since any dependence on
\tilde{\lambda}^{I}_{\dot{\alpha}}
can be converted using Dirac equation, Eq. (
2.19
).
Thus we can pull out overall factors of
\lambda_{i}^{I}
from the amplitude,

M_{n}^{\cdots\{I_{1},I_{2},\cdots,I_{2s_{i}}\}\cdots}=\lambda^{I_{1}}_{i\,\alpha_{1}}\lambda^{I_{2}}_{i\,\alpha_{2}}\cdots\lambda^{I_{2s_{i}}}_{i\,\alpha_{2s_{i}}}M_{n}^{\cdots\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\cdots,\alpha_{2s_{i}}\}\cdots}\;.

(4.19)

leaving behind a function that is symmetric in
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
indices instead.
We will refer to this representation as the chiral basis, reflecting the fact that we are using the un-dotted
SL(2,\mathbb{C})

indices. Notice that one may equally use the anti-chiral basis.

For a three-point amplitude, we have three possible momenta, related by momentum conservation. As the amplitude is now just a tensor in the
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
Lorentz indices, the problem reduces to finding two linear independent 2-component spinors that span this space, which
we will denote as
(v_{\alpha},u_{\alpha})
.

The full categorization of three-point amplitude with arbitrary masses is discussed in detail in Ref.
[
98
]
, however I will focus only on the case of one-massless two-massive particles, and in particular on the equal mass case.

4.2.1
Three Particle Amplitudes, one-massless two-massive

For two massive legs, the three-point amplitude is labeled by
(h,S_{1},S_{2})

\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={0.5}]{amp32.jpg}}}\quad M^{h}\,_{\{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\cdots\alpha_{2S_{1}}\},\,\{\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\cdots\beta_{2S_{2}}\}}

(4.20)

where h is the helicity of the massless leg
3
3

3

Figure reproduced from Ref.
[
98
]
.
. Now we have a
\{2s\}\otimes\{2s\}\,SL(2,\mathbb{C})
tensor, and we are interested in the general structure of all possible couplings. As mentioned above, this entails the need of a basis to span the two-dimensional space. It is preferable to use the kinematic variables of
the problem to serve as a basis.

For unequal mass, one of the basis spinor can be
\lambda
of the massless leg, while the remaining can be chosen to be
\tilde{\lambda}
contracted with one of the massive momentum. For example one can choose:

\left(v_{\alpha},u_{\alpha}\right)=\left(\lambda_{3\alpha},\;\frac{p_{1\alpha\dot{\beta}}}{m_{1}}\tilde{\lambda}_{3}^{\dot{\beta}}\right)\,,

(4.21)

where
p_{1}
is the momentum of one of the massive particles, and
p_{3}
the momentum of the massless one.

From now on we will focus on the equal mass case.

If the masses are identical, then
u
and
v
are no longer independent. Indeed, using momentum conservation,

p_{1}+p_{2}+p_{3}=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad p_{1}+p_{3}=-p_{2}\quad\Rightarrow\quad p^{2}_{1}+p^{2}_{3}+2p_{1}\cdot p_{3}=p^{2}_{2}\,,

(4.22)

and, as we are on-shell,
p^{2}_{1}=p^{2}_{2}=m^{2},\,p^{2}_{3}=0
. Thus

0=\frac{2p_{3}\cdot p_{1}}{m}=\frac{\langle 3|p_{1}|3]}{m}\equiv v^{\alpha}u_{\alpha}\,,

(4.23)

i.e.
the spinor
\lambda_{3}^{\alpha}
must be proportional to
\tilde{\lambda}_{3\dot{\alpha}}p_{1}^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}
.

Taking into account this result, we introduce as the expansion basis

\lambda_{3\alpha},\;\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}\,,

(4.24)

where
\lambda_{3\alpha}
is once again the chiral spinor of the massless leg, while
\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}
is the Levi-Civita symbol.

Notice however that
\lambda_{3}
carries helicity weight
-\frac{1}{2}
of the massless leg. In order for amplitude to be little group invariant, one should also have a variable that carries positive weights. We call this variable “
x
”, and it is defined as the (dimensionless) proportionality constant between
\lambda_{3\alpha}
and
p_{1\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\tilde{\lambda}_{3}^{\dot{\alpha}}
:

x\lambda_{3\alpha}=\frac{p_{1\alpha\dot{\alpha}}}{m}\tilde{\lambda}_{3}^{\dot{\alpha}}\,,\quad x^{-1}\tilde{\lambda}^{\dot{\alpha}}_{3}=\frac{p_{1}^{\dot{\alpha}\alpha}\lambda_{3\alpha}}{m}\,.

(4.25)

Note that
x
carries
+1
little group weight of the massless leg. Furthermore,
x
cannot be expressed in a manifestly local way. Indeed contracting both sides of the above equation with a reference spinor
\zeta
yields:

x=\frac{\langle\zeta|p_{1}|3]}{m\langle\zeta 3\rangle},

(4.26)

so while
x
is independent of
\zeta
, any concrete expression for it has an apparent, spurious pole in
\zeta
.

The above shows that
x
can be nicely written in terms of polarization vectors:

mx=\sqrt{2}\,\varepsilon^{+}_{\mu}\,p_{1}^{\mu}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,\varepsilon^{+}_{\mu}(p_{1}-p_{2})^{\mu}

(4.27)

where the auxiliary spinor is identified with the reference spinor of the polarization vector.

Equipped with this new variable, we can write down the general structure of a three point amplitude for two spin
s
and a helicity
h
state: the only objects we have carrying
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
indices are
\lambda_{3\alpha}
and
\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}
, thus

M_{3}^{h,\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{2s}\},\{\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{2s}\}}=(mx)^{h}\left[\sum_{a=0}^{2s}g_{a}\epsilon^{2s-a}\biggl(x\frac{\lambda_{3}\lambda_{3}}{m}\biggr)^{\!a}\right]^{\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{2s}\},\{\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{2s}\}}\,,

(4.28)

where the
\{2s\}\otimes\{2s\}
separately symmetrized
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
indices are distributed across the Levi-Civita tensors
\epsilon
and the
\lambda_{3}
. Thus we see that there are in total
2s{+}1
structures for spin
s
states, and we have normalized the couplings such that the
g_{i}
are dimensionless.

It will be convenient to make connection with the amplitudes computed from the usual Feynman diagram approach. For this, we simply put back the
\lambda_{i}^{I}
factors that was pulled out that defined the chiral basis in Eq. (
4.19
). For example,

\displaystyle M_{3}^{h,s,s}

\displaystyle=(mx)^{h}\left[g_{0}\frac{\langle\mathbf{2}\mathbf{1}\rangle^{2s}}{m^{2s-1}}+g_{1}x\frac{\langle\mathbf{2}\mathbf{1}\rangle^{2s-1}\langle\mathbf{2}3\rangle\langle 3\mathbf{1}\rangle}{m^{2s}}+\dots+g_{2s}x^{2s}\frac{\langle\mathbf{2}3\rangle^{2s}\langle 3\mathbf{1}\rangle^{2s}}{m^{4s-1}}\right]\,.

(4.29)

4.2.2
The simplest three-point Amplitude

In the previous section, we have seen that for a massive spin-
s
particle, whether it is fundamental or composite, the emission of a photon or graviton can in general be parameterized by Eq. (
4.29
).
This parameterization is unique in the sense that the expansion basis is defined on kinematic grounds unambiguously. The expansion is organized in terms of powers of
1/m
, with higher order terms hinting at potential problems in the UV region,
i.e.
in the
m\rightarrow 0
limit.

Consider an amplitude with only the leading term in Eq. (
4.29
). The above discussion would indicate that not only is the amplitude simple in the number of terms involved, but is also simple in the sense of having the best UV behavior: at high energies our massive states behave, at leading order, as massless ones, and we can ask what amplitude in the UV does this pure
x
-piece matches to.
The two massive spin-
s
states will translate into a
+s
and
-s
helicity state separately at high energies. To avoid a singular piece we must have
\lambda_{1}\sim\lambda_{2}\sim\lambda_{3}
. Thus minimal coupling in the UV uniquely picks out

M_{UV}^{h,s,s}=\frac{g_{0}}{m}\,(mx)^{h}\left(\frac{\langle\mathbf{1}\mathbf{2}\rangle}{m}\right)^{2s}

(4.30)

For this reason it is natural to refer to the choice of setting all coupling constants except
g_{0}
to zero as
minimal coupling
. One can straightforwardly check that subleading terms in Eq. (
4.29
) match to higher derivative couplings in the UV, see Ref.
[
99
]
.

The minimal coupling three-point amplitude of a graviton with two massive spin-
s
particles is given by:

M^{+2,s,s}_{3}=x^{2}\,\frac{\kappa}{2}\frac{\langle\mathbf{1}\mathbf{2}\rangle^{2s}}{m^{2s-2}},\qquad M^{-2,s,s}_{3}=x^{-2}\,\frac{\kappa}{2}\frac{[\mathbf{1}\mathbf{2}]^{2s}}{m^{2s-2}}

(4.31)

4.2.3
Classical Spin-operators from Amplitudes

Following Ref.
[
99
,
100
]
, we see that it is very convenient to view the three-point amplitude as an operator acting on the Hilbert space of
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
irreps; in particular this operator
maps the spin-
s
representation in the Hilbert space of particle 1 to that of particle 2,
i.e.

M^{+h,s,s}=\frac{1}{m^{2s-1}}\lambda^{I_{1}\alpha_{1}}_{1}\cdots\lambda^{I_{2s}\alpha_{2s}}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{2s}\},\{\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{2s}\}}\lambda^{J_{1}\beta_{1}}_{2}\cdots\lambda^{J_{2s}\beta_{2s}}_{2}

(4.32)

The operator
\mathcal{O}_{\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{2s}\},\{\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{2s}\}}
is a linear combination of polynomials in
\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}
and
\lambda_{3\alpha}\lambda_{3\beta}
, see Eq. (
4.28
).
As we will now demonstrate, the former can be naturally identified as the identity operator, while the latter as the
spin-operator
.

The spin vector
S^{\mu}
is defined, in the Hilbert space of our spinors, as the Pauli-Lubanski pseudo-vector
S^{\mu}=-\frac{1}{2m}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}p_{1\nu}J_{\rho\sigma}
.
We now focus on the Lorentz generator
J_{\mu\nu}
.
Using the conventions explained in App. (
A
), it is easy to check that

(J^{\mu\nu})_{\text{spinor}}=\frac{i}{4}[\gamma^{\mu},\gamma^{\nu}]=\frac{i}{2}\begin{pmatrix}(\sigma^{[\mu}\bar{\sigma}^{\nu]})_{\alpha}{}^{\beta}&0\\
0&(\bar{\sigma}^{[\mu}\sigma^{\nu]})^{\dot{\alpha}}{}_{\dot{\beta}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}(J^{\mu\nu})_{\alpha}{}^{\beta}&0\\
0&(J^{\mu\nu})^{\dot{\alpha}}{}_{\dot{\beta}}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(4.33)

forms a representation of the Lorentz algebra.
We are now interested in its action on
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
irreps.
For spin-
s
, using the fact that the irreps are symmetric tensors of the 2s indices, it is possible to write:

(J_{\mu\nu})_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\cdots\alpha_{2s}}\,^{\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\cdots\beta_{2s}}=\sum_{i=1}^{2s}(J_{\mu\nu})_{\alpha_{i}}\,^{\beta_{i}}\,\bar{{\hbox to0.0pt{1\hss}\hskip 1.6pt\hbox{1}}}_{i}=2s(J_{\mu\nu})_{\alpha_{1}}\,^{\beta_{1}}\,\bar{{\hbox to0.0pt{1\hss}\hskip 1.6pt\hbox{1}}}_{1},\quad(J_{\mu\nu})_{\alpha}\,^{\beta}=\frac{i}{2}\sigma_{[\mu}\bar{\sigma}_{\nu]}\,,

(4.34)

where
\bar{{\hbox to0.0pt{1\hss}\hskip 1.6pt\hbox{1}}}_{i}=\delta_{\alpha_{1}}^{\beta_{1}}\cdots\delta_{\alpha_{i{-}1}}^{\beta_{i{-}1}}\cancel{\delta_{\alpha_{i}}^{\beta_{i}}}\delta_{\alpha_{i{+}1}}^{\beta_{i{+}1}}\cdots\delta_{\alpha_{2s}}^{\beta_{2s}}
, and I have used the symmetry of the 2s indices to pick, without loss of generality, 2s-copies of the tensor
(J_{\mu\nu})_{\alpha_{1}}\,^{\beta_{1}}
.
Using this, we find that

\begin{cases}m\left(S_{\mu}\right)_{\alpha}^{~\beta}&=\frac{1}{4}\left[\sigma_{\mu}(p_{1}\cdot\bar{\sigma})-(p_{1}\cdot\sigma)\bar{\sigma}_{\mu}\right]_{\alpha}^{~\beta}\\
m\left(S_{\mu}\right)^{\dot{\alpha}}_{~\dot{\beta}}&=-\frac{1}{4}\left[\bar{\sigma}_{\mu}(p_{1}\cdot\sigma)-(p_{1}\cdot\bar{\sigma})\sigma_{\mu}\right]^{\dot{\alpha}}_{~\dot{\beta}}\end{cases}\,.

(4.35)

If we now multiply each side of these equations by the massless momentum
p_{3}
, we obtain:

(p_{3}\cdot S)_{\alpha}^{~\beta}=\frac{x}{2}\lambda_{3\alpha}\lambda_{3}^{\beta}\equiv\frac{x}{2}|3\rangle\langle 3|\,,\qquad(p_{3}\cdot S)^{\dot{\alpha}}_{~\dot{\beta}}=-\frac{\lambda_{3}^{\dot{\alpha}}\lambda_{3\dot{\beta}}}{2x}\equiv-\frac{|3][3|}{2x}\,.

(4.36)

Therefore, the operator
\mathcal{O}_{\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{s}\},\{\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{s}\}}
is comprised of identity operators and spin vector operators.

4.3
Multipole expansion of three-point amplitudes

In this section, I will introduce the key steps in the multipole expansion of three-point amplitudes. This methodology becomes crucial in the analysis of Kerr black holes scattering, as it organizes our amplitudes in an extremely convenient way in angular momentum multipoles.

In this section, I will use the latin letters
a
,
b
for the massive little group indices.

Prior to delving into the specifics, we may construct the spin-
s
external wavefunctions, using the relation

p^{\mu}=\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\dot{\beta}}p^{\alpha\dot{\beta}}=\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{\alpha}_{a}\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\dot{\beta}}\tilde{\lambda}^{\dot{\beta}}_{a}=\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathbf{p}_{a}|\sigma^{\mu}|\mathbf{p}^{a}]
, as

\varepsilon_{p,\mu}^{ab}=\frac{\langle\mathbf{p}^{(a}|\sigma_{\mu}|\mathbf{p}^{b)}]}{\sqrt{2}m}\,,

(4.37)

where the round brackets indicate a symmetrization. With this definition, the following relations hold:

p\cdot\varepsilon_{p}^{ab}=0\,,\qquad\varepsilon^{ab}_{p,\mu}\varepsilon_{p,\nu\,ab}=\eta_{\mu\nu}-\frac{p_{\mu}p_{\nu}}{m^{2}}\,,\qquad\varepsilon_{p,11}\cdot\varepsilon_{p}^{11}=\varepsilon_{p,22}\cdot\varepsilon_{p}^{22}=2\varepsilon_{p,12}\cdot\varepsilon_{p}^{12}=1\,.

(4.38)

4.3.1
Massive spin-1 Matter

To study the details of the multipole expansion it is instructive to consider the simple case of graviton emission by two massive vector fields.

Consider the massive spin-1 Lagrangian

{\cal L}=-\frac{1}{4}F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}+\dfrac{1}{2}m^{2}A_{\mu}A^{\mu},

(4.39)

where
F^{\mu\nu}
is the field strength
F^{\mu\nu}=\partial^{\mu}A^{\nu}-\partial^{\nu}A^{\mu}
.

To compute the energy-momentum tensor in flat space, we can apply Noether’s theorem for translations, which gives us the following result:

T_{N}^{\mu\nu}=-F^{\mu\sigma}\partial^{\nu}A_{\sigma}-\eta^{\mu\nu}{\cal L}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\partial_{\mu}T_{N}^{\mu\nu}=0.

(4.40)

However, its contraction with an on-shell graviton,
\varepsilon_{\mu\nu}T_{N}^{\mu\nu}
, fails to produce the correct three-point amplitude (since
\partial_{\nu}T_{N}^{\mu\nu}\neq 0
); consequently, the (dual) orbital angular momentum

L^{\lambda\mu\nu}=x^{\mu}T_{N}^{\lambda\nu}-x^{\nu}T_{N}^{\lambda\mu}

(4.41)

is not conserved.
Notice however that it is possible to generalize
T_{N}^{\mu\nu}
to a larger class of tensors that still satisfy Eq. (
4.40
):

T^{\mu\nu}=T_{N}^{\mu\nu}+\partial_{\lambda}B^{\lambda\mu\,\nu},\qquad\quad B^{\lambda\mu\,\nu}=-B^{\mu\lambda\,\nu}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\partial_{\mu}T^{\mu\nu}=0,

(4.42)

furthermore the Belinfante tensor
B^{\mu\nu\rho}
may be adjusted to yield a symmetric energy- momentum tensor matching the gravitational one.

To ensure the conservation of total angular momentum, we can then apply Noether’s theorem to Lorentz transformations, which gives:

T_{N}^{\mu\nu}-T_{N}^{\nu\mu}=-\partial_{\lambda}J^{\lambda\,\mu\nu},\qquad\quad J^{\lambda\,\mu\nu}=-i\frac{\partial{\cal L}}{\partial(\partial_{\lambda}A^{\sigma})}\Sigma^{\mu\nu,\sigma}_{~~~~\tau}A^{\tau}=iF^{\lambda\sigma}\Sigma^{\mu\nu}_{\sigma\tau}A^{\tau}\,,

(4.43)

where
\Sigma_{\mu\nu}
are the Lorentz generators

\Sigma^{\mu\nu,\sigma}_{~~~~\tau}=i[\eta^{\mu\sigma}\delta^{\nu}_{\tau}-\eta^{\nu\sigma}\delta^{\mu}_{\tau}]
.

We may now substitute Eq. (
4.42
) into Eq. (
4.43
), and after imposing that
T^{\mu\nu}
is symmetric, we obtain the following expression:

\partial_{\lambda}B^{\lambda[\mu\,\nu]}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\lambda}J^{\lambda\,\mu\nu}\,,

(4.44)

which is solved by

B^{\lambda\mu\,\nu}=\frac{1}{2}\big[J^{\lambda\,\mu\nu}+J^{\mu\,\nu\lambda}-J^{\nu\,\lambda\mu}\big].

(4.45)

Contracting the resulting energy-momentum tensor with a traceless symmetric graviton
h_{\mu\nu}
and integrating by parts, we obtain the gravitational interaction vertex

-h_{\mu\nu}T^{\mu\nu}=h_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\sigma}\partial^{\nu}A_{\sigma}-i(\partial_{\lambda}h_{\mu\nu})F^{\nu\sigma}\Sigma^{\lambda\mu}_{\sigma\tau}A^{\tau}\,.

(4.46)

The corresponding three-particle amplitude reads (for more details see Ref.
[
100
]
):

{\cal M}_{3}(p_{1},p_{2},k)=-2(p\cdot\varepsilon)\big[(p\cdot\varepsilon)(\varepsilon_{1}\!\cdot\varepsilon_{2})-2k_{\mu}\varepsilon_{\nu}\varepsilon_{1}^{[\mu}\varepsilon_{2}^{\nu]}\big],\qquad\text{with}\,\,p=\frac{1}{2}(p_{1}-p_{2}).

(4.47)

Naively, we may interpret the combination
\varepsilon_{1}^{[\mu}\varepsilon_{2}^{\nu]}
as being proportional to the classical spin tensor.
However, the spin-1 amplitude contains up to quadratic spin interactions (see Ref.
[
101
]
), whereas only the linear piece is apparent in Eq. (
4.47
).
To rewrite this contribution in terms of multipoles, we can use a redefined spin tensor (see App. (
B
)):

S^{\mu\nu}=-\frac{i}{\varepsilon_{1}\!\cdot\varepsilon_{2}}\bigg\{2\varepsilon_{1}^{[\mu}\varepsilon_{2}^{\nu]}-\frac{1}{m^{2}}p^{[\mu}\big((k\cdot\varepsilon_{2})\varepsilon_{1}+(k\cdot\varepsilon_{1})\varepsilon_{2}\big)^{\nu]}\bigg\}\,.

(4.48)

Inserting this spin tensor in Eq. (
4.47
), we rewrite the above amplitude as

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{3}(p_{1},p_{2},k^{\pm})

\displaystyle=-m^{2}x_{\pm}^{2}(\varepsilon_{1}\!\cdot\varepsilon_{2})\bigg[1-\frac{i\sqrt{2}}{mx_{\pm}}k_{\mu}\varepsilon^{\pm}_{\nu}S^{\mu\nu}+\frac{(k\cdot\varepsilon_{1})(k\cdot\varepsilon_{2})}{m^{2}(\varepsilon_{1}\!\cdot\varepsilon_{2})}\bigg]\,,

(4.49)

where
x_{\pm}=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{m}p\cdot\varepsilon^{\pm}
.
It is important to note that, when the polarization of the graviton is negative, we have the so called
x_{-}
, related to the
x
previously discussed and presented in Ref.
[
98
]
(labeled
x_{+}
) via the relation
x_{+}x_{-}=-1
(whenever omitted
x\equiv x_{+}
).

We now want to express this amplitude, obtained from Feynman rules, in spinor-helicity variables to possibly rewrite it in a more convenient form. Choosing the polarization of the graviton to be negative, we have

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\varepsilon_{1}^{a_{1}a_{2}}\cdot\varepsilon_{2}^{b_{1}b_{2}}=-\frac{1}{m^{2}}\langle\mathbf{1}^{(a_{1}}\mathbf{2}^{(b_{1}}\rangle\bigg[\langle\mathbf{1}^{a_{2})}\mathbf{2}^{b_{2})}\rangle-\frac{1}{mx_{-}}\langle\mathbf{1}^{a_{2})}k\rangle\langle k\mathbf{2}^{b_{2})}\rangle\bigg]\,,

(4.50a)

\displaystyle\hskip-25.6073pt\big[(\varepsilon_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{2})k_{\mu}\varepsilon_{\nu}^{-}S^{\mu\nu}\big]^{a_{1}a_{2}b_{1}b_{2}}=i\frac{\langle\mathbf{1}^{(a_{1}}k\rangle}{\sqrt{2}m^{2}}\bigg[\langle\mathbf{1}^{a_{2})}\mathbf{2}^{(b_{1}}\rangle-\frac{1}{2mx_{-}}\langle\mathbf{1}^{a_{2})}k\rangle\langle k\mathbf{2}^{(b_{1}}\rangle\bigg]\langle k\mathbf{2}^{b_{2})}\rangle,

(4.50b)

\displaystyle\hskip 48.36958pt(k\cdot\varepsilon_{1}^{a_{1}a_{2}})(k\cdot\varepsilon_{2}^{b_{1}b_{2}})=-\frac{1}{2m^{2}x_{-}^{2}}\langle\mathbf{1}^{(a_{1}}k\rangle\langle\mathbf{1}^{a_{2})}k\rangle\langle k\mathbf{2}^{(b_{1}}\rangle\langle k\mathbf{2}^{b_{2})}\rangle,

(4.50c)

where we have reduced all
[1^{a}|
and
|2^{b}]
to the chiral spinor basis of
\langle 1^{a}|
and
|2^{b}\rangle
using the identities:

\displaystyle[\mathbf{2}\mathbf{1}]=\langle\mathbf{2}\mathbf{1}\rangle-\frac{\langle\mathbf{2}3\rangle\langle 3\mathbf{1}\rangle}{mx_{-}}=\langle\mathbf{2}|\left({\hbox to0.0pt{1\hss}\hskip 1.6pt\hbox{1}}+\frac{\langle 3||3\rangle}{mx_{-}}\right)|\mathbf{1}\rangle

(4.51)

\displaystyle[\mathbf{2}3][3\mathbf{1}]=-\frac{\langle\mathbf{2}3\rangle\langle 3\mathbf{1}\rangle}{x_{-}^{2}}=\langle\mathbf{2}|\left(-\frac{|3\rangle\langle 3|}{x_{-}^{2}}\right)|\mathbf{1}\rangle\,.

(4.52)

Written in this form, we can easily extract the factors
\langle 1^{(a_{1}}|\otimes\langle 1^{a_{2})}|
and
|2^{(b_{1}}\rangle\otimes|2^{b_{2})}\rangle
, from each of these identities. Furthermore, we can interpret them as representations of the massive-particle states 1 and 2.
Introducing the symbol
\odot
for the symmetrized tensor product,
we can rewrite Eq. (
4.50a
) as:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{m^{2}}\langle\mathbf{1}|^{\odot 2}\bigg[{\hbox to0.0pt{1\hss}\hskip 1.6pt\hbox{1}}\odot{\hbox to0.0pt{1\hss}\hskip 1.6pt\hbox{1}}-\frac{1}{mx_{-}}{\hbox to0.0pt{1\hss}\hskip 1.6pt\hbox{1}}\odot\Big(|k\rangle\langle k|\Big)\bigg]|\mathbf{2}\rangle^{\odot 2}\,=

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{m^{2}}\bigg[\langle\mathbf{1}\mathbf{2}\rangle^{\odot 2}-\frac{1}{mx_{-}}\langle\mathbf{1}\mathbf{2}\rangle\odot\Big(\langle\mathbf{1}k\rangle\langle k\mathbf{2}\rangle\Big)\bigg]\,.

(4.53)

Combining all the terms in Eq. (
4.50
) into the amplitude, we obtain

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{3}(p_{1},p_{2},k^{-})

\displaystyle=x_{-}^{2}\bigg[\langle\mathbf{1}\mathbf{2}\rangle^{\odot 2}-\frac{2}{mx_{-}}\langle\mathbf{1}\mathbf{2}\rangle\odot\Big(\langle\mathbf{1}k\rangle\langle k\mathbf{2}\rangle\Big)+\frac{1}{m^{2}x_{-}^{2}}\langle\mathbf{1}k\rangle^{\odot 2}\langle k\mathbf{2}\rangle^{\odot 2}\bigg].

(4.54)

In App. (
C
) we construct the differential form of the angular-momentum operator in momentum space. We can now act with the operator
k_{\mu}\varepsilon_{\nu}J^{\mu\nu}
on the product state

|p^{a}\rangle^{\odot 2}=|p^{a_{1}}\rangle\otimes|p^{a_{2}}\rangle
.
For the negative helicity of the graviton, we have

k_{\mu}\varepsilon_{\nu}^{-}J^{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{4\sqrt{2}}\lambda^{\alpha}\lambda^{\beta}\epsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}J_{\alpha\dot{\alpha},\beta\dot{\beta}}=-\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}\langle kp^{a}\rangle\langle k\frac{\partial~}{\partial\lambda_{p}^{a}}\rangle,\qquad\langle k\frac{\partial~}{\partial\lambda_{p}^{b}}\rangle|p^{a}\rangle=|k\rangle\delta^{a}_{b}\,.

(4.55)

Thus,

\displaystyle i\sqrt{2}(k_{\mu}\varepsilon_{\nu}^{-}J^{\mu\nu})|p^{a}\rangle^{\odot 2}

\displaystyle=\langle kp^{b}\rangle\bigg\{\bigg[\langle k\frac{\partial~}{\partial\lambda_{p}^{b}}\rangle|p^{a_{1}}\rangle\bigg]\otimes|p^{a_{2}}\rangle+|p^{a_{1}}\rangle\otimes\bigg[\langle k\frac{\partial~}{\partial\lambda_{p}^{b}}\rangle|p^{a_{2}}\rangle\bigg]\bigg\}

\displaystyle=|k\rangle\langle kp^{a_{1}}\rangle\otimes|p^{a_{2}}\rangle+|p^{a_{1}}\rangle\otimes|k\rangle\langle kp^{a_{2}}\rangle=2|k\rangle\langle kp^{a}\rangle\odot|p^{a}\rangle\,.

(4.56)

Furthermore, the action of the operator
(k_{\mu}\varepsilon_{\nu}J^{\mu\nu})^{j}\,,\,\,j\text{in}\mathbb{N}
on the state
|p^{a}\rangle^{\odot 2}
is
4
4

4

To keep the notation simple we may omit the
SU(2)
indices of the massive little group and the symmetrization symbol for the external massive momenta, nevertheless in more rigorous formulation their presence is understood
:

\displaystyle\bigg(\frac{ik_{\mu}\varepsilon_{\nu}^{-}J^{\mu\nu}}{p\cdot\varepsilon^{-}}\bigg)|\mathbf{p}\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{mx_{-}}|k\rangle\langle k\mathbf{p}\rangle|\mathbf{p}\rangle,

(4.57a)

\displaystyle\bigg(\frac{ik_{\mu}\varepsilon_{\nu}^{-}J^{\mu\nu}}{p\cdot\varepsilon^{-}}\bigg)^{\!2}|\mathbf{p}\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{m^{2}x_{-}^{2}}|k\rangle^{2}\langle k\mathbf{p}\rangle^{2},

(4.57b)

\displaystyle\bigg(\frac{ik_{\mu}\varepsilon_{\nu}^{-}J^{\mu\nu}}{p\cdot\varepsilon^{-}}\bigg)^{\!j}|\mathbf{p}\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle=0,\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad j\geq 3.

(4.57c)

Notice that one of the possible algebraic realizations of
J^{\mu\nu}
is the tensor-product version,
-(\sigma^{\mu\nu}\otimes{\hbox to0.0pt{1\hss}\hskip 1.6pt\hbox{1}}+{\hbox to0.0pt{1\hss}\hskip 1.6pt\hbox{1}}\otimes\sigma^{\mu\nu})
, of the standard
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
chiral generator

\sigma^{\mu\nu}=\frac{i}{2}\sigma^{[\mu}\bar{\sigma}^{\nu]}
.
Therefore, it is direct to check that

\frac{ik_{\mu}\varepsilon_{\nu}^{-}J^{\mu\nu}}{p\cdot\varepsilon^{-}}=\frac{|k\rangle\langle k|}{mx_{-}}\otimes{\hbox to0.0pt{1\hss}\hskip 1.6pt\hbox{1}}+{\hbox to0.0pt{1\hss}\hskip 1.6pt\hbox{1}}\otimes\frac{|k\rangle\langle k|}{mx_{-}}\,.

(4.58)

In light of the previous observations, we may rewrite the last two terms in Eq. (
4.54
) as:

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{mx_{-}}\langle\mathbf{1}\mathbf{2}\rangle\langle\mathbf{1}k\rangle\langle k\mathbf{2}\rangle=\langle\mathbf{2}|^{2}\bigg(\frac{ik_{\mu}\varepsilon_{\nu}^{-}J_{1}^{\mu\nu}}{p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon^{-}}\bigg)|\mathbf{1}\rangle^{2}\,,

(4.59a)

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\frac{1}{m^{2}x_{-}^{2}}\langle\mathbf{1}k\rangle^{2}\langle k\mathbf{2}\rangle^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathbf{2}|^{2}\bigg(\frac{ik_{\mu}\varepsilon_{\nu}^{-}J_{1}^{\mu\nu}}{p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon^{-}}\bigg)^{\!2}|\mathbf{1}\rangle^{2}\,.

(4.59b)

Therefore

{\cal M}_{3}(p_{1},p_{2},k^{-})=x_{-}^{2}\langle\mathbf{2}|^{2}\bigg\{1+i\bigg(\frac{k_{\mu}\varepsilon_{\nu}^{-}J_{1}^{\mu\nu}}{p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon^{-}}\bigg)-\frac{1}{2}\bigg(\frac{k_{\mu}\varepsilon_{\nu}^{-}J_{1}^{\mu\nu}}{p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon^{-}}\bigg)^{\!2}\bigg\}|\mathbf{1}\rangle^{2}\,.

(4.60)

Written in this form, we can clearly see how to interpret the terms in the amplitude Eq. (
4.54
) as the multipole contributions with respect to the chiral spinor basis, despite the fact that they do not equal the multipoles in Eq. (
4.49
) individually.

As a final remark, note that since the operator
(k_{\mu}\varepsilon_{\nu}^{-}J^{\mu\nu})^{j}
annihilates the spin-1 state for
j\geq 3
, the preceding expression can be expressed compactly in an exponential form:

{\cal M}_{3}(p_{1},p_{2},k^{-})=x_{-}^{2}\langle\mathbf{2}|^{2}\exp\!{\bigg(i\frac{k_{\mu}\varepsilon^{-}_{\nu}J^{\mu\nu}}{p\cdot\varepsilon^{-}}}\bigg)|\mathbf{1}\rangle^{2}\,.

(4.61)

It can be checked explicitly that acting with the operator on the
state
\langle\mathbf{2}|^{2}
yields the same result.
On the other hand, choosing the other helicity of the graviton will yield the parity conjugated version of Eq. (
4.61
):

{\cal M}_{3}(p_{1},p_{2},k^{+})={x_{+}^{2}}[\mathbf{2}|^{2}\exp\!\bigg(i\frac{k_{\mu}\varepsilon^{+}_{\nu}J^{\mu\nu}}{p\cdot\varepsilon^{+}}\bigg)|\mathbf{1}]^{2}.

(4.62)

4.3.2
Exponential form of three-particle Amplitude

In this section I will generalize the previous discussion to arbitrary spin-
s
.
I will focus only on integer spin to ignore the factors
(-1)^{2s}
that appear when dealing with also half-integer spin.

We start by considering the three-point amplitudes for massive matter minimally coupled to gravity in Eq. (
4.31
):

{\cal M}_{3}^{(s)}(p_{1},p_{2},k^{+})=\frac{\kappa}{2}\frac{\langle\mathbf{1}\mathbf{2}\rangle^{2s}x^{2}}{m^{2s-2}},\qquad\quad{\cal M}_{3}^{(s)}(p_{1},p_{2},k^{-})=\frac{\kappa}{2}\frac{[\mathbf{1}\mathbf{2}]^{2s}x^{-2}}{m^{2s-2}}.

(4.63)

We focus once again on the minus-helicity amplitude.

In such a compact form all the dependence on the spin tensor is completely hidden.
In order to restore it, we need to rewrite the minus-helicity amplitude in the chiral basis

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{3}^{(s)}(p_{1},p_{2},k^{-})

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa}{2}\,\frac{x_{-}^{2}}{m^{2s-2}}\bigg(\langle\mathbf{2}\mathbf{1}\rangle+\frac{\langle\mathbf{2}k\rangle\langle k\mathbf{1}\rangle}{mx_{-}}\bigg)^{\!\odot 2s}\,=

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{\kappa}{2}\,\frac{x_{-}^{2}}{m^{2s-2}}\langle\mathbf{2}|^{2s}\Bigg[\sum_{j=0}^{2s}\binom{2s}{j}\bigg(\frac{|k\rangle\langle k|}{mx_{-}}\bigg)^{j}\Bigg]|\mathbf{1}\rangle^{2s}\,.

(4.64)

We now recast the operator in square brackets into exponential form using the differential angular momentum operator in Eq. (
4.55
).
Indeed, the formulae in Eq. (
4.57
) can be easily generalized
to the product states of spin-
s
, namely

\bigg(i\frac{k_{\mu}\varepsilon^{-}_{\nu}J^{\mu\nu}}{p\cdot\varepsilon^{-}}\bigg)^{\!j}|\mathbf{p}\rangle^{2s}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\dfrac{(2s)!}{(2s-j)!}|\mathbf{p}\rangle^{2s-j}\bigg(\dfrac{|k\rangle\langle k\mathbf{p}\rangle}{mx_{-}}\bigg)^{\!j}&,\quad j\leq 2s\,,\\
\hskip 56.9055pt0&,\quad j>2s\,.\end{array}\right.

(4.65)

From this we can derive the formal relations

\bigg(i\frac{k_{\mu}\varepsilon^{-}_{\nu}J^{\mu\nu}}{p\cdot\varepsilon^{-}}\bigg)^{\!\odot j}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\dfrac{(2s)!}{(2s-j)!}\bigg(\dfrac{|k\rangle\langle k|}{mx_{-}}\bigg)^{\!\otimes j}\!\odot{\hbox to0.0pt{1\hss}\hskip 1.6pt\hbox{1}}^{\otimes 2s-j}&,\quad j\leq 2s\,,\\
\hskip 56.9055pt0&,\quad j>2s\,.\end{array}\right.

(4.66)

Hence,

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{2}|^{2s}\Bigg[\sum_{j=0}^{2s}\binom{2s}{j}\bigg(\frac{|k\rangle\langle k|}{mx_{-}}\bigg)^{j}\Bigg]|\mathbf{1}\rangle^{2s}\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hskip-85.35826pt=\langle\mathbf{2}|^{2s}\!\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{j!}\bigg(i\frac{k_{\mu}\varepsilon^{-}_{\nu}J^{\mu\nu}}{p\cdot\varepsilon^{-}}\bigg)^{\!j}|\mathbf{1}\rangle^{2s}\!=\langle\mathbf{2}|^{2s}\!\exp\!\bigg(i\frac{k_{\mu}\varepsilon^{-}_{\nu}J^{\mu\nu}}{p\cdot\varepsilon^{-}}\bigg)|\mathbf{1}\rangle^{2s}\,.

(4.67)

Therefore, for finite (or infinite) spins we have:

\hat{{\cal M}}_{3}^{(s)}(p_{1},p_{2},k^{-})={\cal M}_{3}^{(0)}\exp\!\bigg(i\frac{k_{\mu}\varepsilon^{-}_{\nu}J^{\mu\nu}}{p\cdot\varepsilon^{-}}\bigg)\,,\qquad\quad{\cal M}_{3}^{(s)}=\frac{1}{m^{2s}}\langle\mathbf{2}|^{2s}\hat{{\cal M}}_{3}^{(s)}|\mathbf{1}\rangle^{2s}\,,

(4.68)

For the plus-helicity amplitude we can follow the same procedure, rewriting the amplitude in the anti-chiral basis, and obtain

\hat{{\cal M}}_{3}^{(s)}(p_{1},p_{2},k^{+})={\cal M}_{3}^{(0)}\exp\!\bigg(i\frac{k_{\mu}\varepsilon^{+}_{\nu}J^{\mu\nu}}{p\cdot\varepsilon^{+}}\bigg),\qquad\quad{\cal M}_{3}^{(s)}=\frac{1}{m^{2s}}[\mathbf{2}|^{2s}\hat{{\cal M}}_{3}^{(s)}|\mathbf{1}]^{2s}\,.

(4.69)

In the above formulae
\mathcal{M}^{(0)}_{3}(p_{1},p_{2},k^{\pm})\equiv\frac{\kappa}{2}(mx_{\pm})^{2}
corresponds to the scalar piece,
i.e.
the Schwarsczhild case.
These formulae match precisely the Kerr energy-momentum tensor, see Ref.
[
102
,
100
]
.

4.4
Classical infinite-spin limit

In App. (
B
) we motivated the definition of a generalized expectation value of an operator
\mathcal{O}
acting on two massive states,
represented by their polarization tensors,

\langle{{\cal O}}\rangle=\frac{\varepsilon_{2,\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{s}}{\cal O}^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{s},\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{s}}\varepsilon_{1,\nu_{1}\ldots\nu_{s}}}{\varepsilon_{2,\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{s}}\varepsilon_{1}^{\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{s}}}\,.

(4.70)

It is possible to obtain the correct result just using the above formula,
e.g.
see section 3 of Ref.
[
100
]
.
Nonetheless, for the rest of this paper I will follow the machinery presented in Ref.
[
103
]
, as it is completely equivalent but I find it more convenient.
In this approach, we recover the entire spin information from the amplitudes by combining the spinor-helicity formalism with the covariant approach to multipoles introduced in Ref.
[
81
]
, while the GEV will only serve to fix the normalization.

Figure 5
:
3-pt amplitude. Figure reproduced from Ref.
[
103
]

As we know from Sec.
4.3
, it is possible to recast the spin structure of the amplitudes in Eq. (
4.71
) in an exponential form:

\displaystyle\langle 2^{b}1^{a}\rangle^{\odot 2s}

\displaystyle=[2^{b}|^{\odot 2s}\!\exp\!\bigg(\!{-i}\frac{k_{\mu}\varepsilon_{\nu}^{+}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu\nu}}{p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon^{+}}\bigg)|1^{a}]^{\odot 2s}\,,

(4.72a)

\displaystyle[2^{b}1^{a}]^{\odot 2s}

\displaystyle=\langle 2^{b}|^{\odot 2s}\!\exp\!\bigg(\!{-i}\frac{k_{\mu}\varepsilon_{\nu}^{-}\sigma^{\mu\nu}}{p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon^{-}}\bigg)|1^{a}\rangle^{\odot 2s}\,,

(4.72b)

where we have used as the algebraic realizations of
J^{\mu\nu}
the tensor-product

-(\sigma^{\mu\nu}\otimes{\hbox to0.0pt{1\hss}\hskip 1.6pt\hbox{1}}+{\hbox to0.0pt{1\hss}\hskip 1.6pt\hbox{1}}\otimes\ \sigma^{\mu\nu})\equiv-\sigma^{\mu\nu}
for the chiral basis and
-(\bar{\sigma}^{\mu\nu}\otimes{\hbox to0.0pt{1\hss}\hskip 1.6pt\hbox{1}}+{\hbox to0.0pt{1\hss}\hskip 1.6pt\hbox{1}}\otimes\ \bar{\sigma}^{\mu\nu})\equiv-\bar{\sigma}^{\mu\nu}
for the anti-chiral basis.

Note that a consistent picture of spin-induced multipoles of a pointlike particle must be formulated in the particle’s rest frame.
However, the spin operator is defined in App. (
B
) for momentum
p_{\rm a}
, with
a=1,2
, but acts, in the rest frame, on the states with momenta
p_{\rm a}\pm k/2
.
The cure for that is to take into account an additional Lorentz boost,
needed to bridge the gap between two states with different momenta,
Ref.
[
81
]
.

To start, we note that any two four-vectors
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
of equal mass
m
may be related by the spinorial transformations:

|2^{b}\rangle=U_{12~a}^{~~b}\exp\!\bigg(\frac{i}{m^{2}}p_{1}^{\mu}k^{\nu}\sigma_{\mu\nu}\!\bigg)|1^{a}\rangle,\qquad|2^{b}]=U_{12~a}^{~~b}\exp\!\bigg(\frac{i}{m^{2}}p_{1}^{\mu}k^{\nu}\bar{\sigma}_{\mu\nu}\!\bigg)|1^{a}],

(4.73)

where
U_{12}\text{in}SU(2)
is a little-group transformation
that depends on the specifics of the massive-spinor realization.

The self-duality property of the spinorial generators,
\sigma^{\mu\nu}=\frac{i}{2}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\sigma_{\rho\sigma}
, allow us to easily rewrite the above exponents as

\frac{i}{m^{2}}\,p_{1}^{\mu}k^{\nu}\sigma_{\mu\nu,\alpha}^{~~~~\;\beta}=k\cdot a_{\alpha}^{~\beta},\qquad\frac{i}{m^{2}}\,p_{1}^{\mu}k^{\nu}\bar{\sigma}^{~~~\dot{\alpha}}_{\mu\nu,~\dot{\beta}}=-k\cdot a^{\dot{\alpha}}_{~\,\dot{\beta}},

(4.74)

where we have defined chiral representations for the Pauli-Lubanski operators, Ref.
[
103
]
,

a_{~~\alpha}^{\mu,~\,\beta}=\frac{1}{2m^{2}}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}p_{a\,\nu}\sigma_{\rho\sigma,\alpha}^{~~~~\;\beta}\,,\qquad a^{\mu,\dot{\alpha}}_{~~~\,\dot{\beta}}=\frac{1}{2m^{2}}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}p_{a\,\nu}\bar{\sigma}^{~~~\dot{\alpha}}_{\rho\sigma,~\dot{\beta}}.

(4.75)

Notice that, already at this level, we are free to pick
p_{\rm a}=p_{1}+k/2
instead of
p_{1}
, as the product
k\cdot a
remains the same.

The natural extension to higher-spin states of the Pauli-Lubanski operators in the chiral representations, is simply the analogous of Eq. (
C.5
):

(a^{\mu})_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{2s}}^{~~~~~~~~\beta_{1}\dots\beta_{2s}}=a_{~~\alpha_{1}}^{\mu,~~\beta_{1}}\delta_{\alpha_{2}}^{\beta_{2}}\!\dots\delta_{\alpha_{2s}}^{\beta_{2s}}+\ldots+\delta_{\alpha_{1}}^{\beta_{1}}\!\dots\delta_{\alpha_{2s-1}}^{\beta_{2s-1}}a_{~~\alpha_{2s}}^{\mu,~~~\beta_{2s}}\,.

(4.76)

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle|\mathbf{2}\rangle^{\odot 2s}

\displaystyle=e^{k\cdot a}\big\{U_{12}|\mathbf{1}\rangle\big\}^{\!\odot 2s}\,,\qquad|\mathbf{2}]^{\odot 2s}\!=e^{-k\cdot a}\big\{U_{12}|\mathbf{1}]\big\}^{\!\odot 2s}\,,

(4.77)

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{2}|^{\odot 2s}

\displaystyle=\big\{U_{12}\langle\mathbf{1}|\big\}^{\!\odot 2s}e^{-k\cdot a}\,,\>\,\quad[\mathbf{2}|^{\odot 2s}\!=\big\{U_{12}[\mathbf{1}|\big\}^{\!\odot 2s}e^{k\cdot a}\,,

where the second line follows from the antisymmetry of
\sigma^{\mu\nu}
and
\bar{\sigma}^{\mu\nu}
in the sense of
\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}\sigma_{~~~\beta}^{\mu\nu,~\gamma}\epsilon_{\gamma\delta}=-\sigma_{~~~\delta}^{\mu\nu,~\alpha}
.

We are finally ready to inspect the spin dependence of the three-point amplitudes. Using Eq. (
4.77
), we can rewrite Eq. (
4.71
) as

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{3}^{(s)}(k^{+})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\kappa}{2}\frac{x^{2}}{m^{2s-2}}[\mathbf{2}|^{\odot 2s}e^{-2k\cdot a}|\mathbf{1}]^{\odot 2s}\,,

(4.78a)

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{3}^{(s)}(k^{-})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\kappa}{2}\frac{x^{-2}}{m^{2s-2}}\langle\mathbf{2}|^{\odot 2s}e^{2k\cdot a}|\mathbf{1}\rangle^{\odot 2s}\,.

(4.78b)

The apparent spin dependence in the amplitude formulae above is of the form
e^{\mp 2k\cdot a}
, while no such dependence seems to exist in the original formulae Eq. (
4.71
).
Still, the true angular-momentum dependence inherent to the minimal-coupling amplitudes should not depend on the choice of the spinorial basis.
This apparent contradiction is resolved by taking into account the transformations Eq. (
4.73
).

To fully grasp this statement, it is helpful to examine the following example:
consider
\langle\mathbf{2}\mathbf{1}\rangle^{\odot 2s}
, in the chiral representation we have

\langle\mathbf{2}\mathbf{1}\rangle^{\odot 2s}=\big\{U_{12}\langle\mathbf{1}|\big\}^{\odot 2s}e^{-k\cdot a}|\mathbf{1}\rangle^{\odot 2s}\,,

(4.79)

whereas in the anti-chiral representation we have

\langle\mathbf{2}\mathbf{1}\rangle^{\odot 2s}=[\mathbf{2}|^{\odot 2s}e^{-2k\cdot a}|\mathbf{1}]^{\odot 2s}=\big\{U_{12}[\mathbf{1}|\big\}^{\odot 2s}e^{-k\cdot a}|\mathbf{1}]^{\odot 2s}\,.

(4.80)

We can now clearly see how in both cases we have the same spin dependence.

In light of these considerations, we strip the spin-states from the amplitudes in Eq. (
4.78
) to cleanly obtain the spin dependence (see App. (
D
)).
Furthermore, as the the total angular momentum of a Kerr Black Hole is large, it is a good approximation to take the infinite-spin limit.

For example, if we were to consider once again
\langle\mathbf{2}\mathbf{1}\rangle^{\odot 2s}
after this manipulation we would get:

\lim_{s\to\infty}m^{2s}(U_{12})^{\odot 2s}e^{-k\cdot a}.

(4.81)

Note that the factor of
m^{2s}
, that apparently diverges, cancels in the actual amplitudes. Thus

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{3}^{(\infty)}(k^{+})

\displaystyle\approx-\frac{\kappa}{2}m^{2}x^{2}e^{-k\cdot a}\!\lim_{s\to\infty}(U_{12})^{\odot 2s},

(4.82a)

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{3}^{(\infty)}(k^{-})

\displaystyle\approx-\frac{\kappa}{2}m^{2}x^{-2}e^{k\cdot a}\!\lim_{s\to\infty}(U_{12})^{\odot 2s}.

(4.82b)

The remaining unitary factor of
(U_{12})^{\odot 2s}
parametrizes an arbitrary little-group transformation that corresponds to the choice of the spin quantization axis (see Ref.
[
103
]
).
As such, it is inherently quantum-mechanical and therefore should be removed in the classical limit.
Indeed, it also appears in the simple product of polarization tensors

\displaystyle\lim_{s\to\infty}\varepsilon_{2}\cdot\varepsilon_{1}

\displaystyle=\lim_{s\to\infty}\frac{1}{m^{2s}}\langle\mathbf{2}\mathbf{1}\rangle^{\odot s}\odot[\mathbf{2}\mathbf{1}]^{\odot s}=

(4.83)

\displaystyle=\lim_{s\to\infty}\frac{1}{m^{2s}}\big\{U_{12}\langle\mathbf{1}|\big\}^{\!\odot s}e^{-k\cdot a}|\mathbf{1}\rangle^{\odot s}\big\{U_{12}[\mathbf{1}|\big\}^{\!\odot s}e^{k\cdot a}|\mathbf{1}]^{\odot s}=\lim_{s\to\infty}(U_{12})^{\odot 2s}\,.

So we can interpret the factor of
(U_{12})^{\odot 2s}
as the state normalization in accord with the notion of GEV, and as such it cancels out whenever we compute a physical quantity.

5
Computing higher-point Amplitudes from the Soft Theorems

In this section, I will discuss how to use the soft theorems, in the spinor-helicity formalism, to obtain amplitudes with an increasing number of gravitons.

Soft theorems describe general properties of scattering amplitudes in the infrared (IR) regime where the four-momentum of one massless particle approaches zero,
p^{\mu}\rightarrow\epsilon p^{\mu}
with
\epsilon\to 0
.
In this limit it is possible to write down a recursion relation connecting the amplitude to a lower-point one with the soft particle removed.
For soft gravitons with
h=\pm 2
the recursions take the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{\pm 2}=\left(\frac{S^{(0)}_{\pm 2}}{\epsilon^{3}}+\frac{S^{(1)}_{\pm 2}}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{S^{(2)}_{\pm 2}}{\epsilon}\right)\mathcal{M}_{n}\,,

(5.1)

where
S^{(i)}_{\pm h}
are called the soft factors.
The soft factors are
universal
, in the sense that they do not depend on what other “hard” particles are involved in the scattering.

The soft graviton theorem was first formulated by Steven Weinberg in 1965 Ref.
[
104
]
. However, after some initial progress Ref.
[
105
]
, Ref.
[
106
]
, this approach saw little use for nearly twenty years.

A new life into the study of soft theorems was brought by the emergence of on-shell methods for calculating scattering processes.
An important step in this development was made by Cachazo and Strominger Ref.
[
107
]
. Using the BCFW recursion relations Ref.
[
108
]
, they were able to extend the graviton soft theorems up to sub-subleading order at tree level.
Despite these rapid developments, all of the results derived with on-shell techniques were valid only for massless theories.

Recently, using the spinor-helicity formalism for
massive
particles introduced in Ref.
[
98
]
and discussed in Sec.
2
, Falkowski and Machado established soft recursion relations valid for general theories with massive particles of any spin.

5.1
BCFW Recursion Relations

In this section I will discuss the BCFW recursion relations, Ref.
[
108
]
. Using these relations we depart from the traditional QFT workflow: the on-shell recursion relations allow us to compute amplitudes without making any reference to a Lagrangian or to Feynman diagrams.

Note that the ‘original’ BCFW relations apply exclusively to amplitudes involving only massless particles. In this section, I will focus solely on the massless case, while the massive case will be addressed in subsequent sections.

The idea behind the Britto-Cachazo-Feng-Witten (BCFW) recursion relations is to consider the amplitude
\mathcal{M}_{n}(p_{1},\dots,p_{n})
as an analytic function of its complex momenta
p_{1},\dots,p_{n}
.
The momenta are complexified by introducing a shift, which preserves
on-shellness
and
momentum conservation
, and which is linear in a complex variable
z
.

Let us shift one of the momenta, say
p_{j}^{\mu}
with
j=1,\dots n
, by a vector
q^{\mu}
,
i.e.

\hat{p}_{j}(z)=p_{j}+zq
, with
z\text{in}\mathbb{C}
.
Furthermore, in order to preserve the total momentum conservation, let us shift another momentum
\hat{p}_{i}(z)=p_{i}-zq
.
On-shellness requires that
\hat{p}^{2}_{j}(z)=\hat{p}^{2}_{i}(z)=0
, which in turn implies the orthogonality conditions
q^{2}=p_{j}\cdot q=p_{i}\cdot q=0
.
The amplitude
\mathcal{M}_{n}(p_{1},\dots,p_{n})
then becomes an analytic function
\mathcal{M}(z)
of this shift parameter
z
.

It is a well known fact that unitarity, in the sense of the optical theorem, implies that the poles of an amplitude correspond to the exchange of on-shell intermediate states.
This means that the original amplitude factorizes on its poles into two smaller amplitudes connected by a vanishing propagator.
Diagrammatically,

where we have defined the momentum flowing through the propagator as

\hat{P}^{\mu}_{1,k}\equiv p_{1}^{\mu}+\dots+\hat{p}^{\mu}_{j}+\dots+p_{k}^{\mu}\,.

(5.2)

Notice that if
i
and
j
are on the same side of the diagram,
P_{1,k}^{\mu}
is independent of
z
and there is no pole.

Now,
\hat{P}_{1,k}^{2}=(P_{1,k}+zq)^{2}=P_{1,k}^{2}+2zP_{1,k}\cdot q
.
The factorization poles occur when
\hat{P}_{1,k}(z)
goes on-shell for some
z_{k}
,
i.e.
0=\hat{P}_{1,k}^{2}=(P_{1,k}+z_{k}q)^{2}=P_{1,k}^{2}+2z_{k}P_{1,k}\cdot q
,
therefore the poles are located at

z_{k}=-\frac{P^{2}_{1,k}}{2P_{1,k}\cdot q}\,,\qquad\mathrm{with}\quad z_{k}\neq 0\,.

(5.3)

Note that
\hat{P}^{2}_{1,k}(z)=2P_{1,k}\cdot q\,(z-z_{k})=-\dfrac{P^{2}_{1,k}}{z_{k}}(z-z_{k})
.

Now,
\mathcal{M}_{n}(z=0)
corresponds to the original amplitude, which is analytic around the origin, as the poles occur at
z_{k}\neq 0
.
Cauchy’s theorem states that on a contour
\mathcal{C}
very close to the origin

\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{\mathcal{C}}dz\frac{\mathcal{M}_{n}(z)}{z}=\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{z\rightarrow 0}\frac{\mathcal{M}_{n}(z)}{z}=\mathcal{M}_{n}(0)\,.

(5.4)

Furthermore, the global residue theorem states that over contour large enough to encompass all its poles,

\mathcal{M}_{n}(0)+\sum_{k}\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{z\rightarrow z_{k}}\frac{\mathcal{M}_{n}(z)}{z}=\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{z\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\mathcal{M}_{n}(z)}{z}\,,

(5.5)

where
z_{k}
are the locations of the factorization poles.

If
\mathcal{M}_{n}(z)\rightarrow 0
as
z\rightarrow\infty
, then the residue at infinity vanishes and we are left with

\mathcal{M}_{n}(0)+\sum_{k}\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{z\rightarrow z_{k}}\frac{\mathcal{M}_{n}(z)}{z}=0

(5.6)

Using then the factorization into two lower-point amplitudes connected by a vanishing propagator,

\displaystyle i\mathcal{M}_{n}(0)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{k}\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{z\rightarrow z_{k}}\frac{i\mathcal{M}_{n}(z)}{z}\,=

(5.7)

\displaystyle\hskip-25.6073pt=-\sum_{h=\pm}\sum_{k=2}^{n-2}\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{z\rightarrow z_{k}}\biggl[i\mathcal{M}_{k+1}(1,2,\dots,\hat{j},\dots,-\hat{P}^{-h}_{1,k})\frac{i}{z\hat{P}^{2}_{1,k}}\,i\mathcal{M}_{n-k+1}(\hat{P}_{1,k}^{h},k+1,\dots,\hat{i},\dots,n)\biggr]\,,

where
\mathcal{M}_{k+1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{n-k+1}
are the amplitudes on either side of the pole.

Recalling now that

\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{z\rightarrow z_{k}}\frac{\mathcal{M}_{n}(z)}{z}=\lim_{z\rightarrow z_{k}}(z-z_{k})\frac{\mathcal{M}_{n}(z)}{z}\,,\qquad\mathrm{and}\quad z-z_{k}=-\frac{z_{k}\hat{P}^{2}_{1,k}}{P^{2}_{1,k}}\,,

(5.8)

we finally obtain the BCFW recursion relations:

\displaystyle i\mathcal{M}(p_{1}\dots p_{n})

\displaystyle=

(5.9)

\displaystyle\hskip-22.76228pt=\sum_{h=\pm}\sum_{k=2}^{n-2}\,\,i\mathcal{M}_{k+1}(1,\dots,\hat{j},\dots,-\hat{P}^{-h}_{1,k})\frac{i}{P^{2}_{1,k}}\,i\mathcal{M}_{n-k+1}(\hat{P}_{1,k}^{h},\dots,\hat{i},\dots,n)\,.

Notice that the sum is over the
(n-3)
partitions of the
n
momenta into two sets, with at least three momenta in both sets (even when considering complex momenta, the smallest possible on-shell amplitudes are the three-point ones).

To complete the proof of the on-shell recursion relations, it is necessary to show that
\mathcal{M}_{n}(z)\rightarrow 0
as
z\rightarrow\infty
. This point is rather delicate, as for a generic amplitude, this behavior is not always assured; however, in the cases I will discuss, the residue at infinity will consistently be zero. A detailed discussion of all possible scenarios can be found in Ref.
[
109
]
.

5.2
Soft Recursions

In this section, I will derive a formula that connects an
n+1
-point amplitude with at least one massless particle to an
n
-point amplitude.
This formula captures the leading terms in the limit where the momentum of a massless particle is continuously taken to zero.
This massless particle is referred to as
soft
, and the formula is referred to as the
soft recursion
.
I will mainly follow Ref.
[
110
]
and Ref.
[
111
]
.

I will employ a similar BCFW approach, but with generalizations to include massive particles. Additionally, the soft limits will be taken in a manifestly on-shell manner using an extra complex deformation of the external massless momenta.

In addition to the new soft theorems, the resulting master formula yields consistency conditions such as the conservation of electric charge, the Einstein equivalence principle, supergravity Ward identities, and the Weinberg-Witten theorem.

5.2.1
Momentum shift

As seen in Sec.
5.1
, a crucial step for any amplitude to factorize is performing a shift of the complex momenta.
I will now examine how to implement such a shift in the massive case and, as an additional step, deform the external massless momenta.

Consider a set of
n+1
particles with momenta
p_{0}\dots p_{n}
satisfying
\sum_{i=0}^{n}p_{i}=0
and the on-shell conditions
p_{i}^{2}=m_{i}^{2}
.
The particle labeled as
0
is always massless,
p_{0}^{2}=0
, and it will be our soft particle.
We perform a two-parameter deformation of this kinematics.
One parameter
z
controls complex deformation of the momenta of the particle
0
and of two other particles, below labeled as
j
and
k
. The other parameter
\epsilon
controls the soft limit of the particle 0.

A convenient choice of the shifts is the following
5
5

5

One advantage of the two-parameter shift in Eq. (
5.10
) compared to simpler BCFW-like one-parameter shifts is that it will directly lead to soft expansion of recursion relations
in powers of
1/\epsilon
. As a bonus, it will allow for more control over the recursions’ boundary terms,
as the latter will start at higher orders in
1/\epsilon
.
:

\hat{p}_{0}=\epsilon p_{0}-zq_{0}\,,\quad\hat{p}_{j}=p_{j}-\frac{(\epsilon-1)(p_{0}p_{k})-z(q_{0}p_{k})}{q_{j}p_{k}}q_{j}\,,\quad\hat{p}_{k}=p_{k}-\frac{(\epsilon-1)(p_{0}p_{j})-z(q_{0}p_{j})}{q_{k}p_{j}}q_{k}\,,

(5.10)

and the remaining particles remain un-shifted.

We now must fix the four-vectors
q_{0}
,
q_{j}
,
q_{k}
in such a way that on-shellness and momentum conservation are preserved also on the shifted kinematics.

In order to preserve on-shellness, we impose

\begin{cases}\hat{p}_{0}^{2}=(\epsilon p_{0}-zq_{0})^{2}=0\\
\hat{p}_{j}=\biggl(p_{j}-\dfrac{(\epsilon-1)(p_{0}p_{k})-z(q_{0}p_{k})}{q_{j}p_{k}}q_{j}\biggr)^{2}=m_{j}^{2}\\
\hat{p}_{k}^{2}=p_{k}-\biggl(\dfrac{(\epsilon-1)(p_{0}p_{j})-z(q_{0}p_{j})}{q_{k}p_{j}}q_{k}\biggr)^{2}=m_{k}^{2}\end{cases}\rightarrow\begin{cases}p_{0}\cdot q_{0}=0\,,\,\,\,q_{0}^{2}=0\\
p_{j}\cdot q_{j}=0\,,\,\,\,q_{j}^{2}=0\\
p_{k}\cdot q_{k}=0\,,\,\,\,q_{k}^{2}=0\\
\end{cases}\,.

(5.11)

Furthermore, in order to preserve momentum conservation, we impose

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{n}\hat{p}_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{n}p_{i}+

(5.12)

\displaystyle\,+\biggl[(\epsilon-1)p_{0}-zq_{0}-\dfrac{(\epsilon-1)(p_{0}p_{k})-z(q_{0}p_{k})}{q_{j}p_{k}}q_{j}-\dfrac{(\epsilon-1)(p_{0}p_{j})-z(q_{0}p_{j})}{q_{k}p_{j}}q_{k}\biggr]=0\,,

thus the term in square bracket must vanish.
Notice that as
\epsilon
and
z
are completely unrelated, in order for the second line of Eq. (
5.12
) to be null, each contribution must individually vanish, so

\begin{cases}p_{0}-\dfrac{p_{0}p_{k}}{q_{j}p_{k}}q_{j}-\dfrac{p_{0}p_{j}}{q_{k}p_{j}}q_{k}=0\\
q_{0}+\dfrac{q_{0}p_{k}}{q_{j}p_{k}}q_{j}+\dfrac{q_{0}p_{j}}{q_{k}p_{j}}q_{k}=0\end{cases}\,.

(5.13)

A possible solution for this system is simply obtained by squaring both equations

\begin{cases}\biggl(p_{0}-\dfrac{p_{0}p_{k}}{q_{j}p_{k}}q_{j}-\dfrac{p_{0}p_{j}}{q_{k}p_{j}}q_{k}\biggr)^{2}=0\\
\biggl(q_{0}+\dfrac{q_{0}p_{k}}{q_{j}p_{k}}q_{j}+\dfrac{q_{0}p_{j}}{q_{k}p_{j}}q_{k}\biggr)^{2}=0\end{cases}\Rightarrow\begin{cases}p_{0}\cdot q_{j}=0\,,\,\,\,p_{0}\cdot q_{k}=0\,,\,\,\,q_{j}\cdot q_{k}=0\\
q_{0}\cdot q_{j}=0\,,\,\,\,q_{0}\cdot q_{k}=0\,,\,\,\,q_{j}\cdot q_{k}=0\\
\end{cases}\,.

(5.14)

Altogether, the four-vectors
q_{0},q_{j},q_{k}
satisfy

q_{0}^{2}=q_{j}^{2}=q_{k}^{2}=q_{0}p_{0}=q_{j}p_{j}=q_{k}p_{k}=0\,,\qquad q_{j}p_{0}=q_{k}p_{0}=q_{j}q_{0}=q_{k}q_{0}=q_{j}q_{k}=0\,.

(5.15)

5.2.2
Recursion

Consider an
n+1
point amplitude
\mathcal{M}_{n+1}\equiv\mathcal{M}(1\dots n0)
.
We denote
\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{z,\epsilon}_{n+1}
its shifted version, which is
\mathcal{M}_{n+1}
evaluated for the “hatted” kinematics in Eq. (
5.10
).
Similarly, we denote
\mathcal{M}_{n}\equiv\mathcal{M}(1\dots n)
, and its shifted version by
\hat{\mathcal{M}}_{n}^{z,\epsilon}
.

Note that the trivial shift is
\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{0,1}=\mathcal{M}
.

At tree level
\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{z,\epsilon}
is a meromorphic function of
z
,
i.e.
a function that is holomorphic on an open subset of the complex plane, except for a set of isolated points, which are poles of the function.
Thus, all the singularities of
\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{z,\epsilon}
are given by a finite set of simple poles at
z=z_{i}
.
As in Sec.
5.1
, we may now apply the Cauchy formula,

\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{0,\epsilon}_{n+1}=-\sum_{i}\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{z\to z_{i}}\dfrac{\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{z,\epsilon}_{n+1}}{z}+B_{\infty}\,,

(5.16)

where the boundary term
B_{\infty}
is the residue at infinity. Once again, if
\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{z,\epsilon}_{n+1}
goes to zero as
z\to\infty
then
B_{\infty}=0
.

Assuming the residue at infinity to be null or at least finite in the limit
\epsilon\to 0
, the only poles of the integrand are at the solutions of

(\hat{p}_{0}(z)+p_{a_{1}}+\dots+p_{a_{m}})^{2}=0\,,

(5.17)

for any non-empty subset
\{a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{m}\}
of
\{1,2,\dots,\dots,n\}
.

As seen in Sec.
5.1
, the residue at each of these poles is determined by unitarity to be the product of two lower
point amplitudes.
For a pole at say
z=z_{m}
, the vector
\hat{P}_{0,m}-m^{2}_{0,m}=\hat{p}_{0}+p_{a_{1}}+\dots+p_{a_{m}}
is null by construction, and the residue is given by

\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{0,\epsilon}_{n+1}=-\sum_{\mathcal{I}}\,\dfrac{\mathcal{M}_{m+1}(\hat{p}_{0},p_{a_{1}},\dots,p_{a_{m}},-\hat{P}_{0,m})\,\mathcal{M}_{n-m+1}(\hat{P}_{0,m},\overline{p_{a_{1}},\dots,p_{a_{m}}})}{P^{2}_{0,m}-m^{2}_{0,m}}\,,

(5.18)

where
\overline{a_{1},\dots,a_{m}}
is the complement of
a_{1},\dots,a_{m}
, and we sum over all possible non-empty subsets
\mathcal{I}=\{a_{1},\dots,a_{m}\}
of
\{1,\dots,n\}
.

Let us now separate the terms in the sum into two groups. The first group is those where the
set
\mathcal{I}
consists of a single element and the second group contains all other sets. The reason for this separation is that, as proven in Appendix A of Ref.
[
107
]
, elements of the former becomes singular when
\lambda_{0}\to 0
while elements of the latter remains finite.

In conclusion, we only focus on a subset of the poles related to the emission of the particle-0 from an external leg of the
n
-point amplitude
\mathcal{M}_{n}
, as
1/\epsilon^{m}
terms in
\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{z,\epsilon}_{n+1}
can only come from their residues.

There are
n
such poles
z_{l}
related to the solutions of the equations
\hat{P}_{0l}^{2}|_{z=z_{l}}=m_{l}^{2}
, where
\hat{P}_{0l}\equiv\hat{p}_{0}+\hat{p}_{l}
and it is understood that
\hat{p}_{l}=p_{l}
for
l\neq j,k
.
The poles are located at:

\displaystyle 0=\hat{P}_{0l}^{2}\big|_{z=z_{l}}-m_{l}^{2}

\displaystyle=(\hat{p}_{0}+\hat{p}_{l})^{2}|_{z=z_{l}}-m_{l}^{2}=(\epsilon p_{0}-z_{l}q_{0}+\hat{p}_{l})^{2}-m_{l}^{2}\,=

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{2}p_{0}^{2}+z_{l}^{2}q_{0}^{2}+\hat{p}_{l}^{2}+2\epsilon p_{0}\cdot\hat{p}_{l}-2\epsilon z_{l}p_{0}\cdot q_{0}-2z_{l}q_{0}\cdot\hat{p}_{l}-m_{l}^{2}\,

(5.19)

using now on-shellness and Eq. (
5.15
), we find

z_{l}=\epsilon\frac{p_{0}p_{l}}{q_{0}p_{l}},\qquad l=1\dots n\,,

(5.20)

notice that this result holds true also for the shifted particles.
Furthermore, we have
\hat{P}_{0l}^{2}-m_{l}^{2}=-2q_{0}p_{l}(z-z_{l})
,
i.e.
poles at
\hat{P}_{0l}^{2}\to m_{l}^{2}
are in one-to-one correspondence with the poles at
z\to z_{l}
.

To sum up, we have:

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{z,\epsilon}(1\dots n0)|_{\hat{P}_{0l}^{2}\to m_{l}^{2}}=-\frac{\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{z_{l},\epsilon}(1\dots P_{0l}\dots n)\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{z_{l},\epsilon}((-P_{0l})l0)}{\hat{P}_{0l}^{2}-m_{l}^{2}}\,=

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt=\frac{\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{z_{l},\epsilon}(1\dots P_{0l}\dots n)\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{z_{l},\epsilon}((-P_{0l})l0)}{2q_{0}p_{l}(z-z_{l})}\,,

(5.21a)

\displaystyle\dfrac{\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{z\to z_{l}}\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{z,\epsilon}(0\dots n)}{z_{l}}=\frac{1}{\epsilon}\frac{\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{z_{l},\epsilon}(1\dots P_{0l}\dots n)\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{z_{l},\epsilon}((-P_{0l})l0)}{2p_{0}p_{l}}\,.

(5.21b)

Plugging this back into the Cauchy formula in Eq. (
5.16
) one obtains the soft recursion:

\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{0,\epsilon}(1\dots n0)=-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\frac{\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{z_{l},\epsilon}(1\dots P_{0l}\dots n)\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{z_{l},\epsilon}((-P_{0l})l0)}{2p_{0}p_{l}}+\ \mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{0}).

(5.22)

As a final remark, notice that the boundary term in Eq. (
5.16
) is absorbed in
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{0})
, as it cannot produce singular terms in the
\epsilon\to 0
limit.

5.2.3
Spinor shift

To compute the desired amplitudes, we find it convenient to use the spinor-helicity formalism. Accordingly, we will now focus on implementing the framework discussed in the previous sections at the spinorial level.

We begin by discussing spinor shifts that realize the momentum shift in Eq. (
5.10
).
The discussion varies depending on whether the shifted particles
j
and
k
are massive or massless.
In our case
m_{j}>0
and
m_{k}>0
; the other possible cases are discusssed in Ref.
[
110
]
.

One possible choice of the shift vectors
q
in Eq. (
5.10
) is

6
6

6

In the following, the spinor Lorentz indices will be often left implicit to keep the notation concise.
:

q_{0}\sigma=y\tilde{\lambda}_{0}\,,\qquad q_{j}\sigma=p_{j}\sigma\tilde{\lambda}_{0}\tilde{\lambda}_{0}\,,\qquad q_{k}\sigma=p_{k}\sigma\tilde{\lambda}_{0}\tilde{\lambda}_{0}\,,

(5.23)

where
y
is an arbitrary spinor satisfying
\langle y0\rangle\neq 0
.
It is easy to check that the constraints in Eq. (
5.15
) are satisfied by this choice.

We may now decompose also the shifted momenta into spinors:

\hat{p}_{0}\sigma=\lambda_{0}^{z}\tilde{\lambda}_{0}\,,\qquad\hat{p}_{j}\sigma=\chi_{j}^{J}\tilde{\chi}_{j\,J}^{z}\,,\qquad\hat{p}_{k}\sigma=\chi_{k}^{J}\tilde{\chi}_{k\,J}^{z}\,.

(5.24)

At the spinor level, a momentum shift can be achieved by shifting only the holomorphic (or anti-holomorphic) spinor while keeping the other spinor fixed.

In particular I will use the
\{0\bar{m}\bar{m}\}
shift, defined as:

\displaystyle{\rm\mathbf{\{}0\bar{m}\bar{m}\}}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\qquad\lambda_{0}^{z}

\displaystyle=\epsilon\lambda_{0}-zy\,,

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}_{j}^{z}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\chi}_{j}+\frac{(\epsilon-1)\langle 0p_{k}0]-z\langle yp_{k}0]}{[0p_{j}p_{k}0]}[\mathbf{j}0]\tilde{\lambda}_{0}\,,

(5.25)

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}_{k}^{z}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\chi}_{k}-\frac{(\epsilon-1)\langle 0p_{j}0]-z\langle yp_{j}0]}{[0p_{j}p_{k}0]}[\mathbf{k}0]\tilde{\lambda}_{0}\,,

with all other spinors kept unshifted.

A crucial point that will become more relevant in the following sections, is that this shift is non-trivial for
z=\epsilon=0
:
in this limit the spinors
\tilde{\chi}_{j/k}
must shift to absorb the original momentum of the particle
0
.
On the other hand, the shift is trivial for
z=0
and
\epsilon=1
, but this is not the soft limit
\epsilon\to 0
.

For the remainder of this section, I will derive some identities that will be useful in later computations.

For the
\{0\bar{m}\bar{m}\}
shift, the location of the poles in Eq. (
5.20
) can be recast as:

z_{l}=\epsilon\frac{\langle 0p_{l}0]}{\langle yp_{l}0]}\,;\qquad\text{which, for $m_{l}=0$, reduces to:}\quad z_{l}=\epsilon\frac{\langle 0l\rangle}{\langle yl\rangle}\,.

(5.26)

At the poles, the shifted spinors can be expressed as

\displaystyle\lambda_{0}^{z_{l}}

\displaystyle=\epsilon\frac{\langle y0\rangle}{\langle yp_{l}0]}p_{l}\sigma\tilde{\lambda}_{0}\,,

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}_{j}^{z_{l}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\chi}_{j}-\frac{2p_{0}p_{k}}{[0p_{j}p_{k}0]}[\mathbf{j}0]\tilde{\lambda}_{0}+\epsilon\frac{\langle y0\rangle[0p_{k}p_{l}0]}{\langle yp_{l}0][0p_{j}p_{k}0]}[\mathbf{j}0]\tilde{\lambda}_{0}\,

(5.27)

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}_{k}^{z_{l}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\chi}_{k}+\frac{2p_{0}p_{j}}{[0p_{j}p_{k}0]}[\mathbf{k}0]\tilde{\lambda}_{0}-\epsilon\frac{\langle y0\rangle[0p_{j}p_{l}0]}{\langle yp_{l}0][0p_{j}p_{k}0]}[\mathbf{k}0]\tilde{\lambda}_{0}\,.

These identities allow us to rewrite
\hat{P}_{0l}
at the poles as:

\text{for $l\neq j,k$}\quad\rightarrow\quad\hat{P}_{0l}\sigma|_{z_{l}}=\hat{p}_{0}\sigma|_{z_{l}}+p_{l}\sigma|_{z_{l}}=\lambda_{0}^{z_{l}}\tilde{\lambda}_{0}+p_{l}\sigma=\epsilon\frac{\langle y0\rangle}{\langle yp_{l}0]}p_{l}\sigma\tilde{\lambda}_{0}\tilde{\lambda}_{0}+p_{l}\sigma\,.

(5.28)

Furthermore, since
\hat{P}_{0l}|_{z=z_{l}}
is on shell by definition, we may also factorize it into spinors:

l\neq j,k\,\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\begin{cases}\hat{P}_{0l}\sigma|_{z_{l}}=\chi_{0l}^{L}\tilde{\chi}_{0l\,L},\quad\chi_{0l}=\chi_{l}\,,\quad\tilde{\chi}_{0l}=\tilde{\chi}_{l}+\epsilon\dfrac{\langle y0\rangle}{\langle yp_{l}0]}[\mathbf{l}0]\tilde{\lambda}_{0}\,,\quad\text{if}\,\,m_{l}>0\\
\hat{P}_{0l}\sigma|_{z_{l}}=\lambda_{0l}\tilde{\lambda}_{0l}\,,\quad\,\,\,\lambda_{0l}=\lambda_{l}\,,\quad\tilde{\lambda}_{0l}=\tilde{\lambda}_{l}+\epsilon\dfrac{\langle y0\rangle}{\langle yl\rangle}\tilde{\lambda}_{0}\,,\qquad\quad\,\text{if}\,\,m_{l}=0\end{cases}

(5.29)

Similarly at
z_{j}
and
z_{k}
we have the decomposition

\displaystyle\hat{P}_{0j}\sigma|_{z_{j}}

\displaystyle=p_{j}\sigma-\frac{2p_{0}p_{k}}{[0p_{j}p_{k}0]}p_{j}\sigma\tilde{\lambda}_{0}\tilde{\lambda}_{0}\,\Rightarrow\,\chi_{0j}=\chi_{j}\,,\quad\tilde{\chi}_{0j}=\tilde{\chi}_{j}-\frac{2p_{0}p_{k}}{[0p_{j}p_{k}0]}[\mathbf{j}0]\tilde{\lambda}_{0}\,,

(5.30)

\displaystyle\hat{P}_{0k}\sigma|_{z_{k}}

\displaystyle=p_{k}\sigma+\frac{2p_{0}p_{j}}{[0p_{j}p_{k}0]}p_{k}\sigma\tilde{\lambda}_{0}\tilde{\lambda}_{0}\,\Rightarrow\,\chi_{0k}=\chi_{k}\,,\quad\tilde{\chi}_{0k}=\tilde{\chi}_{k}+\frac{2p_{0}p_{j}}{[0p_{j}p_{k}0]}[\mathbf{k}0]\tilde{\lambda}_{0}\,.

Note that
\hat{P}_{0j}\sigma|_{z_{j}}
and
\hat{P}_{0k}\sigma|_{z_{k}}
, and similarly the shifts
\tilde{\chi}_{j}^{z_{k}}
and
\tilde{\chi}_{k}^{z_{j}}
, are independent of
\epsilon
.

5.3
Soft Theorems

We are now ready to apply the recursion formula in Eq. (
5.22
) to derive the soft factors for amplitudes involving particles of any mass and spin, with the graviton as the soft particle.
I will restrict the analysis to the case of single soft particle emission.

Consider a gravity theory where the massless spin-2 graviton is minimally coupled to matter and to itself.

In the on-shell formalism, the minimal coupling of the graviton to two matter particle of same mass
m>0
and spin
s
corresponds to the on-shell 3-point amplitudes in Eq. (
4.31
):

\mathcal{M}(p_{1},-p_{2},k_{3}^{-})=-\frac{\kappa}{2}\frac{\langle 3p_{1}\zeta]^{2}}{[3\zeta]^{2}}\frac{[\mathbf{2}\mathbf{1}]^{2s}}{m^{2s}}\,,\qquad\mathcal{M}(p_{1},-p_{2},k_{3}^{+})=-\frac{\kappa}{2}\frac{\langle\zeta p_{1}3]^{2}}{[3\zeta]^{2}}\frac{\langle\mathbf{2}\mathbf{1}\rangle^{2s}}{m^{2s}}\,,

(5.31)

whereas the pure gravity on-shell 3-point amplitudes correspond to Eq. (
4.18
):

\mathcal{M}(k_{1}^{-},-k_{2}^{-},k_{3}^{+})=-\frac{\kappa}{2}\frac{\langle 12\rangle^{6}}{\langle 13\rangle^{2}\langle 23\rangle^{2}}\,,\qquad\mathcal{M}(k_{1}^{+},-k_{2}^{+},k_{3}^{-})=-\frac{\kappa}{2}\frac{[12]^{6}}{[13]^{2}[23]^{2}}\,.

(5.32)

Without loss of generality, we may focus on the emission of a soft graviton with plus-helicity

7
7

7

The minus-helicity and plus-helicity cases do not interfere with each other. Moreover, the minus-helicity case can be easily obtained from the plus-helicity one by applying a parity transformation to the amplitude
.
Thus, the shifted three-point amplitude computed at the
z_{l}
-poles is:

\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{z_{l},\epsilon}((-\hat{P}_{0l})l0^{+}_{h})=-\frac{\kappa}{2}\,\frac{\langle yp_{l}0]^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}\langle 0y\rangle^{2}}\,.

(5.33)

Plugging this back into Eq. (
5.22
) we obtain:

\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{0,\epsilon}(1\dots n0^{+}_{h})=\frac{\kappa}{2}\,\frac{1}{\epsilon^{3}}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\frac{\langle yp_{l}0]^{2}}{(2p_{0}p_{l})\langle 0y\rangle^{2}}\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{z_{l},\epsilon}(1\dots\hat{P}_{0l}\dots n)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{0})\,.

(5.34)

To proceed we need to expand the
n
-point amplitude above in powers of
\epsilon
.

As shown in App. (
E
), we have

\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{z_{l},\epsilon}(1\dots\hat{P}_{0l}\dots n)=\left\{1+\epsilon\dfrac{\langle 0y\rangle}{\langle yp_{l}0]}\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}+\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2}\dfrac{\langle 0y\rangle^{2}}{\langle yp_{l}0]^{2}}\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}^{2}\right\}\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{0,0}(1\dots n)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{3})\,,

(5.35)

where the differential operator
\tilde{\cal D}_{l}
is

\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}=[0\mathbf{l}][0\partial_{l}]+\dfrac{[0p_{k}p_{l}0]}{[0p_{j}p_{k}0]}[0\mathbf{j}][0\partial_{j}]-\dfrac{[0p_{j}p_{l}0]}{[0p_{j}p_{k}0]}[0\mathbf{k}][0\partial_{k}]\,,\qquad{\rm for}\,\,l\neq j,k,

(5.36)

and
\tilde{\cal D}_{l}=0
for
l=j,k

8
8

8

The operator
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}
can be related to the angular momentum operator
J^{\mu\nu}_{l}
.

Plugging the expanded
\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{z_{l},\epsilon}
back into Eq. (
5.34
) we obtain the soft theorem in the standard form:

\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{0,\epsilon}(1\dots n0_{h}^{+})=\bigg\{\frac{1}{\epsilon^{3}}S_{+2}^{(0)}+\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}S_{+2}^{(1)}+\frac{1}{\epsilon}S_{+2}^{(0)}\bigg\}\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{0,0}(1\dots n)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{0})\,,

(5.37)

where we have defined

S^{(0)}_{+2}=\frac{\kappa}{2}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\dfrac{\langle yp_{l}0]^{2}}{(2p_{0}\cdot p_{l})\langle 0y\rangle^{2}},\quad S^{(1)}_{+2}=\frac{\kappa}{2}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\dfrac{\langle yp_{l}0]}{(2p_{0}\cdot p_{l})\langle 0y\rangle}\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l},\quad S^{(2)}_{+2}=\frac{\kappa}{4}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\frac{1}{4p_{0}\cdot p_{l}}\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}\,.

(5.38)

It is important to remark that the soft factors do not depend on
y
(either as a consequence of momentum conservation or of angular momentum conservation), see Ref.
[
110
]
.

The soft factors above are the natural extension to the massive case of the ones presented in Ref.
[
107
]
and Ref.
[
111
]
, moreover
S^{(0)}_{+2}
is nothing but the Weinberg soft factor.

Finally, notice that for the emission of a minus-helicity graviton the computation is the same, and the final result is related to the former by a parity transformation, see Ref.
[
110
]
.

5.3.1
Soft exponentiation

I now discuss the exponential representation of the soft theorems.

Ref.
[
112
]
showed that the soft theorem for gravitons and gluons could be written in terms of an exponential operator acting on lower point amplitudes.
Furthermore, in Ref.
[
110
]
, it was shown that the exponential operator can be extended for amplitudes with
massive particles of any spin and with a soft particle of any integer helicity
|h|\leq 2
.

The Taylor expansion in Eq. (
5.35
) has been trucated at second order, nonetheless we may consider the complete series.
It is straightforward to check that it can be recast in the exponential form:

\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{z_{l},\epsilon}(1\dots\hat{P}_{0l}\dots n)=\exp\left(\epsilon\frac{\langle 0y\rangle}{\langle yp_{l}0]}\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}\right)\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{0,0}(1\dots n)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{3}).

(5.39)

Substituting now in Eq. (
5.37
), we obtain:

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{0,\epsilon}(1\dots n0^{+}_{h})

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa}{2}\,\frac{1}{\epsilon^{3}}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\frac{\langle yp_{l}0]^{2}}{(2p_{0}p_{l})\langle 0y\rangle^{2}}\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{z_{l},\epsilon}(1\dots\hat{P}_{0l}\dots n)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{0})=

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa}{2}\,\frac{1}{\epsilon^{3}}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\frac{\langle yp_{l}0]^{2}}{(2p_{0}p_{l})\langle 0y\rangle^{2}}\exp\left(\epsilon\frac{\langle 0y\rangle}{\langle yp_{l}0]}\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}\right)\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{0,0}(1\dots n)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{0})\,,

(5.40)

where the differential operator
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}
is given in Eq. (
5.36
).

5.3.2
Consistency checks

I conclude this discussion on the soft theorems by verifying that the formulae derived in the previous sections using the spinor-helicity formalism are consistent with those found in the literature, which were obtained through different approaches.

As seen in Ref.
[
110
]
, the Taylor expansion around
\epsilon\to 0
of the minimally coupled gravity amplitude in the soft limit can be recast in an exponential form Eq. (
5.40
):

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{0,\epsilon}(1\dots n0^{+}_{h})

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa}{2}\,\frac{1}{\epsilon^{3}}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\frac{\langle yp_{l}0]^{2}}{(2p_{0}p_{l})\langle 0y\rangle^{2}}\exp\left(\epsilon\frac{\langle 0y\rangle}{\langle yp_{l}0]}\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}\right)\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{0,0}(1\dots n)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{0})=

\displaystyle\hskip-42.67912pt=\frac{\kappa}{2}\,\frac{1}{\epsilon^{3}}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\frac{\langle yp_{l}0]^{2}}{(2p_{0}p_{l})\langle 0y\rangle^{2}}\exp\Biggl\{\epsilon\frac{\langle 0y\rangle}{\langle yp_{l}0]}\,\times

(5.41)

\displaystyle\times\left([0\mathbf{l}][0\partial_{l}]+\dfrac{[0p_{k}p_{l}0]}{[0p_{j}p_{k}0]}[0\mathbf{j}][0\partial_{j}]-\dfrac{[0p_{j}p_{l}0]}{[0p_{j}p_{k}0]}[0\mathbf{k}][0\partial_{k}]\right)\Biggr\}\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{0,0}(1\dots n)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{0})\,.

We are now interested in comparing this result with the one originally obtained by Cachazo and Strominger in Ref.
[
107
]
and analyzed comprehensively by Guevara et all in Ref.
[
100
]
:

{\cal M}_{n+1}=\frac{\kappa}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\frac{(p_{i}\cdot\varepsilon)^{2}}{p_{i}\cdot k}+i\frac{(p_{i}\cdot\varepsilon)(k_{\mu}\varepsilon_{\nu}J_{i}^{\mu\nu})}{p_{i}\cdot k}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{(k_{\mu}\varepsilon_{\nu}J_{i}^{\mu\nu})^{2}}{p_{i}\cdot k}\right]{\cal M}_{n}+{\cal O}(k^{2})\,.

(5.42)

Here the soft momentum
k
corresponds to the external soft
graviton, and we have constructed its polarization tensor as

\varepsilon_{\mu\nu}=\varepsilon_{\mu}\varepsilon_{\nu}
.

In Ref.
[
100
]
it was shown that for the three- and four- point amplitudes we are able to recast the term in square bracket in an exponential form. Following this intuition, we may write

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{n+1}(1\dots n,0^{+})=\frac{\kappa}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\left(p_{i}\cdot\varepsilon\right)^{2}}{p_{i}\cdot k}\exp\left(i\frac{k_{\mu}\varepsilon_{\nu}J^{\mu\nu}_{i}}{p_{i}\cdot\varepsilon}\right){\cal M}_{n}(1\dots n)\,.

(5.43)

We now recall that
\hat{\cal M}=\hat{\cal M}^{(0,1)}
.
Thus, to make contact with Eq. (
5.41
) we generalize to the case of arbitrary
\epsilon
shift.
To this end, we perform the
\{0,\bar{m},\bar{m}\}
shift in Eq. (
5.25
), where we choose once again the shifted spinors to be the one associated with the soft graviton “0”, and the massive spinors
j
,
k
,
and expand around
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(0,\epsilon)}_{n}(1\dots n)=\mathcal{M}^{(0,\epsilon)}_{n}(1\dots\hat{\mathbf{j}}\dots\hat{\mathbf{k}}\dots n)\,,\qquad\mathcal{M}^{(0,\epsilon)}_{n}=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\dfrac{\epsilon^{m}}{m!}\bigg(\frac{\partial}{\partial\epsilon}\bigg)^{\!\!m}\mathcal{M}^{(0,\epsilon)}_{n}\Big|_{\epsilon=0}\,,

(5.44)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\epsilon}\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(0,\epsilon)}=\left(\frac{\partial\tilde{\chi}_{j}}{\partial\epsilon}\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\chi}_{j}}+\frac{\partial\tilde{\chi}_{k}}{\partial\epsilon}\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\chi}_{k}}\right)\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(0,\epsilon)}=

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\left(\frac{\langle 0y\rangle[0p_{k}p_{l}0]}{\langle yp_{l}0][0p_{j}p_{k}0]}[0\mathbf{j}][0\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\chi}_{j}}]-\frac{\langle 0y\rangle[0p_{j}p_{l}0]}{\langle yp_{l}0][0p_{j}p_{k}0]}[0\mathbf{k}][0\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\chi}_{k}}]\right)\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(0,\epsilon)}\,.

(5.45)

Altogether

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(0,\epsilon)}(1\dots n,0^{+})=\frac{\kappa}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\epsilon^{3}}\frac{\left(p_{i}\cdot\varepsilon\right)^{2}}{p_{i}\cdot k}\exp\left(i\frac{k_{\mu}\varepsilon_{\nu}J^{\mu\nu}_{i}}{p_{i}\cdot\varepsilon}\right)\left(\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\dfrac{\epsilon^{m}}{m!}\bigg(\frac{\partial}{\partial\epsilon}\bigg)^{\!\!m}\mathcal{M}^{(0,\epsilon)}_{n}\Big|_{\epsilon=0}\right)=

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{\kappa}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\epsilon^{3}}\frac{\left(p_{i}\cdot\varepsilon\right)^{2}}{p_{i}\cdot k}\exp\left(i\frac{k_{\mu}\varepsilon_{\nu}J^{\mu\nu}_{i}}{p_{i}\cdot\varepsilon}\right)\,\times

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\times\left(\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\dfrac{\epsilon^{m}}{m!}\left(\frac{\langle 0y\rangle[0p_{k}p_{l}0]}{\langle yp_{l}0][0p_{j}p_{k}0]}[0\mathbf{j}][0\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\chi}_{j}}]-\frac{\langle 0y\rangle[0p_{j}p_{l}0]}{\langle yp_{l}0][0p_{j}p_{k}0]}[0\mathbf{k}][0\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\chi}_{k}}]\right)^{\!\!m}\mathcal{M}^{(0,\epsilon)}_{n}\Big|_{\epsilon=0}\right)=

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{\kappa}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\epsilon^{3}}\frac{\left(p_{i}\cdot\varepsilon\right)^{2}}{p_{i}\cdot k}\exp\left(\epsilon\frac{\langle 0y\rangle}{\langle yp_{l}0]}[0\mathbf{l}][0\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\chi}_{l}}]\right)\,\times

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\times\exp\left(\frac{\langle 0y\rangle[0p_{k}p_{l}0]}{\langle yp_{l}0][0p_{j}p_{k}0]}[0\mathbf{j}][0\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\chi}_{j}}]-\frac{\langle 0y\rangle[0p_{j}p_{l}0]}{\langle yp_{l}0][0p_{j}p_{k}0]}[0\mathbf{k}][0\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\chi}_{k}}]\right)\mathcal{M}^{(0,\epsilon)}_{n}\Big|_{\epsilon=0}=
