\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{\kappa}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\epsilon^{3}}\frac{\left(p_{i}\cdot\varepsilon\right)^{2}}{p_{i}\cdot k}\exp\left(\epsilon\frac{\langle 0y\rangle}{\langle yp_{l}0]}\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}\right)\mathcal{M}^{(0,0)}_{n}\,,

(5.46)

where we have used the parity reversed form of Eq. (
4.55
) to go from the second to the third line.

From Eq. (
5.46
) it is clear that the two formulas match perfectly, confirming the consistency of our procedure. Moreover, by recalling that the soft expansion was performed by defining the parameter
\epsilon
via the rescaling
p^{\mu}=\epsilon p^{\mu}
, or in spinor variables
\lambda_{0}=\epsilon\lambda_{0}
, we can fully absorb the
\epsilon
in this formula. As a result, the two outcomes are exactly the same, further validating our consistency check.

6
Gravitational Compton Amplitude from the Soft Theorems

In this section I will apply the soft theorems discussed in Sec.
5
to the three-point amplitudes obtained in Sec.
4
to obtain the four-point Compton gravitational amplitude.

As seen in Sec.
5.3.2
, our soft theorems can be formulated using Eq. (
5.41
) or Eq. (
5.43
) equivalently.
In this section, I will use the latter formulation as most of the results found in literature are expressed in this way.
Furthermore as I’m not interested in this section to explore the soft limit of the four-point amplitude, but rather to use the soft theorems to obtain this higher-point amplitude, I find it more convenient to use directly Eq. (
5.43
), rather than computing Eq. (
5.41
) and taking the
\epsilon\to 1
limit.

I will start by discussing the spinless Schwarzschild case and later generalize also to the spinning Kerr case.

6.1
Compton Amplitude for Schwarzschild Black Holes

Our starting point are the three-point amplitudes Eq. (
4.69
), that I rewrite below for convenience,

{\cal M}_{3}^{(s)}(p_{1},p_{2},k^{+}_{3})=\frac{\kappa}{2}\frac{\langle\mathbf{1}\mathbf{2}\rangle^{2s}x_{3}^{2}}{m^{2s-2}},\qquad\quad{\cal M}_{3}^{(s)}(p_{1},p_{2},k^{-}_{3})=\frac{\kappa}{2}\frac{[\mathbf{1}\mathbf{2}]^{2s}x_{3}^{-2}}{m^{2s-2}}\,.

(6.1)

As I’m now interested the Schwarzscild case,
s=0
; hence, after expliciting the
x
-factors, these amplitudes reduce to:

{\cal M}_{3}^{(0)}(p_{1},p_{2},k^{+}_{3})=\kappa(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3}^{+})^{2}\,,\qquad\quad{\cal M}_{3}^{(0)}(p_{1},p_{2},k^{-}_{3})=\kappa(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3}^{-})^{2}\,.

(6.2)

Let us start by considering the plus-helicity case.

To get the corresponding four-point amplitude we may now use Eq. (
5.43
):

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{4}^{(0)}(p_{1},p_{2},k_{3}^{+},k_{4}^{+})=\frac{\kappa}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{\left(p_{i}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}\right)^{2}}{p_{i}\cdot k_{4}}\exp\left(i\frac{k_{4,\mu}\varepsilon_{4,\nu}^{+}J^{\mu\nu}_{i}}{p_{i}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}}\right){\cal M}_{3}^{(0)}(p_{1},p_{2},k_{3}^{+})\,.

(6.3)

This result is gauge-independent, therefore we can make a convenient gauge choice to simplify the computations. Nonetheless, to make the final result general, we must express it too in a gauge-independent way.

A convenient gauge choice is the one in which we fix the reference vectors for the massless particles labeled
3
and
4
to be
\varepsilon_{3}=\varepsilon(3,4)
,
\varepsilon_{4}=\varepsilon(4,3)
, this way all products
p_{i}\cdot\varepsilon_{j}=0
, for
i,j=3,4
,
and the sum in Eq. (
6.3
) reduces to:

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{4}^{(0)}(p_{1},p_{2},k_{3}^{+},k_{4}^{+})=

(6.4)

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{\kappa}{2}\left[\frac{\left(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}\right)^{2}}{p_{1}\cdot k_{4}}\exp\left(i\frac{k_{4,\mu}\varepsilon_{4,\nu}^{+}J^{\mu\nu}_{1}}{p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}}\right)+\frac{\left(p_{2}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}\right)^{2}}{p_{2}\cdot k_{4}}\exp\left(i\frac{k_{4,\mu}\varepsilon_{4,\nu}^{+}J^{\mu\nu}_{2}}{p_{2}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}}\right)\right]{\cal M}_{3}^{(0)}(p_{1},p_{2},k_{3}^{+})\,.

Notice now that, as we are studying the scalar case, whenever the arguments of the exponential operators in Eq. (
6.4
) act on the three-point scalar amplitudes
\mathcal{M}_{3}^{(0)}
they vanish. Thus:

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{4}^{(0)}(p_{1},p_{2},k_{3}^{+},k_{4}^{+})

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa}{2}\left[\frac{\left(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}\right)^{2}}{p_{1}\cdot k_{4}}+\frac{\left(p_{2}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}\right)^{2}}{p_{2}\cdot k_{4}}\right]{\cal M}_{3}^{(0)}(p_{1},p_{2},k_{3}^{+})=

\displaystyle=\left(\dfrac{\kappa}{2}\right)^{\!2}\left[\frac{\left(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}\right)^{2}}{p_{1}\cdot k_{4}}+\frac{\left(p_{2}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}\right)^{2}}{p_{2}\cdot k_{4}}\right]2(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3}^{+})^{2}\,.

(6.5)

Furthermore, from the three-point amplitude, we inherit the relation
p_{1}\cdot k_{3}=0

9
9

9

Note that this relation, in the context of BCFW, naturally arises when considering the
t
-channel residue within the on-shell formalism. Furthermore, it is important to emphasize that a consistent description of spin-induced multipoles for a pointlike particle must be formulated in the particle’s rest frame. This relation aligns perfectly with that requirement.
.
Using total momentum conservation, and the relations previously discussed we are able to rewrite Eq. (
6.5
) as:

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{4}^{(0)}(p_{1},p_{2},k_{3}^{+},k_{4}^{+})

\displaystyle=\left(\dfrac{\kappa}{2}\right)^{\!2}\left[\frac{\left(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}\right)^{2}}{p_{1}\cdot k_{4}}+\frac{\left((p_{1}+k_{3}+k_{4})\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}\right)^{2}}{p_{2}\cdot k_{4}}\right]2(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3}^{+})^{2}=

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\kappa^{2}}{2}\left(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3}^{+}\right)^{2}\left(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}\right)^{2}\left[\dfrac{1}{p_{1}\cdot k_{4}}+\dfrac{1}{p_{2}\cdot k_{4}}\right]=

(6.6)

\displaystyle=-\dfrac{\kappa^{2}}{2}\dfrac{\left(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3}^{+}\right)^{2}\left(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}\right)^{2}(k_{3}\cdot k_{4})^{2}}{(p_{1}\cdot k_{4})(p_{2}\cdot k_{4})(k_{3}\cdot k_{4})}\,.

We remark that this expression is still gauge-invariant, and it is possible to rewrite it in a manifestly gauge invariant way if we notice that

10
10

10

This relation is easily proven using the fact that in our specific gauge choice,
p_{1}\cdot k_{3}=0
and
p_{i}\cdot\varepsilon_{j}=0
, for
i,j=3,4
.
:

p_{1}\cdot F_{3}\cdot F_{4}\cdot p_{1}=-\left(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3}^{+}\right)\left(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}\right)(k_{3}\cdot k_{4})\,,

(6.7)

where
F_{\mu\nu}
is the field strength
F_{\mu\nu}=k_{\mu}\varepsilon_{\nu}-k_{\nu}\varepsilon_{\mu}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{4}^{(0)}(p_{1},p_{2},k_{3}^{+},k_{4}^{+})=\dfrac{-\kappa^{2}\omega_{(0)}^{2}}{(2p_{1}\cdot k_{4})(2p_{2}\cdot k_{4})(2k_{3}\cdot k_{4})}\,,

(6.8)

where we have defined the quantity
\omega_{(0)}\equiv-2\,p_{1}\cdot F_{3}\cdot F_{4}\cdot p_{1}
.

The computation for the minus-helicity case is exactly the same, the only difference being the helicity of graviton-
3
in
\omega_{(0)}
.

The remaining helicity configurations,
i.e.
when the graviton-
4
has negative helicity, are easily obtained from Eq. (
6.8
) using parity and charge conjugation transformations.

This result is in perfect agreement with the ones found in the literature,

e.g.
see Ref.
[
81
]
, Ref.
[
113
]
.

6.2
Compton Amplitude for Kerr Black Holes

In this section I generalize the discussion in Sec.
6.1
to the case of spinning black holes.

Once again our starting point are the amplitudes

{\cal M}_{3}^{(s)}(p_{1},p_{2},k^{+}_{3})=\frac{\kappa}{2}\frac{\langle\mathbf{1}\mathbf{2}\rangle^{2s}x_{3}^{2}}{m^{2s-2}},\qquad\quad{\cal M}_{3}^{(s)}(p_{1},p_{2},k^{-}_{3})=\frac{\kappa}{2}\frac{[\mathbf{1}\mathbf{2}]^{2s}x_{3}^{-2}}{m^{2s-2}}\,.

(6.9)

In this form the angular momentum dependence of the amplitude is hidden inside the spinors, however it is convenient to make it manifest:

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{3}^{(s)}(p_{1},p_{2},k^{+}_{3})

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\kappa}{m^{2s}}(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3}^{+})^{2}[\mathbf{1}|^{2s}\exp\left(i\dfrac{k_{3,\mu}\varepsilon_{3,\nu}^{+}J_{1}^{\mu\nu}}{p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3}^{+}}\right)|\mathbf{2}]^{2s}\,,

(6.10a)

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{3}^{(s)}(p_{1},p_{2},k^{-}_{3})

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\kappa}{m^{2s}}(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3}^{-})^{2}\langle\mathbf{1}|^{2s}\exp\left(i\dfrac{k_{3,\mu}\varepsilon_{3,\nu}^{-}J_{1}^{\mu\nu}}{p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3}^{-}}\right)|\mathbf{2}\rangle^{2s}\,,

(6.10b)

where I have used the fact that the action of the operator:
\exp\left(i\frac{k_{3,\mu}\varepsilon_{3,\nu}^{+}J^{\mu\nu}}{p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3}^{+}}\right)
, on either the spinors
\langle\mathbf{1}|^{2s}
(
[\mathbf{1}|^{2s}
)
or
|\mathbf{2}\rangle^{2s}
(
|\mathbf{2}]^{2s}
) gives the same result, to pick without loss of generality
J\to J_{1}

11
11

11

For a rigorous proof of the above statement see section 2.3 of Ref.
[
100
]
.

Let us start by considering the plus-helicity case.

To get the corresponding four-point amplitude we may use once again Eq. (
5.43
):

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{4}^{(s)}(p_{1},p_{2},k_{3}^{+},k_{4}^{+})=\frac{\kappa}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{\left(p_{i}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}\right)^{2}}{p_{i}\cdot k_{4}}\exp\left(i\frac{k_{4,\mu}\varepsilon_{4,\nu}^{+}J^{\mu\nu}_{i}}{p_{i}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}}\right){\cal M}_{3}^{(s)}(p_{1},p_{2},k_{3}^{+})\,.

(6.11)

Also this time, it is convenient to pick
\varepsilon_{3}=\varepsilon(3,4)
and
\varepsilon_{4}=\varepsilon(4,3)
, this way all products
p_{i}\cdot\varepsilon_{j}=0
, for
i,j=3,4
and the last term of the sum drops out. Hence,

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{4}^{(s)}(p_{1},p_{2},k_{3}^{+},k_{4}^{+})=

(6.12)

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{\kappa}{2}\left[\frac{\left(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}\right)^{2}}{p_{1}\cdot k_{4}}\exp\left(i\frac{k_{4,\mu}\varepsilon_{4,\nu}^{+}J^{\mu\nu}_{1}}{p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}}\right)+\frac{\left(p_{2}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}\right)^{2}}{p_{2}\cdot k_{4}}\exp\left(i\frac{k_{4,\mu}\varepsilon_{4,\nu}^{+}J^{\mu\nu}_{2}}{p_{2}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}}\right)\right]{\cal M}_{3}^{(s)}(p_{1},p_{2},k_{3}^{+})\,.

Recalling now that the action of the operator
\exp\left(i\frac{k_{4,\mu}\varepsilon_{4,\nu}^{+}J^{\mu\nu}}{p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}}\right)
gives the same result whether it acts on the spinors-
1
or -
2
, the two exponential operators in Eq. (
6.12
) are completely equivalent and we can write:

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{4}^{(s)}(p_{1},p_{2},k_{3}^{+},k_{4}^{+})

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa}{2}\left[\frac{\left(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}\right)^{2}}{p_{1}\cdot k_{4}}+\frac{\left(p_{2}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}\right)^{2}}{p_{2}\cdot k_{4}}\right]\exp\left(i\frac{k_{4,\mu}\varepsilon_{4,\nu}^{+}J_{1}^{\mu\nu}}{p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}}\right){\cal M}_{3}^{(s)}(p_{1},p_{2},k_{3}^{+})=

\displaystyle\hskip-42.67912pt=\left(\dfrac{\kappa}{2}\right)^{\!2}\left[\frac{\left(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}\right)^{2}}{p_{1}\cdot k_{4}}+\frac{\left(p_{2}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}\right)^{2}}{p_{2}\cdot k_{4}}\right]2(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3}^{+})^{2}\,\times

(6.13)

\displaystyle\times\dfrac{1}{m^{2s}}[\mathbf{1}|^{2s}\exp\left(i\frac{k_{4,\mu}\varepsilon_{4,\nu}^{+}J_{1}^{\mu\nu}}{p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}}\right)\exp\left(i\dfrac{k_{3,\mu}\varepsilon_{3,\nu}^{+}J_{1}^{\mu\nu}}{p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3}^{+}}\right)|\mathbf{2}]^{2s}\,.

Notice that the first line of Eq. (
6.13
) is simply Eq. (
6.5
). This is to be expected, as the scalar contribution relative to the scattering of Kerr Black Holes is simply the Schwarzschild Compton scattering.

Focus now on the the second line. I define the operators:

J_{3}\equiv i\dfrac{k_{3,\mu}\varepsilon_{3,\nu}^{+}J_{1}^{\mu\nu}}{p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3}^{+}}\,,\qquad\qquad J_{4}\equiv i\dfrac{k_{4,\mu}\varepsilon_{4,\nu}^{+}J_{1}^{\mu\nu}}{p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}}\,.

(6.14)

In general these operators do not commute, therefore the product of the two exponentials is not just the exponential of the sum. Nonetheless using the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorf formula:

e^{X}e^{Y}=e^{Z}\,,\quad Z=X+Y+\dfrac{1}{2}[X,Y]+\dfrac{1}{12}[X,[X,Y]]+\dfrac{1}{12}[[X,Y],Y]+\dots

(6.15)

we can write

[\mathbf{1}|^{2s}e^{J_{4}}e^{J_{3}}|\mathbf{2}]^{2s}=[\mathbf{1}|^{2s}\exp\left\{J_{3}+J_{4}-\dfrac{1}{2}[J_{3},J_{4}]+\dots\right\}|\mathbf{2}]^{2s}\,.

(6.16)

The sum is then guaranteed to truncate due to the Cayley-Hamilton theorem.
From the Lorentz algebra,
[J,J]\sim J
, thus even higher commutators
can be put in terms of lower multipoles. It is important however to notice that starting from the third commutator we begin developing unphysical poles in the argument of the exponential, which make us question the validity of this approximation.

The amplitude before the tensorization only has the singlet, the dipole and the quadrupole contribution, that we grouped in an exponential form, furthermore also the soft theorems have been proven to be universal (in the sense that they are related to a physical symmetry) only up to second order in the series expansion.
Thus when considering the multipole expansion order by order for miniamal gravity, the only universal terms are the ones up to the first commutator and all higher contributions are negligible.
Indeed up to order
J^{3}
, we have:

e^{J_{4}}e^{J_{3}}=e^{J_{3}+J_{4}-\frac{1}{2}[J_{3},J_{4}]}\,.

(6.17)

Let us then study in detail the argument of the exponential in Eq. (
6.17
):

\displaystyle J_{3}+J_{4}-\frac{1}{2}[J_{3},J_{4}]=i\dfrac{k_{3,\mu}\varepsilon_{3,\nu}^{+}J_{1}^{\mu\nu}}{p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3}^{+}}+i\dfrac{k_{4,\mu}\varepsilon_{4,\nu}^{+}J_{1}^{\mu\nu}}{p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}}-\dfrac{1}{2}\left[i\dfrac{k_{3,\mu}\varepsilon_{3,\nu}^{+}J_{1}^{\mu\nu}}{p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3}^{+}},i\dfrac{k_{4,\rho}\varepsilon_{4,\sigma}^{+}J_{1}^{\rho\sigma}}{p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}}\right]\,.

(6.18)

Using the antisymmetry of
J^{\mu\nu}
, and recalling that the field strength
F_{\mu\nu}
is defined as
F_{\mu\nu}=2\,k_{[\mu}\varepsilon_{\nu]}
, we can rewrite Eq. (
6.18
) as

i\dfrac{F_{3,\mu\nu}J_{1}^{\mu\nu}}{2\,p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3}^{+}}+i\dfrac{F_{4,\mu\nu}J_{1}^{\mu\nu}}{2\,p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}}+\dfrac{1}{2}\left[\dfrac{F_{3,\mu\nu}J_{1}^{\mu\nu}}{2\,p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3}^{+}},\dfrac{F_{4,\rho\sigma}J_{1}^{\rho\sigma}}{2\,p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}}\right]\,.

(6.19)

Let us now focus on this commutator:

\dfrac{1}{2}\left[\dfrac{F_{3,\mu\nu}J_{1}^{\mu\nu}}{2\,p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3}^{+}},\dfrac{F_{4,\rho\sigma}J_{1}^{\rho\sigma}}{2\,p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}}\right]=\dfrac{F_{3,\mu\nu}J_{1}^{\mu\nu}F_{4,\rho\sigma}J_{1}^{\rho\sigma}-F_{4,\rho\sigma}J_{1}^{\rho\sigma}F_{3,\mu\nu}J_{1}^{\mu\nu}}{8\,(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3}^{+})(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+})}\,,

(6.20)

notice that the numerator in Eq. (
6.20
) can be rewritten as

F_{3,\mu\nu}F_{4,\rho\sigma}J_{1}^{\mu\nu}J_{1}^{\rho\sigma}-F_{3,\mu\nu}F_{4,\rho\sigma}J_{1}^{\rho\sigma}J_{1}^{\mu\nu}=F_{3,\mu\nu}F_{4,\rho\sigma}\left[J_{1}^{\mu\nu},J_{1}^{\rho\sigma}\right]\,,

(6.21)

and the remaining commutator is fixed by the Lorentz algebra,

\left[J^{\mu\nu},J^{\rho\sigma}\right]=-i\left(\eta^{\mu\rho}J^{\nu\sigma}+\eta^{\nu\sigma}J^{\mu\rho}-\eta^{\mu\sigma}J^{\nu\rho}-\eta^{\nu\rho}J^{\mu\sigma}\right)\,.

(6.22)

Thus

F_{3,\mu\nu}F_{4,\rho\sigma}\left[J_{1}^{\mu\nu},J_{1}^{\rho\sigma}\right]=2i\left[F_{3},F_{4}\right]_{\mu\nu}J_{1}^{\mu\nu}\,,

(6.23)

and substituting in Eq. (
6.20
), we find

\dfrac{1}{2}\left[\dfrac{F_{3,\mu\nu}J_{1}^{\mu\nu}}{2\,p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3}^{+}},\dfrac{F_{4,\rho\sigma}J_{1}^{\rho\sigma}}{2\,p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}}\right]=i\dfrac{\left[F_{3},F_{4}\right]_{\mu\nu}J_{1}^{\mu\nu}}{4\,(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3}^{+})(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+})}\,.

(6.24)

Let us now return to Eq. (
6.18
), which we can now rewrite as

J_{3}+J_{4}-\frac{1}{2}[J_{3},J_{4}]=i\dfrac{F_{3,\mu\nu}J_{1}^{\mu\nu}}{2\,p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3}^{+}}+i\dfrac{F_{4,\mu\nu}J_{1}^{\mu\nu}}{2\,p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}}-i\dfrac{\left[F_{3},F_{4}\right]_{\mu\nu}J_{1}^{\mu\nu}}{4\,(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3}^{+})(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+})}\,.

(6.25)

Once again, I stress the fact that this expression is gauge-invariant, and it is possible to perform the following manipulation to obtain a manifestly gauge invariant result:

\displaystyle i\dfrac{F_{3,\mu\nu}J_{1}^{\mu\nu}}{2\,p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3}^{+}}+i\dfrac{F_{4,\mu\nu}J_{1}^{\mu\nu}}{2\,p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}}-i\dfrac{\left[F_{3},F_{4}\right]_{\mu\nu}J_{1}^{\mu\nu}}{4\,(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3}^{+})(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+})}=

(6.26)

\displaystyle\qquad=i\dfrac{F_{3,\mu\nu}J_{1}^{\mu\nu}(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+})(k_{4}\cdot k_{3})+F_{4,\mu\nu}J_{1}^{\mu\nu}(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3}^{+})(k_{3}\cdot k_{4})-\left[F_{3},F_{4}\right]_{\mu\nu}J_{1}^{\mu\nu}(k_{3}\cdot k_{4})/2}{2\,(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3}^{+})(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+})(k_{4}\cdot k_{3})}\,.

We now recall that

\omega_{(0)}\equiv 2\left(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3}^{+}\right)\left(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+}\right)(k_{3}\cdot k_{4})=-2p_{1}\cdot F_{3}\cdot F_{4}\cdot p_{1}\,,

(6.27)

furthermore, using
p_{i}\cdot\varepsilon_{j}=0
, for
i,j=3,4
:

k_{3}\cdot F_{4}\cdot p_{1}=(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}^{+})(k_{4}\cdot k_{3})\,,\qquad k_{4}\cdot F_{3}\cdot p_{1}=(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3}^{+})(k_{3}\cdot k_{4})\,,

(6.28)

and thanks to the conservation of the total momentum and the on-shellness of the amplitude, we have

\displaystyle p_{1}+p_{2}+k_{3}+k_{4}=0\,\,\Rightarrow\,\,\left(p_{1}+(k_{3}+k_{4})\right)^{2}=p_{2}^{2}\,\,\Rightarrow

(6.29)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\,\,2p_{1}\cdot(k_{3}+k_{4})=-(k_{3}+k_{4})^{2}=-2(k_{3}\cdot k_{4})\,\,\Rightarrow\,\,-(k_{3}\cdot k_{4})=-p_{1}\cdot(k_{3}+k_{4})

Altogether, we have

\displaystyle J_{3}+J_{4}-\frac{1}{2}[J_{3},J_{4}]=\dfrac{\omega_{(1)}}{\omega_{(0)}}=

(6.30)

\displaystyle\qquad=-i\dfrac{F_{3,\mu\nu}(p_{1}\cdot F_{4}\cdot k_{3})+F_{4,\mu\nu}(p_{1}\cdot F_{3}\cdot k_{4})+\left[F_{3},F_{4}\right]_{\mu\nu}p_{1}\cdot(k_{3}+k_{4})/2}{\omega_{(0)}}J_{1}^{\mu\nu}\,,

where I have defined the quantity:

i\omega_{(1)}\equiv F_{3,\mu\nu}J^{\mu\nu}_{1}(p_{1}\cdot F_{4}\cdot k_{3})+F_{4,\mu\nu}J^{\mu\nu}_{1}(p_{1}\cdot F_{3}\cdot k_{4})+\left[F_{3},F_{4}\right]_{\mu\nu}J^{\mu\nu}_{1}p_{1}\cdot\frac{(k_{3}+k_{4})}{2}

(6.31)

Therefore,

{\cal M}_{4}^{(s)}(p_{1},p_{2},k_{3}^{+},k_{4}^{+})=\dfrac{-\kappa^{2}\omega_{(0)}^{2}}{(2p_{1}\cdot k_{4})(2p_{2}\cdot k_{4})(2k_{3}\cdot k_{4})}\dfrac{1}{m^{2s}}[\mathbf{1}|^{2s}\exp\left(\frac{\omega_{(1)}}{\omega_{(0)}}\right)|\mathbf{2}]^{2s}\,.

(6.32)

It is often convenient to consider the kinematic configuration in which the particles
1
and
3
are incoming and particle
2
and
4
are outgoing, in this case Eq. (
6.32
) becomes:

{\cal M}_{4}^{(s)}(p_{1},-p_{2},k_{3}^{+},-k_{4}^{+})=\dfrac{\kappa^{2}\omega_{(0)}^{2}}{(2p_{1}\cdot k_{4})(2p_{2}\cdot k_{4})(2k_{3}\cdot k_{4})}\dfrac{1}{m^{2s}}[\mathbf{1}|^{2s}\exp\left(\frac{\tilde{\omega}_{(1)}}{\tilde{\omega}_{(0)}}\right)|\mathbf{2}]^{2s}\,,

(6.33)

where

\begin{gathered}\tilde{\omega}_{(0)}=\omega_{(0)}\,,\\
i\tilde{\omega}_{(1)}\equiv F_{3,\mu\nu}J^{\mu\nu}_{1}(p_{1}\cdot F_{4}\cdot k_{3})+F_{4,\mu\nu}J^{\mu\nu}_{1}(p_{1}\cdot F_{3}\cdot k_{4})-\left[F_{3},F_{4}\right]_{\mu\nu}J^{\mu\nu}_{1}p_{1}\cdot\frac{(k_{3}-k_{4})}{2}\end{gathered}

(6.34)

The computation for the minus-helicity case is exactly the same, the only difference being the helicity of graviton-3 in the
\omega
-s.

We can now compute the classical infinite-spin limit. This is achieved in a straightforward manner by replacing all instances of
J_{\mu\nu}
with
-S_{\mu\nu}
, as the entire contribution from the orbital angular momentum is utilized to boost the spinors-2 to match spinors-1. Moreover, the contraction of these spinors cancels out the
1/m^{2s}
term.
As a final remark, the boost needed to map
1
to
2
is the same one that aligns
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
.
Thus

{\cal M}_{4}^{(\infty)}(p_{1},-p_{2},k_{3},-k_{4})=\dfrac{\kappa^{2}\omega_{(0)}^{2}}{(2p_{1}\cdot k_{4})^{2}(2k_{3}\cdot k_{4})}\exp\left(\frac{\Omega_{(1)}}{\Omega_{(0)}}\right)\,,

(6.35)

where

\begin{gathered}\Omega_{(0)}=\omega_{(0)}\,,\\
-i\Omega_{(1)}\equiv F_{3,\mu\nu}S^{\mu\nu}_{1}(p_{1}\cdot F_{4}\cdot k_{3})+F_{4,\mu\nu}S^{\mu\nu}_{1}(p_{1}\cdot F_{3}\cdot k_{4})-\left[F_{3},F_{4}\right]_{\mu\nu}S^{\mu\nu}_{1}p_{1}\cdot\frac{(k_{3}-k_{4})}{2}\end{gathered}

(6.36)

This result is in perfect agreement with the one presented in Ref.
[
114
]
.

6.3
Classical formulation of the Soft Theorems

In the previous sections, we obtained the classical three-point amplitude and subsequently the classical four-point amplitude.
To obtain the classical four-point amplitude we started from the quantum version of the three-point amplitude, used the soft theorems discussed in Sec.
5
to obtain the quantum version of the four-point amplitude and only then computed the classical limit.

A natural question arises: is it possible to obtain directly the classical four point amplitude starting from the classical three-point amplitude in a completely classical formulation?

In this section I will discuss this procedure.

Let us begin by considering the classical three- and four- point amplitudes

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{3}^{(\infty)}(p_{1},-p_{2},k)=-\kappa(p_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3})^{2}\exp\left(i\dfrac{\varepsilon\cdot S\cdot k}{\varepsilon\cdot p}\right)\,,

(6.37a)

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{4}^{(\infty)}(p_{1},-p_{2},k_{3},-k_{4})=\dfrac{\kappa^{2}\omega_{(0)}^{2}}{(2p_{1}\cdot k_{4})^{2}(2k_{3}\cdot k_{4})}\exp\left(\frac{\Omega_{(1)}}{\Omega_{(0)}}\right)\,,

(6.37b)

As we are considering a classical amplitude, it is useful to express this amplitude using the rules presented in Sec.
3
, that can be summarized as follows: we restore all the
\hbar
-s, replace all massless momenta with their corresponding wavenumber, and associate all massive momenta with their classical four-velocity,
i.e.

\kappa\to\dfrac{\kappa}{\sqrt{\hbar}}\,,\qquad J\to\dfrac{J}{\hbar}\,,\qquad F^{\mu\nu}\to\hbar\bar{F}^{\mu\nu}\,,\qquad k\to\hbar\bar{k}\,,\qquad p\to mv\,.

(6.38)

In light of such substitutions, it is convenient to define the mass-normalized spin tensor
\tilde{S}^{\mu\nu}
:

S^{\mu\nu}=\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}p_{\rho}a_{\sigma}\,\,\Rightarrow\,\,\tilde{S}^{\mu\nu}=\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}v_{\rho}a_{\sigma}

(6.39)

Using these definitions we rewrite Eq. (
6.37
) as

12
12

12

In the following equations the subscript-0 is not related to the spin of the massive particles involved in the scattering, but rather it indicates that the amplitude considered is the tree-level contribution

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(0)}_{3,\rm cl}(v,k)=-\dfrac{\kappa}{\sqrt{\hbar}}\,m^{2}\,(\varepsilon\cdot v)^{2}\,\exp\left(i\frac{\,\varepsilon\cdot\tilde{S}\cdot\bar{k}}{\varepsilon\cdot v}\right)\,,

(6.40a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(0)}_{4,\rm cl}(v,\bar{k}_{3},\bar{k}_{4})=\frac{\kappa^{2}m^{2}\bar{\omega}_{(0)}^{2}}{8\hbar(\bar{k}_{3}\cdot\bar{k}_{4})(\bar{k}_{4}\cdot v)^{2}}\exp\left(\frac{\bar{\omega}_{(1)}}{\bar{\omega}_{(0)}}\right)\,,

(6.40b)

where we have defined

\begin{gathered}\bar{\omega}_{(0)}=-2\,v\cdot\bar{F}_{3}\cdot\bar{F}_{4}\cdot v\,,\\
-i\bar{\omega}_{(1)}\equiv\bar{F}_{3,\mu\nu}\tilde{S}^{\mu\nu}_{1}(v\cdot\bar{F}_{4}\cdot\bar{k}_{3})+\bar{F}_{4,\mu\nu}\tilde{S}^{\mu\nu}_{1}(v\cdot\bar{F}_{3}\cdot\bar{k}_{4})-\left[\bar{F}_{3},\bar{F}_{4}\right]_{\mu\nu}\tilde{S}^{\mu\nu}_{1}v\cdot\frac{(\bar{k}_{3}-\bar{k}_{4})}{2}\end{gathered}

(6.41)

Notice that in Eq. (
6.40a
) the argument of the exponential scale as
\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{0})
, therefore the result is completely classical at all orders in the multipole expansion.
Meanwhile in Eq. (
6.40b
), even if the argument of the exponential apparently scales as
\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{0})
, this result is valid only up to cubic order in the spin (actually up to fourth order as pointed out in Ref.
[
114
]
):
this is due to the fact that starting from this order we should start considering also the higher-order commutators, that scale as
\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{n})
, with
n\geq 1
and are related to the quantum contributions to the scattering.

Nonetheless, these contributions are strongly suppressed as
\hbar\ll 1
and we can reasonably assume Eq. (
6.40a
) to be exact.

The goal is now to construct some purely classical soft recursion relations, that relate the four-point classical amplitude to the three point one.

Let us consider the quantum soft recursion relations:

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{4}^{(s)}(p_{1},p_{2},k_{3},k_{4})=\frac{\kappa}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{\left(p_{i}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}\right)^{2}}{p_{i}\cdot k_{4}}\exp\left(i\frac{k_{4,\mu}\varepsilon_{4,\nu}J^{\mu\nu}_{i}}{p_{i}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}}\right){\cal M}_{3}^{(s)}(p_{1},p_{2},k_{3})\,

(6.42)

an educated guess consists in replacing them with their classical counterpart

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(0)}_{4,\rm cl}(v_{1},v_{2},\bar{k}_{3},\bar{k}_{4})=\frac{\kappa}{2\hbar^{3/2}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{2}m\frac{\left(v_{i}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}\right)^{2}}{v_{i}\cdot\bar{k}_{4}}\right]\exp\left(-i\frac{\bar{F}_{4,\mu\nu}\tilde{S}^{\mu\nu}}{v\cdot\varepsilon_{4}}\right)\mathcal{M}^{(0)}_{3,\rm cl}(v_{1},\bar{k}_{3})\,

(6.43)

where once again the gauge freedom is used to fix
\bar{k}_{i}\cdot\varepsilon_{j}=0
for
i,j=3,4
.

Substituting now
\mathcal{M}^{(0)}_{3,\rm cl}(v_{1},\bar{k}_{3})
in Eq. (
6.43
) we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(0)}_{4,\rm cl}(v_{1},v_{2},\bar{k}_{3},\bar{k}_{4})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\kappa^{2}}{2\hbar^{2}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{2}m\frac{\left(v_{i}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}\right)^{2}}{v_{i}\cdot\bar{k}_{4}}\right]m^{2}\left(v_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3}\right)^{2}\times

(6.44)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\exp\left(-i\frac{\bar{F}_{4,\mu\nu}\tilde{S}^{\mu\nu}}{v\cdot\varepsilon_{4}}\right)\exp\left(-i\frac{\bar{F}_{3,\mu\nu}\tilde{S}^{\mu\nu}}{v\cdot\varepsilon_{3}}\right)\,.

Let us focus on the first line of Eq. (
6.44
)

\displaystyle-\frac{\kappa^{2}}{2\hbar^{2}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{2}m\frac{\left(v_{i}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}\right)^{2}}{v_{i}\cdot\bar{k}_{4}}\right]m^{2}\left(v_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3}\right)^{2}=-\frac{\kappa^{2}}{2\hbar^{2}}\left[m\frac{\left(v_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}\right)^{2}}{v_{1}\cdot\bar{k}_{4}}+m\frac{\left(v_{2}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}\right)^{2}}{v_{2}\cdot\bar{k}_{4}}\right]m^{2}\left(v_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3}\right)^{2}=

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=\frac{\kappa^{2}m^{2}}{2\hbar}\dfrac{\left(v_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}\right)^{2}\left(v_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3}\right)^{2}(\bar{k}_{3}\cdot\bar{k}_{4})^{2}}{(v_{1}\cdot\bar{k}_{4})(v_{2}\cdot\bar{k}_{4})(\bar{k}_{3}\cdot\bar{k}_{4})}=\frac{1}{\hbar}\frac{\kappa^{2}m^{2}\bar{\omega}_{(0)}^{2}}{(2\,v_{1}\cdot\bar{k}_{4})(2\,v_{2}\cdot\bar{k}_{4})(2\,\bar{k}_{3}\cdot\bar{k}_{4})}\,.

(6.45)

Recalling now that we the momenta
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are aligned, we can write

-\frac{\kappa^{2}}{2\hbar^{2}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{2}m\frac{\left(v_{i}\cdot\varepsilon_{4}\right)^{2}}{v_{i}\cdot\bar{k}_{4}}\right]m^{2}\left(v_{1}\cdot\varepsilon_{3}\right)^{2}=\frac{\kappa^{2}m^{2}\bar{\omega}_{(0)}^{2}}{8\hbar(v\cdot\bar{k}_{4})^{2}(\bar{k}_{3}\cdot\bar{k}_{4})}\,,

(6.46)

which matches precisely the scalar contribution of
\mathcal{M}_{4\rm cl}^{(0)}(v,\bar{k}_{3},\bar{k}_{4})
.

Let us now consider the exponentials. Notice that on the three-point kinematics we can trade the classical spin operator with the classical angular momentum operator,
i.e.

\dfrac{F_{\mu\nu}J^{\mu\nu}}{2\,p\cdot\varepsilon}=-\dfrac{F_{\mu\nu}S^{\mu\nu}}{p\cdot\varepsilon}\,.

(6.47)

We can then perform the same procedure as in Sec.
6.2
.
Notice however that after expliciting all
\hbar
-s, Eq. (
6.22
) becomes:

\left[J^{\mu\nu},J^{\rho\sigma}\right]=-i\hbar\left(\eta^{\mu\rho}J^{\nu\sigma}+\eta^{\nu\sigma}J^{\mu\rho}-\eta^{\mu\sigma}J^{\nu\rho}-\eta^{\nu\rho}J^{\mu\sigma}\right)\,,

(6.48)

therefore, apparently, another power of
\hbar
arises when taking the commutator.
However in a purely classical setting the commutator should be replaced by the corresponding Poisson brackets.
Let us discuss in detail in what they differ.

Both classical mechanics and quantum mechanics use bi-linear brackets of variables with similar algebraic properties. In classical mechanics the variables are functions of the canonical
coordinates and momenta, and the Poisson bracket of two such variables
A(q,p)
and
B(q,p)
are defined as

[A,B]_{P}=\sum_{i}\left(\frac{\partial A}{\partial q_{i}}\frac{\partial B}{\partial p_{i}}-\frac{\partial A}{\partial p_{i}}\frac{\partial B}{\partial q_{i}}\right)\,.

(6.49)

In quantum mechanics the variables are linear operators in some Hilbert space, and the commutator bracket of two operators is

[A,B]_{C}=AB-BA\,.

(6.50)

Both types of brackets share similar algebraic properties: they are linear, antisymmetric, satisfy the Leibniz rule, and possess the Jacobi identity.

Also, both types of brackets involving the Hamiltonian can be used to describe the time dependence of the classical/quantum variables. In classical mechanics,

\frac{d}{dt}A(q,p)=[A,H]_{P}\,,

(6.51)

where
H
is the Hamiltonian operator and I have used the Hamilton equations.

Meanwhile in quantum-mechanics, and in particular in the Heisenberg picture, we have

i\hbar\frac{d}{dt}\hat{A}=[\hat{A},\hat{H}]_{C}\,.

(6.52)

Furthermore, it is well known that, once we generalize the Poisson brackets to the non-commuting variables of quantum mechanics, they become proportional to the commutator brackets,
i.e.

[\hat{A},\hat{B}]_{P}=\frac{[\hat{A},\hat{B}]_{C}}{i\hbar}

(6.53)

Thus the correct
\hbar
scaling is restored also in the classical computation and with the same procedure it is possible to check that also the exponentials match.

7
The tree-level four- and five- point Amplitude in the Soft Expansion

In this section, I start by computing the four-point amplitude corresponding to the scattering of two Kerr black holes with different masses and spins. I then apply the soft theorems discussed in Sec.
5
to derive the corresponding five-point amplitude.

This amplitude is crucial as it relates to the emission of radiation, allowing us to compute a waveform using the KMOC formalism explained in Sec.
3
. Moreover, within the KMOC approach, this amplitude is the only one relevant at leading order.

7.1
Four-point Amplitude

Let us begin by computing the four-point amplitude. This amplitude is of particular interest because it contains the complete information about the classical 1PM scattering of two spinning black holes without radiation emission.

Consider two Kerr black holes with spins
s_{A}
and
s_{B}
and masses
m_{A}
and
m_{B}
, respectively.

To compute the four-point amplitude, we would typically consider contributions from the
s
,
t
, and
u
channels. However, the only contribution relevant to classical scattering arises from the
t
-channel.
Therefore, to compute this amplitude, we can utilize the BCFW recursion relations discussed in Sec.
5.1
, calculating the residue of the scattering amplitude in Fig. (
6
) just at the pole
t\equiv k^{2}=0
on finite complex kinematics and then analytically continuing the result to real kinematics at a later stage.

Figure 6
:
4-pt amplitude. Figure reproduced from Ref.
[
100
]

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{4}^{(s_{A},s_{B})}(p_{1},-p_{2},p_{3},-p_{4})=\sum_{h=\pm}\mathcal{M}_{3}^{(s_{A})}(p_{1},-p_{2},k^{h})\dfrac{-1}{t}\mathcal{M}_{3}^{(s_{B})}(p_{3},-p_{4},k^{-h})+\mathcal{O}(t^{0})=

\displaystyle\qquad\quad=\frac{-(\kappa/2)^{2}}{m_{A}^{2s_{A}-2}m_{B}^{2s_{B}-2}t}\Bigl(x_{A}^{2}x_{B}^{-2}\langle\mathbf{2}\mathbf{1}\rangle^{\odot 2s_{A}}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{3}]^{\odot 2s_{B}}+x_{A}^{-2}x_{B}^{2}[\mathbf{2}\mathbf{1}]^{\odot 2s_{A}}\langle\mathbf{4}\mathbf{3}\rangle^{\odot 2s_{B}}\Bigr)+\mathcal{O}(t^{0})\,.

(7.1)

It is now convenient to introduce the following kinematic variables

x_{A}/x_{B}=\gamma(1-v)\,,\quad x_{B}/x_{A}=\gamma(1+v)\,,\quad\text{with}\quad\gamma=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-v^{2}}}=\dfrac{p_{A}\cdot p_{B}}{m_{A}m_{B}}=u_{A}\cdot u_{B}\,.

(7.2)

Following the procedure outlined in Sec.
4.4
, we can thus write:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{4}^{(s_{A},s_{B})}

\displaystyle=\frac{-(\kappa/2)^{2}\gamma^{2}}{m_{A}^{2s_{A}-2}m_{B}^{2s_{B}-2}t}\,\times

\displaystyle\times\Bigl((1-v)^{2}\{U_{12}\langle\mathbf{1}|\}^{\odot 2s_{A}}e^{-k\cdot a_{A}}|\mathbf{1}\rangle^{\odot 2s_{A}}\{U_{34}[\mathbf{3}|\}^{\odot 2s_{B}}e^{-k\cdot a_{B}}|\mathbf{3}]^{\odot 2s_{B}}+

(7.3)

\displaystyle\qquad+(1+v)^{2}\{U_{12}[\mathbf{1}|\}^{\odot 2s_{A}}e^{k\cdot a_{A}}|\mathbf{1}]^{\odot 2s_{A}}\{U_{34}\langle\mathbf{3}|\}^{\odot 2s_{B}}e^{k\cdot a_{B}}|\mathbf{3}\rangle^{\odot 2s_{B}}\Bigr)+\mathcal{O}(t^{0})\,.

It is now useful to perform a manipulation of these exponentials to obtain a more convenient expression.
We recall that on the pole kinematics,
k^{2}=0
; furthermore, due to the transversality of the exchange momentum,
p_{A}\cdot k=p_{B}\cdot k=0
, and by the spin supplementary condition (SSC),
p_{A}\cdot a_{A}=p_{B}\cdot a_{B}=0
.

Therefore the following relations hold true

13
13

13

These equalities can be derived by squaring the left-hand
sides and computing the resulting Gram determinants, using the fact that
\epsilon^{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{n}}\epsilon_{\beta_{1}\dots\beta_{n}}=\delta^{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{n}}_{\beta_{1}\dots\beta_{n}}
.
:

i\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}p_{A}^{\mu}p_{B}^{\nu}k^{\rho}a_{A}^{\sigma}=m_{A}m_{B}\gamma v(k\cdot a_{A})\,,\quad i\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}p_{A}^{\mu}p_{B}^{\nu}k^{\rho}a_{B}^{\sigma}=m_{A}m_{B}\gamma v(k\cdot a_{B})\,.

(7.4)

Hence, if we define

w^{\mu\nu}=\frac{2p_{A}^{[\mu}p_{B}^{\nu]}}{m_{A}m_{B}\gamma v}\,,\qquad[w*a]_{\mu}=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}w^{\alpha\beta}a^{\nu}\,,

(7.5)

and strip the unitary transition factors

U_{12}^{\odot 2s_{A}}
and
U_{34}^{\odot 2s_{B}}
via the GEV,
we obtain the classical limit of the scattering amplitude Eq. (
7.3
) as

\langle\mathcal{M}_{4}(k)\rangle=-\Big(\frac{\kappa}{2}\Big)^{\!2}\frac{m_{A}^{2}m_{B}^{2}}{k^{2}}\gamma^{2}\sum_{\pm}(1\pm v)^{2}\exp[{\pm i}\bigl(k\cdot(w*a_{0})\bigr)]\,,

(7.6)

where
a_{0}^{\mu}=a_{A}^{\mu}+a_{B}^{\mu}
is the total spin pseudo-vector.

7.2
Five-point Amplitude

In this section, I will compute the five-point amplitude

14
14

14

This section has been inspired by the notes on the spinning waveforms based on results of Riccardo Gonzo and inspired by discussions with Marc Canaj, David Kosower, Pavel Novichkov and Arshia Momeni.

.

Before delving into the full calculations, I will first review and summarize key results from the previous sections.

7.2.1
Preliminaries

Our starting point is the 4-point amplitude in Fig. (
7
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{4}^{s_{A},s_{B}}(p_{1},\,p_{2},-p_{3},-p_{4})

\displaystyle=\frac{8\pi G}{m_{A}^{2s_{A}-2}m_{B}^{2s_{B}-2}(p_{1}+p_{3})^{2}}\,\times

\displaystyle\times\big(\gamma^{2}(1-v)^{2}\langle\mathbf{3}\mathbf{1}\rangle^{2s_{A}}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}+\gamma^{2}(1+v)^{2}[\mathbf{3}\mathbf{1}]^{2s_{A}}\langle\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}\rangle^{2s_{B}}\big)\,,

where we work with all external momenta incoming and we have defined

\gamma=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{1-v^{2}}}=\dfrac{p_{1}\!\cdot p_{2}}{m_{1}m_{2}}

.

p_{1}
p_{2}
p_{3}
p_{4}
p_{1}+p_{3}

Figure 7
:
Four-point amplitude with all incoming external momenta

To simplify the calculations, I will restrict to the case where the particle A is spinless,
i.e.
s_{A}=0
.
The amplitude then becomes:

\mathcal{M}_{4}^{0,s_{B}}(p_{1},\,p_{2},\,p_{3},\,p_{4})=\frac{8\pi Gm_{A}^{2}}{m_{B}^{2s_{B}-2}(p_{1}+p_{3})^{2}}\big(\gamma^{2}(1-v)^{2}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}+\gamma^{2}(1+v)^{2}\langle\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}\rangle^{2s_{B}}\big)

(7.7)

It is also convenient to define the function
I_{\pm}(p_{1}\cdot p_{2})\equiv\gamma^{2}(1\pm v)^{2}
. So,

\mathcal{M}_{4}^{0,s_{B}}(p_{1},\,p_{2},\,p_{3},\,p_{4})=\frac{8\pi Gm_{A}^{2}}{m_{B}^{2s_{B}-2}(p_{1}+p_{3})^{2}}\big(I_{-}(p_{1}\cdot p_{2})[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}+I_{+}(p_{1}\cdot p_{2})\langle\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}\rangle^{2s_{B}}\big)

(7.8)

As outlined earlier, our aim is to apply the soft theorems discussed in Sec.
5
to derive the five-point amplitude with an emitted graviton starting from the four-point amplitude in Eq. (
7.8
).

Our strategy consists of shifting the four-point amplitude in the soft limit,
\epsilon\to 0
; then, multiplying it by the appropriate soft factors, we are able to reconstruct the five-point amplitude in the soft limit, where the additional leg corresponds to the soft graviton, labeled as particle
5
.

This approach is somewhat unconventional, as the soft limit is typically used to study the infrared behavior of an amplitude. In such cases, one generally starts with a higher-point amplitude and expands it to express it as a lower-point amplitude multiplied by soft factors.
However, by utilizing Eq. (
5.37
), we can reverse this process, provided we remain consistent with our definition of the shifts.

The natural BCFW shifts we want to use are the ones in Eq. (
5.10
):

\hat{p}_{5}=\epsilon p_{5}-zq_{5}\,,\quad\hat{p}_{1}=p_{1}-\frac{(\epsilon-1)(p_{5}p_{3})-z(q_{5}p_{3})}{q_{1}p_{3}}q_{1}\,,\quad\hat{p}_{3}=p_{3}-\frac{(\epsilon-1)(p_{5}p_{1})-z(q_{5}p_{1})}{q_{3}p_{1}}q_{3}\,,

(7.9)

where
q_{5}
,
q_{1}
,
q_{3}
are four-vectors satisfying

q_{5}^{2}=q_{1}^{2}=q_{3}^{2}=q_{5}p_{5}=q_{1}p_{1}=q_{3}p_{3}=0\,,\qquad q_{1}p_{5}=q_{3}p_{5}=q_{1}q_{5}=q_{3}q_{5}=q_{1}q_{3}=0\,.

(7.10)

Once again, we choose the the shift vectors
q
as

q_{5}\sigma=y\tilde{\lambda}_{5}\,,\qquad q_{1}\sigma=p_{1}\sigma\tilde{\lambda}_{5}\tilde{\lambda}_{5}\,,\qquad q_{3}\sigma=p_{3}\sigma\tilde{\lambda}_{5}\tilde{\lambda}_{5}\,.

(7.11)

We recall that at the spinor level, this shift is achieved by promoting the momenta to be complex, thereby making the holomorphic and antiholomorphic spinors as independent. The shift is then implemented as follows:

\displaystyle\{5\bar{\mathbf{1}}\bar{\mathbf{3}}\}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\qquad\lambda_{5}^{z}

\displaystyle=\epsilon\lambda_{5}-zy\,,

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}_{1}^{z}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\chi}_{1}+\frac{(\epsilon-1)\langle 5p_{3}5]-z\langle yp_{3}5]}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}[\mathbf{1}5]\tilde{\lambda}_{5}\,,

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}_{3}^{z}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\chi}_{3}-\frac{(\epsilon-1)\langle 5p_{1}5]-z\langle yp_{1}5]}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}[\mathbf{3}5]\tilde{\lambda}_{5}\,,

(7.12)

while keeping all other spinors fixed.

The poles
z_{l}
are located at
\hat{P}_{5l}^{2}\big|_{z=z_{l}}-m_{l}^{2}=0
, so

z_{l}=\epsilon\dfrac{p_{5}\cdot p_{l}}{q_{5}\cdot p_{l}}=\epsilon\dfrac{\langle 5p_{l}5]}{\langle yp_{l}5]}\,,

(7.13)

and the shifted spinors evaluated at the poles location are:

\displaystyle\lambda_{5}^{z_{l}}

\displaystyle=\epsilon\frac{\langle y5\rangle}{\langle yp_{l}5]}p_{l}\sigma\tilde{\lambda}_{5}\,,

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}_{1}^{z_{l}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\chi}_{1}-\frac{2p_{5}p_{3}}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}[\mathbf{1}5]\tilde{\lambda}_{5}+\epsilon\frac{\langle y5\rangle[5p_{3}p_{l}5]}{\langle yp_{l}5][5p_{1}p_{3}5]}[\mathbf{1}5]\tilde{\lambda}_{5}\,,

(7.14)

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}_{3}^{z_{l}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\chi}_{3}+\frac{2p_{5}p_{1}}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}[\mathbf{3}5]\tilde{\lambda}_{5}-\epsilon\frac{\langle y5\rangle[5p_{1}p_{l}5]}{\langle yp_{l}5][5p_{1}p_{3}5]}[\mathbf{3}5]\tilde{\lambda}_{5}\,.

Finally we can rewrite
\hat{P}_{5l}\sigma\big|_{z_{l}}
, that being evaluated at the poles is on-shell, as

\displaystyle\hat{P}_{5l}\sigma|_{z_{l}}=p_{l}\sigma+\epsilon\dfrac{\langle y5\rangle}{\langle yp_{l}5]}p_{l}\sigma\tilde{\lambda}_{5}\tilde{\lambda}_{5}\,,\,\,\,\Rightarrow\,\,\,\chi_{5l}=\chi_{l}\,,\quad\tilde{\chi}_{5l}=\tilde{\chi}_{l}+\epsilon\frac{\langle y5\rangle}{\langle yp_{l}5]}[\mathbf{l}5]\tilde{\lambda}_{5}\,,\quad l\neq 1,3,

\displaystyle\hat{P}_{51}\sigma|_{z_{1}}=p_{1}\sigma-\frac{2p_{5}p_{3}}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}p_{1}\sigma\tilde{\lambda}_{5}\tilde{\lambda}_{5}\,,\,\Rightarrow\,\chi_{51}=\chi_{1}\,,\quad\tilde{\chi}_{51}=\tilde{\chi}_{1}-\frac{2p_{5}p_{3}}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}[\mathbf{1}5]\tilde{\lambda}_{5}\,,

(7.15)

\displaystyle\hat{P}_{53}\sigma|_{z_{3}}=p_{3}\sigma-\frac{2p_{5}p_{1}}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}p_{3}\sigma\tilde{\lambda}_{5}\tilde{\lambda}_{5}\,,\,\Rightarrow\,\chi_{53}=\chi_{3}\,,\quad\tilde{\chi}_{53}=\tilde{\chi}_{1}-\frac{2p_{5}p_{1}}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}[\mathbf{3}5]\tilde{\lambda}_{5}\,.

Using now the soft theorems discussed in Sec.
5
we have:

\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{0,\epsilon}_{5}(\mathbf{1},\mathbf{2},\mathbf{3},\mathbf{4},5_{h}^{+})=\bigg\{\frac{1}{\epsilon^{3}}S_{+2}^{(0)}+\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}S_{+2}^{(1)}+\frac{1}{\epsilon}S_{+2}^{(0)}\bigg\}\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{0,0}_{4}(\hat{\mathbf{1}},\mathbf{2},\hat{\mathbf{3}},\mathbf{4})+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{0})\,,

(7.16)

where

S^{(0)}_{+2}=\frac{\kappa}{2}\sum_{l=1}^{4}\frac{\langle yp_{l}5]^{2}}{(2p_{5}\cdot p_{l})\langle 5y\rangle^{2}}\,,\quad S^{(1)}_{+2}=\frac{\kappa}{2}\sum_{l=1}^{4}\frac{\langle yp_{l}5]}{(2p_{5}\cdot p_{l})\langle 5y\rangle}\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}\,,\quad S^{(2)}_{+2}=\frac{\kappa}{4}\sum_{l=1}^{4}\frac{1}{4p_{5}\cdot p_{l}}\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}\,,

(7.17)

and we have defined the quantities

\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}=\tilde{d}_{l}+\frac{[5p_{3}p_{l}5]}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}[5\mathbf{1}][5\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\chi}_{1}}]-\frac{[5p_{1}p_{l}5]}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}[5\mathbf{3}][5\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\chi}_{3}}]\,,\qquad\tilde{d}_{l}=[5\mathbf{l}][5\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\chi}_{l}}]\,,

(7.18)

with
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}=0
for l = 1, 3.

Notice that for
\epsilon=0
,

\hat{P}_{5l}\sigma\big|_{z_{l},\epsilon=0}=p_{l}\sigma\big|_{\epsilon=0}\,,\qquad\tilde{\chi}_{1}^{z_{l}}\big|_{\epsilon=0}=\tilde{\chi}_{1}-\frac{2p_{5}p_{3}}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}[\mathbf{1}5]\tilde{\lambda}_{5}\,,\qquad\tilde{\chi}_{3}^{z_{l}}\big|_{\epsilon=0}=\tilde{\chi}_{3}+\frac{2p_{5}p_{1}}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}[\mathbf{3}5]\tilde{\lambda}_{5}\,.

(7.19)

7.2.2
Explicit soft factors

I will now compute Eq. (
7.16
) explicitly.

First, I will shift the four-point amplitude Eq. (
7.8
) to obtain its shifted version,
\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{0,0}
. Note that the trivial shift corresponds to
(0,1)
, not
(0,0)
. Therefore, to transition from Eq. (
7.8
) to its shifted version, I will apply the shift to the momenta
p_{1}\to\hat{p}_{1}
and
p_{3}\to\hat{p}_{3}
:

\hat{\mathcal{M}}_{4}^{0,s_{B}}(\hat{p}_{1},\,p_{2},\,\hat{p}_{3},\,p_{4})\Big|_{\epsilon=0}=\frac{8\pi Gm_{A}^{2}}{m_{B}^{2s_{B}-2}(p_{1}+p_{3})^{2}}\big(\hat{I}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}+\hat{I}_{+}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\langle\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}\rangle^{2s_{B}}\big)

(7.20)

Let us now focus on the functions
\hat{I}_{\pm}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})
and see how they are related to their un-shifted version
I_{\pm}(p_{1}\cdot p_{2})
.
To this end we find convenient to write
I_{\pm}(p_{1}\cdot p_{2})
explicitly as:

\displaystyle I_{\pm}(p_{1}\cdot p_{2})

\displaystyle=\gamma^{2}(1\pm v)^{2}=

(7.21)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{p_{1}\cdot p_{2}}{m_{1}m_{2}}\left(1\pm\sqrt{1-\frac{m_{1}^{2}m_{2}^{2}}{(p_{1}\cdot p_{2})^{2}}}\right)\right)^{2}=\left(\frac{p_{1}\cdot p_{2}\pm\sqrt{(p_{1}\cdot p_{2})^{2}-m_{1}^{2}m_{2}^{2}}}{m_{1}m_{2}}\right)^{2}\,.

Thus
\hat{I}_{\pm}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})
is nothing but:

I_{\pm}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})=\left(\frac{\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2}\pm\sqrt{(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})^{2}-m_{1}^{2}m_{2}^{2}}}{m_{1}m_{2}}\right)^{2}\,.

(7.22)

We can now easily express the product
\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2}
in terms of the un-shifted spinors using Eq. (
7.19
) and the identity:

\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2}=\frac{1}{2}\langle\hat{\mathbf{1}}\mathbf{2}\rangle[\mathbf{2}\hat{\mathbf{1}}]\,.

(7.23)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\langle\hat{\mathbf{1}}\mathbf{2}\rangle[\mathbf{2}\hat{\mathbf{1}}]=

(7.24)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\langle{\mathbf{1}}\mathbf{2}\rangle[\mathbf{2}{\mathbf{1}}]-\frac{p_{5}p_{3}}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}\langle\mathbf{1}\mathbf{2}\rangle[\mathbf{1}5][\mathbf{2}5]=p_{1}\cdot p_{2}-\frac{p_{5}p_{3}}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}[\mathbf{1}5]\langle\mathbf{1}\mathbf{2}\rangle[\mathbf{2}5]\,.

We are now ready to compute the desired five-point amplitude.

For simplicity, I will work out the terms in Eq. (
7.16
) one by one, starting with
S^{(0)}_{+2}
.
Remembering that
\kappa^{2}=32\pi G
, we have:

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{\epsilon^{3}}S_{+2}^{(0)}\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{0,0}_{4}(\hat{\mathbf{1}},\mathbf{2},\hat{\mathbf{3}},\mathbf{4})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt=\dfrac{1}{\epsilon^{3}}\left(\dfrac{\kappa}{2}\right)^{\!3}\dfrac{m_{A}^{2}}{m_{B}^{2s_{B}-2}(p_{1}+p_{3})^{2}}\,\times

(7.25)

\displaystyle\times\sum_{l=1}^{4}\frac{\langle yp_{l}5]^{2}}{(2p_{5}\cdot p_{l})\langle 5y\rangle^{2}}\left(\hat{I}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}+\hat{I}_{+}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\langle\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}\rangle^{2s_{B}}\right)\,.

I then compute the term
S^{(1)}_{+2}
:

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}S_{+2}^{(1)}\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{0,0}_{4}(\hat{\mathbf{1}},\mathbf{2},\hat{\mathbf{3}},\mathbf{4})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt=\dfrac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}\right)^{\!3}\dfrac{m_{A}^{2}}{m_{B}^{2s_{B}-2}(p_{1}+p_{3})^{2}}\,\times

(7.26)

\displaystyle\times\sum_{l=1}^{4}\dfrac{\langle yp_{l}5]}{(2p_{5}\cdot p_{l})\langle 5y\rangle}\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}\left(\hat{I}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}+\hat{I}_{+}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\langle\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}\rangle^{2s_{B}}\right)\,.

Notice now that the operator
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}
is simply the realization of the derivative
\frac{\partial}{\partial\epsilon}
, hence it behaves as the usual derivative, in the sense that it satisfies the usual Leibniz product rule and the chain rule. Hence,

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}\left(\hat{I}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}+\hat{I}_{+}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\langle\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}\rangle^{2s_{B}}\right)=

(7.27)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\left(\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}(\hat{I}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\right)[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}+\hat{I}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\left(\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}\right)+\left(\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}\hat{I}_{+}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\right)\langle\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}\rangle^{2s_{B}}\,+

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+\hat{I}_{+}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\left(\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}\langle\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}\rangle^{2s_{B}}\right)

Let us examine each of these contributions individually. Consider the first and the third terms in Eq. (
7.27
):

\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}(\hat{I}_{\pm}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2}))=\frac{\partial\big(\hat{I}_{\pm}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\big)}{\partial(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})}\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\equiv\hat{I}^{\prime}_{\pm}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\,,

(7.28)

where we have defined the function

\hat{I}^{\prime}_{\pm}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\equiv\frac{\partial\big(\hat{I}_{\pm}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\big)}{\partial(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})}=\pm\frac{2}{m_{1}^{2}m_{2}^{2}}\dfrac{\left(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2}\pm\sqrt{(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})^{2}-m_{1}^{2}m_{2}^{2}}\right)^{2}}{\sqrt{(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})^{2}-m_{1}^{2}m_{2}^{2}}}

(7.29)

We recall that
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}=0
for
l=1,3
; so we are left to compute just
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{2}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})
and
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{4}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})
.
To compute such derivatives, I will use the parity conjugate version of the identity in Eq. (
4.55
), that I rewrite below for convenience:

[k\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\chi}_{p}^{b}}]|p^{a}]=\delta^{a}_{b}|k]\,.

(7.30)

So,

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{2}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})

\displaystyle=\left([5\mathbf{2}][5\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\chi}_{2}}]+\dfrac{[5p_{3}p_{2}3]}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}[5\mathbf{1}][5\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\chi}_{1}}]-\dfrac{[5p_{1}p_{2}5]}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}[5\mathbf{3}][5\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\chi}_{3}}]\right)\left(\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathbf{1}\mathbf{2}\rangle[\mathbf{2}\hat{\mathbf{1}}]\right)=

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2}[5\mathbf{1}]\langle\mathbf{1}\mathbf{2}\rangle[\mathbf{2}5]+\dfrac{1}{2}\dfrac{[5p_{3}p_{2}5]}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}[5\mathbf{1}]\langle\mathbf{1}\mathbf{2}\rangle[\mathbf{2}5]=

(7.31a)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}[5\mathbf{1}]\langle\mathbf{1}\mathbf{2}\rangle[\mathbf{2}5]\left(1+\dfrac{[5p_{3}p_{2}5]}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{4}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})

\displaystyle=\left([5\mathbf{4}][5\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\chi}_{4}}]+\dfrac{[5p_{3}p_{4}3]}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}[5\mathbf{1}][5\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\chi}_{1}}]-\dfrac{[5p_{1}p_{4}5]}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}[5\mathbf{3}][5\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\chi}_{3}}]\right)\left(\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathbf{1}\mathbf{2}\rangle[\mathbf{2}\hat{\mathbf{1}}]\right)=

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2}[5\mathbf{1}]\langle\mathbf{1}\mathbf{2}\rangle[\mathbf{2}5]\dfrac{[5p_{3}p_{4}5]}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}\,.

(7.31b)

Consider now the second term in Eq. (
7.27
). In this case the derivative acts only on the un-shifted spinors, so we simply have:

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{2}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}

\displaystyle=\left([5\mathbf{2}][5\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\chi}_{2}}]\right)[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}=\tilde{d}_{2}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}

(7.32a)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{4}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}

\displaystyle=\left([5\mathbf{4}][5\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\chi}_{4}}]\right)[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}=\tilde{d}_{4}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}

(7.32b)

Furthermore, it is straightforward to check that:
\tilde{d}_{2}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}=-\tilde{d}_{4}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}
.

At last, consider the fourth term in. in Eq. (
7.27
).
As we are working with complex momenta, holomorphic and anti-holomorphic spinors are completely independent, thus

\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{2}\langle\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}\rangle^{2s_{B}}=\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{4}\langle\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}\rangle^{2s_{B}}=0\,.

(7.33)

We can now finally substitute Eq. (
7.27
) in Eq. (
7.26
) to get:

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}S_{+2}^{(1)}\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{0,0}_{4}(\hat{\mathbf{1}},\mathbf{2},\hat{\mathbf{3}},\mathbf{4})

\displaystyle=

(7.34)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\left(\dfrac{\kappa}{2}\right)^{\!3}\dfrac{m_{A}^{2}}{m_{B}^{2s_{B}-2}(p_{1}+p_{3})^{2}}\,\times

\displaystyle\times\Biggl\{\dfrac{\langle yp_{2}5]}{(2p_{5}\cdot p_{2})\langle 5y\rangle}\biggl(\hat{I}^{\prime}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{2}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}+\hat{I}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\big(\tilde{d}_{2}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}\big)\,+

\displaystyle+\hat{I}^{\prime}_{+}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{2}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\langle\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}\rangle^{2s_{B}}\biggr)\,+

\displaystyle+\dfrac{\langle yp_{4}5]}{(2p_{5}\cdot p_{4})\langle 5y\rangle}\biggl(\hat{I}^{\prime}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{4}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}+\hat{I}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\big(\tilde{d}_{4}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}\big)\,+

\displaystyle+\hat{I}^{\prime}_{+}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{4}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\langle\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}\rangle^{2s_{B}}\biggr)\,\Biggr\}=

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\left(\dfrac{\kappa}{2}\right)^{\!3}\dfrac{m_{A}^{2}}{m_{B}^{2s_{B}-2}(p_{1}+p_{3})^{2}}\,\times

\displaystyle\times\Biggl\{\left[\dfrac{\langle yp_{2}5]}{(4p_{5}\cdot p_{2})\langle 5y\rangle}\left(1+\dfrac{[5p_{3}p_{2}5]}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}\right)+\dfrac{\langle yp_{4}5]}{(4p_{5}\cdot p_{4})\langle 5y\rangle}\dfrac{[5p_{3}p_{4}5]}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}\right][5\mathbf{1}]\langle\mathbf{1}\mathbf{2}\rangle[\mathbf{2}5]\,\times

\displaystyle\times\left(\hat{I}^{\prime}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}+\hat{I}^{\prime}_{+}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\langle\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}\rangle^{2s_{B}}\right)\,+

\displaystyle+\left[\dfrac{\langle yp_{2}5]}{(2p_{5}\cdot p_{2})\langle 5y\rangle}-\dfrac{\langle yp_{4}5]}{(2p_{5}\cdot p_{4})\langle 5y\rangle}\right]\hat{I}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\,\big(\tilde{d}_{2}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}\big)\Biggr\}\,.

I then compute the term
S^{(2)}_{+2}
:

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{\epsilon}S_{+2}^{(2)}\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{0,0}_{4}(\hat{\mathbf{1}},\mathbf{2},\hat{\mathbf{3}},\mathbf{4})

\displaystyle=

(7.35)

\displaystyle\hskip-71.13188pt=\dfrac{1}{\epsilon}\left(\dfrac{\kappa}{2}\right)^{\!3}\dfrac{m_{A}^{2}}{m_{B}^{2s_{B}-2}(p_{1}+p_{3})^{2}}\sum_{l=1}^{4}\frac{1}{4p_{5}\cdot p_{l}}\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}\left(\hat{I}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}+\hat{I}_{+}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\langle\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}\rangle^{2s_{B}}\right)\,.

Let us now focus on this second derivative:

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}\left(\hat{I}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}+\hat{I}_{+}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\langle\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}\rangle^{2s_{B}}\right)\right)=

(7.36)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}\biggl(\hat{I}^{\prime}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}+\hat{I}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\left(\tilde{d}_{l}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}\right)\,+

\displaystyle+\hat{I}^{\prime}_{+}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\langle\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}\rangle^{2s_{B}}\biggr)=

\displaystyle=\hat{I}^{\prime\prime}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\left(\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\right)^{\!2}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}+\hat{I}^{\prime}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\,\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\right)[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}\,+

\displaystyle+2\,\hat{I}^{\prime}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\tilde{d}_{l}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}+\hat{I}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\,\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}\left(\tilde{d}_{l}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}\right)\,+

\displaystyle+\hat{I}^{\prime\prime}_{+}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\left(\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\right)^{\!2}\langle\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}\rangle^{2s_{B}}+\hat{I}^{\prime}_{+}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\,\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\right)\langle\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}\rangle^{2s_{B}}\,,

where we have defined the function

\displaystyle\hat{I}^{\prime\prime}_{\pm}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\partial{}^{\,2}\big(\hat{I}_{\pm}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\big)}{\partial(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})^{2}}=

(7.37)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{2\left(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2}\pm\sqrt{(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})^{2}-m_{1}^{2}m_{2}^{2}}\right)^{2}}{m_{1}^{2}m_{2}^{2}\left[(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})^{2}-m_{1}^{2}m_{2}^{2}\right]^{3/2}}\left(2\sqrt{(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})^{2}-m_{1}^{2}m_{2}^{2}}\mp\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2}\right)\,.

Let’s examine, once again, each of the new contributions individually.
Consider the second and and sixth terms in Eq. (
7.36
):

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{2}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{2}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\right)=\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{2}\left(\frac{1}{2}[5\mathbf{1}]\langle\mathbf{1}\mathbf{2}\rangle[\mathbf{2}5]\left(1+\dfrac{[5p_{3}p_{2}5]}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}\right)\right)\propto[55]=0\,,

(7.38)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{4}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{4}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\right)=\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{4}\left(\dfrac{1}{2}[5\mathbf{1}]\langle\mathbf{1}\mathbf{2}\rangle[\mathbf{2}5]\dfrac{[5p_{3}p_{4}5]}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}\right)\propto[55]=0\,.

It is worth noting that this outcome is expected: although the amplitude itself has no specific
\epsilon
-dependence, the momentum shifts are linear in
\epsilon
. Given now that
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{l}
is merely the realization via the chain rule of
\frac{\partial}{\partial\epsilon}
, it follows that the second derivative vanishes when acting on (the linear
\epsilon
-dependent term)
\hat{p_{1}}\cdot p_{2}
.

Consider now the fourth term in Eq. (
7.36
). As for Eq. (
7.32
) it is straightforward to check that:

\begin{gathered}\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{2}\left(\tilde{d}_{2}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}\right)=\tilde{d}_{2}\left(\tilde{d}_{2}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}\right)\,,\qquad\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{4}\left(\tilde{d}_{4}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}\right)=\tilde{d}_{4}\left(\tilde{d}_{4}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}\right)\,,\\
\tilde{d}_{2}\left(\tilde{d}_{2}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}\right)=\tilde{d}_{4}\left(\tilde{d}_{4}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}\right)\,.\end{gathered}

(7.39)

We can now finally substitute Eq. (
7.36
) in Eq. (
7.35
) to get:

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{\epsilon}S_{+2}^{(2)}\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{0,0}_{4}(\hat{\mathbf{1}},\mathbf{2},\hat{\mathbf{3}},\mathbf{4})

\displaystyle=

(7.40)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{\epsilon}\left(\dfrac{\kappa}{2}\right)^{\!3}\dfrac{m_{A}^{2}}{m_{B}^{2s_{B}-2}(p_{1}+p_{3})^{2}}\,\times

\displaystyle\times\Biggl\{\dfrac{1}{4p_{5}\cdot p_{2}}\biggl(\hat{I}^{\prime\prime}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\left(\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{2}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\right)^{\!2}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}+2\,\hat{I}^{\prime}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{2}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\tilde{d}_{2}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}\,+

\displaystyle+\hat{I}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\,\tilde{d}_{2}\left(\tilde{d}_{2}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}\right)+\hat{I}^{\prime\prime}_{+}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\left(\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{2}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\right)^{\!2}\langle\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}\rangle^{2s_{B}}\biggr)\,+

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4p_{5}\cdot p_{4}}\biggl(\hat{I}^{\prime\prime}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\left(\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{4}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\right)^{\!2}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}+2\,\hat{I}^{\prime}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{4}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\tilde{d}_{4}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}\,+

\displaystyle+\hat{I}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\,\tilde{d}_{4}\left(\tilde{d}_{4}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}\right)+\hat{I}^{\prime\prime}_{+}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\left(\tilde{\mathcal{D}}_{4}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\right)^{\!2}\langle\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}\rangle^{2s_{B}}\biggr)\Biggr\}\,=

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{\epsilon}\left(\dfrac{\kappa}{2}\right)^{\!3}\dfrac{m_{A}^{2}}{m_{B}^{2s_{B}-2}(p_{1}+p_{3})^{2}}\,\times

\displaystyle\times\Biggl\{\Biggl[\frac{1}{16p_{5}\cdot p_{2}}\left(1+\dfrac{[5p_{3}p_{2}5]}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}\right)^{\!\!2}+\dfrac{1}{16p_{5}\cdot p_{4}}\left(\dfrac{[5p_{3}p_{4}5]}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}\right)^{\!\!2}\Biggr]\left([5\mathbf{1}]\langle\mathbf{1}\mathbf{2}\rangle[\mathbf{2}5]\right)^{2}\times

\displaystyle\times\left(\hat{I}^{\prime\prime}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}+\hat{I}^{\prime\prime}_{+}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\langle\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}\rangle^{2s_{B}}\right)\,+

\displaystyle+\Biggl[\dfrac{1}{4p_{5}\cdot p_{2}}\left(1+\dfrac{[5p_{3}p_{2}5]}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}\right)-\dfrac{1}{4p_{5}\cdot p_{4}}\dfrac{[5p_{3}p_{4}5]}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}\Biggr][5\mathbf{1}]\langle\mathbf{1}\mathbf{2}\rangle[\mathbf{2}5]\,\times

\displaystyle\times\hat{I}^{\prime}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\left(\tilde{d}_{2}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}\right)\,+

\displaystyle+\left[\dfrac{1}{4p_{5}\cdot p_{2}}+\dfrac{1}{4p_{5}\cdot p_{4}}\right]\hat{I}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\left(\tilde{d}_{2}(\tilde{d}_{2}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}})\right)\Biggr\}

Substituting now Eq. (
7.25
), Eq. (
7.34
) and Eq. (
7.40
) in Eq. (
7.16
), we finally obtain the five-point amplitude in the soft expansion up to sub-sub-leading order:

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{0,\epsilon}_{5}(\mathbf{1},\mathbf{2},\mathbf{3},\mathbf{4},5_{h}^{+})

\displaystyle=\left(\dfrac{\kappa}{2}\right)^{\!3}\dfrac{m_{A}^{2}}{m_{B}^{2s_{B}-2}(p_{1}+p_{3})^{2}}\,\times

(7.41)

\displaystyle\times\Biggl\{\dfrac{1}{\epsilon^{3}}\sum_{l=1}^{4}\frac{\langle yp_{l}5]^{2}}{(2p_{5}\cdot p_{l})\langle 5y\rangle^{2}}\left(\hat{I}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}+\hat{I}_{+}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\langle\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}\rangle^{2s_{B}}\right)\,+

\displaystyle+\dfrac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\Biggl\{\left[\dfrac{\langle yp_{2}5]}{(4p_{5}\cdot p_{2})\langle 5y\rangle}\left(1+\dfrac{[5p_{3}p_{2}5]}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}\right)+\dfrac{\langle yp_{4}5]}{(4p_{5}\cdot p_{4})\langle 5y\rangle}\dfrac{[5p_{3}p_{4}5]}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}\right][5\mathbf{1}]\langle\mathbf{1}\mathbf{2}\rangle[\mathbf{2}5]\,\times

\displaystyle\times\left(\hat{I}^{\prime}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}+\hat{I}^{\prime}_{+}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\langle\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}\rangle^{2s_{B}}\right)\,+

\displaystyle+\left[\dfrac{\langle yp_{2}5]}{(2p_{5}\cdot p_{2})\langle 5y\rangle}-\dfrac{\langle yp_{4}5]}{(2p_{5}\cdot p_{4})\langle 5y\rangle}\right]\hat{I}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\,\big(\tilde{d}_{2}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}\big)\Biggr\}\,+

\displaystyle+\dfrac{1}{\epsilon}\Biggl\{\Biggl[\frac{1}{16p_{5}\cdot p_{2}}\left(1+\dfrac{[5p_{3}p_{2}5]}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}\right)^{\!\!2}+\dfrac{1}{16p_{5}\cdot p_{4}}\left(\dfrac{[5p_{3}p_{4}5]}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}\right)^{\!\!2}\Biggr]\left([5\mathbf{1}]\langle\mathbf{1}\mathbf{2}\rangle[\mathbf{2}5]\right)^{2}\times

\displaystyle\times\left(\hat{I}^{\prime\prime}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}+\hat{I}^{\prime\prime}_{+}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\langle\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}\rangle^{2s_{B}}\right)\,+

\displaystyle+\Biggl[\dfrac{1}{4p_{5}\cdot p_{2}}\left(1+\dfrac{[5p_{3}p_{2}5]}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}\right)-\dfrac{1}{4p_{5}\cdot p_{4}}\dfrac{[5p_{3}p_{4}5]}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}\Biggr][5\mathbf{1}]\langle\mathbf{1}\mathbf{2}\rangle[\mathbf{2}5]\,\times

\displaystyle\times\hat{I}^{\prime}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\left(\tilde{d}_{2}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}\right)\,+

\displaystyle+\left[\dfrac{1}{4p_{5}\cdot p_{2}}+\dfrac{1}{4p_{5}\cdot p_{4}}\right]\hat{I}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\left(\tilde{d}_{2}(\tilde{d}_{2}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}})\right)\Biggr\}\Biggr\}\,.

7.2.3
Classical large spin limit

We are now interested in taking the large classical spin limit of the
amplitude Eq. (
7.41
) in order to compute a sensible amplitude.
I will follow the works presented in Ref.
[
100
,
103
]
and discussed in Sec.
4.4
, and extend it to the five-point amplitude.

We recall that a consistent picture of spin-induced multipoles of a pointlike particle must be formulated in the particle’s rest frame and that
all spin multipoles of the amplitude can be extracted through a finite Lorentz boost which is needed to bridge the gap between two states with different momenta.
To start, we note that any two four-vectors
p_{4}
and
p_{2}
of equal mass
m
may be related by the spinorial transformations:

\displaystyle|\mathbf{4}^{b}\rangle

\displaystyle=U_{24~a}^{~~b}\exp\!\left(\dfrac{i}{m^{2}}p_{2}^{\mu}q^{\nu}\sigma_{\mu\nu}\!\right)|\mathbf{2}^{a}\rangle\,,\qquad|\mathbf{4}^{b}]

\displaystyle=U_{24~a}^{~~b}\exp\!\left(\dfrac{i}{m^{2}}p_{2}^{\mu}q^{\nu}\bar{\sigma}_{\mu\nu}\!\right)|\mathbf{2}^{a}]\,,

(7.42)

where
U_{24}\text{in}SU(2)
is a little-group transformation
that depends on the specifics of the massive-spinor realization,
q
is the boost
q\equiv p_{2}+p_{4}
needed to connect the two spinors.
Note that
q^{2}=(p_{2}+p_{4})^{2}=0
as we are working in the on-shell framework and we are in the center of mass frame of the interactions.

Furthermore, using Eq. (
4.74
), we write the above
exponents as:

\frac{i}{m^{2}}p_{2}^{\mu}q^{\nu}\sigma_{\mu\nu,\alpha}^{~~~~\;\beta}=q\cdot(a_{B})_{\alpha}^{~\beta},\qquad\frac{i}{m^{2}}p_{2}^{\mu}q^{\nu}\bar{\sigma}^{~~~\dot{\alpha}}_{\mu\nu,~\dot{\beta}}=-q\cdot(a_{B})^{\dot{\alpha}}_{~\,\dot{\beta}}\,

(7.43)

and using Eq. (
4.77
), we write

\displaystyle|\mathbf{4}\rangle^{\odot 2s_{B}}\!=e^{q\cdot a_{B}}\big\{U_{24}|\mathbf{2}\rangle\big\}^{\!\odot 2s_{B}}\,,\qquad|\mathbf{4}]^{\odot 2s_{B}}\!=e^{-q\cdot a_{B}}\big\{U_{24}|\mathbf{2}]\big\}^{\!\odot 2s_{B}}\,,

(7.44)

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{4}|^{\odot 2s_{B}}\!=\big\{U_{24}\langle\mathbf{2}|\big\}^{\!\odot 2s_{B}}e^{-q\cdot a_{B}}\,,\qquad\!\!\![\mathbf{4}|^{\odot 2s_{B}}\!=\big\{U_{24}\langle\mathbf{4}|\big\}^{\!\odot 2s_{B}}e^{q\cdot a_{B}}\,.

With this brief recap complete, we now have all the necessary tools to compute the classical limit of the amplitude in Eq. (
7.41
).

I begin by considering the terms

\displaystyle\lim_{s_{B}\to\infty}\langle\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}\rangle^{2s_{B}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{s_{B}\to\infty}\big\{U_{24}\langle\mathbf{2}|\big\}^{\odot 2s_{B}}e^{-q\cdot a_{B}}|\mathbf{2}\rangle^{\odot 2s_{B}}=

\displaystyle\qquad=e^{-q\cdot a_{B}}\lim_{s_{B}\to\infty}m_{B}^{2s_{B}}(U_{24})^{\odot 2s_{B}}\,,

(7.45a)

\displaystyle\lim_{s_{B}\to\infty}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{s_{B}\to\infty}\big\{U_{24}[\mathbf{2}|\big\}^{\odot 2s_{B}}e^{q\cdot a_{B}}|\mathbf{2}]^{\odot 2s_{B}}=

\displaystyle\qquad=e^{q\cdot a_{B}}\lim_{s_{B}\to\infty}m_{B}^{2s_{B}}(U_{24})^{\odot 2s_{B}}\,.

(7.45b)

We remark that, as expected, the mass-infinities (
m^{2s_{B}}
) in Eq. (
7.45a
), Eq. (
7.45b
) cancel out the terms (
m^{-2s_{B}}
) that appear in the amplitude Eq. (
7.41
); and that the remaining unitary factor of
(U_{24})^{\odot 2s_{B}}
parametrizes an arbitrary little-group transformation, and it cancels out in a GEV sense as in Sec.
4.4

The discussion about the terms
\tilde{d}_{l}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}
, and
\tilde{d}_{l}\left(\tilde{d}_{l}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}\right)
is more delicate. Consider

\lim_{s_{B}\to\infty}\tilde{d}_{2}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}=\lim_{s_{B}\to\infty}[5\mathbf{2}][5\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\chi}_{2}}][\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}\,,

(7.46)

we aim to express
\tilde{d}_{2}
in a more familiar way, extract it from the limit, and subsequently evaluate the limit.

I will use the relation

k_{\mu}\varepsilon_{k,\nu}^{+}J_{\mathbf{p}}^{\mu\nu}=-\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}[k\mathbf{p}][k\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\chi}_{p}}]\,.

(7.47)

I will focus on the
\tilde{d}_{2}
derivatives, the computation for the
\tilde{d}_{4}
is perfectly analogous.

\displaystyle\lim_{s_{B}\to\infty}\tilde{d}_{2}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}=[5\mathbf{2}][5\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\chi}_{2}}]\lim_{s_{B}\to\infty}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}=i\sqrt{2}\big(p_{5,\mu}\varepsilon_{5,\nu}^{+}J_{2}^{\mu\nu}\big)\lim_{s_{B}\to\infty}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}=

\displaystyle\hskip 79.6678pt=i\sqrt{2}\big(p_{5,\mu}\varepsilon_{5,\nu}^{+}J_{2}^{\mu\nu}\big)e^{q\cdot a_{B}}\lim_{s_{B}\to\infty}m_{B}^{2s_{B}}(U_{24})^{\odot 2s_{B}}

(7.48a)

\displaystyle\lim_{s_{B}\to\infty}\tilde{d}_{2}\left(\tilde{d}_{2}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}\right)=-2\big(p_{5,\mu}\varepsilon_{5,\nu}^{+}J_{2}^{\mu\nu}\big)^{2}e^{q\cdot a_{B}}\lim_{s_{B}\to\infty}m_{B}^{2s_{B}}(U_{24})^{\odot 2s_{B}}

(7.48b)

Using now Eq. (
D.6
), we can write:

k_{\mu}\varepsilon_{k,\nu}^{+}J^{\mu\nu}_{\mathbf{p}}=2i\,(a_{B}\cdot k)(p\cdot\varepsilon^{+})\,,

(7.49)

thus

\displaystyle\lim_{s_{B}\to\infty}\tilde{d}_{2}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}=-2\sqrt{2}(a_{B}\cdot k)(p_{2}\cdot\varepsilon_{5}^{+})e^{q\cdot a_{B}}\lim_{s_{B}\to\infty}m_{B}^{2s_{B}}(U_{24})^{\odot 2s_{B}}=

\displaystyle\hskip 79.6678pt=2\dfrac{\langle yp_{2}5]}{\langle 5y\rangle}(a_{B}\cdot k)e^{q\cdot a_{B}}\lim_{s_{B}\to\infty}m_{B}^{2s_{B}}(U_{24})^{\odot 2s_{B}}

(7.50a)

\displaystyle\lim_{s_{B}\to\infty}\tilde{d}_{2}\left(\tilde{d}_{2}[\mathbf{4}\mathbf{2}]^{2s_{B}}\right)=8(a_{B}\cdot k)^{2}(p_{2}\cdot\varepsilon_{5}^{+})^{2}e^{q\cdot a_{B}}\lim_{s_{B}\to\infty}m_{B}^{2s_{B}}(U_{24})^{\odot 2s_{B}}=

\displaystyle\hskip 105.2751pt=4\dfrac{\langle yp_{2}5]^{2}}{\langle 5y\rangle^{2}}(a_{B}\cdot k)^{2}e^{q\cdot a_{B}}\lim_{s_{B}\to\infty}m_{B}^{2s_{B}}(U_{24})^{\odot 2s_{B}}

(7.50b)

Substituting now Eq. (
7.45
) and Eq. (
7.50
) in Eq. (
7.41
) we finally obtain the classical 5-point amplitude in the in infinite spin limit, normalized in the GEV sense:

\displaystyle\Big\langle\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{0,\epsilon}_{5}(\mathbf{1},\mathbf{2},\mathbf{3},\mathbf{4},5_{h}^{+})\Big\rangle_{\!\text{cl}}

\displaystyle=\left(\dfrac{\kappa}{2}\right)^{\!3}\dfrac{m_{A}^{2}m_{B}^{2}}{(p_{1}+p_{3})^{2}}\,\times

(7.51)

\displaystyle\times\Biggl\{\dfrac{1}{\epsilon^{3}}\sum_{l=1}^{4}\frac{\langle yp_{l}5]^{2}}{(2p_{5}\cdot p_{l})\langle 5y\rangle^{2}}\left(\hat{I}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})e^{q\cdot a_{B}}+\hat{I}_{+}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})e^{-q\cdot a_{B}}\right)\,+

\displaystyle+\dfrac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\Biggl\{\left[\dfrac{\langle yp_{2}5]}{(4p_{5}\cdot p_{2})\langle 5y\rangle}\left(1+\dfrac{[5p_{3}p_{2}5]}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}\right)+\dfrac{\langle yp_{4}5]}{(4p_{5}\cdot p_{4})\langle 5y\rangle}\dfrac{[5p_{3}p_{4}5]}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}\right][5\mathbf{1}]\langle\mathbf{1}\mathbf{2}\rangle[\mathbf{2}5]\,\times

\displaystyle\times\left(\hat{I}^{\prime}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})e^{q\cdot a_{B}}+\hat{I}^{\prime}_{+}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})e^{-q\cdot a_{B}}\right)\,+

\displaystyle+2\dfrac{\langle yp_{2}5]}{\langle 5y\rangle}\left[\dfrac{\langle yp_{2}5]}{(2p_{5}\cdot p_{2})\langle 5y\rangle}-\dfrac{\langle yp_{4}5]}{(2p_{5}\cdot p_{4})\langle 5y\rangle}\right]\hat{I}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})\,(a_{B}\cdot k)e^{q\cdot a_{B}}\Biggr\}\,+

\displaystyle+\dfrac{1}{\epsilon}\Biggl\{\Biggl[\frac{1}{16p_{5}\cdot p_{2}}\left(1+\dfrac{[5p_{3}p_{2}5]}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}\right)^{\!\!2}+\dfrac{1}{16p_{5}\cdot p_{4}}\left(\dfrac{[5p_{3}p_{4}5]}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}\right)^{\!\!2}\Biggr]\left([5\mathbf{1}]\langle\mathbf{1}\mathbf{2}\rangle[\mathbf{2}5]\right)^{2}\times

\displaystyle\times\left(\hat{I}^{\prime\prime}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})e^{q\cdot a_{B}}+\hat{I}^{\prime\prime}_{+}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})e^{-q\cdot a_{B}}\right)\,+

\displaystyle+2\dfrac{\langle yp_{2}5]}{\langle 5y\rangle}\Biggl[\dfrac{1}{4p_{5}\cdot p_{2}}\left(1+\dfrac{[5p_{3}p_{2}5]}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}\right)-\dfrac{1}{4p_{5}\cdot p_{4}}\dfrac{[5p_{3}p_{4}5]}{[5p_{1}p_{3}5]}\Biggr][5\mathbf{1}]\langle\mathbf{1}\mathbf{2}\rangle[\mathbf{2}5]\,\times

\displaystyle\times\hat{I}^{\prime}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})(a_{B}\cdot k)e^{q\cdot a_{B}}\,+

\displaystyle+4\dfrac{\langle yp_{2}5]^{2}}{\langle 5y\rangle^{2}}\left[\dfrac{1}{4p_{5}\cdot p_{2}}+\dfrac{1}{4p_{5}\cdot p_{4}}\right]\hat{I}_{-}(\hat{p}_{1}\cdot p_{2})(a_{B}\cdot k)^{2}e^{q\cdot a_{B}}\Biggr\}\Biggr\}\,.

This result is consistent with the one presented in section 4.4 of Ref.
[
115
]
for the scattering of two classical black holes, found via the standard BCFW approach.

8
Spinning waveforms from KMOC at leading order

In this section, I employ the methods discussed in Sec.
3
to compute the strain at future null infinity. At leading order, the only relevant amplitude is the five-point amplitude. While it may seem straightforward to simply substitute this amplitude and compute the observable, this naive approach proves difficult to apply in practice. A more efficient strategy is the one used in Ref.
[
114
]
:
we factorize the five-point amplitude into the three- and four-point amplitudes obtained in Sec.
4
and Sec.
6
, respectively.

Let us start with a brief recap of the results obtained in Sec.
3
.

The strain at future null infinity is:

\displaystyle h(x)=\frac{\kappa}{8\pi|\vec{x}\;\!|}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\hat{\rm{d}}\omega\,\Big[W(b;k^{-})e^{-i\omega u}+\left[W(b;k^{+})\right]^{*}e^{i\omega u}\Big]\,,

(8.1)

where
k^{\mu}=\omega n^{\mu}=\omega(1,\hat{x})
with
\hat{x}=\vec{x}/\lvert\vec{x}\rvert
,
u=x^{0}-\lvert\vec{x}\rvert
is the retarded time,
W(b;k^{\pm})
is the helicity-dependent
spectral waveform
of the emitted gravitational wave and
b
is the impact parameter.

We recall that, for the classical scattering of two massive particles, the spectral waveform can be computed from the S-matrix

iW(b,k^{h})=\biggl\langle\!\!\!\biggl\langle\int\mathrm{d}\mu\,e^{i(q_{1}\cdot b_{1}+q_{2}\cdot b_{2})}\ \mathcal{I}_{a_{h}}\biggr\rangle\!\!\!\biggr\rangle\,,

(8.2)

where the double-angle brackets enforce the
classical limit of the expression inside, and we have defined

\displaystyle\hat{\delta}^{(D)}(q_{1}+q_{2}-k)\,\mathcal{I}_{a_{h}}=\langle p_{1}^{\prime}p_{2}^{\prime}|S^{\dagger}a_{h}(k)S|p_{1}p_{2}\rangle\,,

(8.3a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\mu=\left[\prod_{i=1,2}\hat{\mathrm{d}}^{D}q_{i}\ \hat{\delta}(-2{p}_{i}\cdot q_{i}+q_{i}^{2})\right]\hat{\delta}^{D}(q_{1}+q_{2}-k)\,,

(8.3b)

and
q_{i}=p_{i}-p_{i}^{\prime}
.

At leading order in perturbation theory, we have

W^{(0)}(b,k^{h})=\int\mathrm{d}\mu\,e^{i(q_{1}\cdot b_{1}+q_{2}\cdot b_{2})}\,\mathcal{M}_{5,\rm cl}^{(0)}(q_{1},q_{2},k^{h})\,,

(8.4)

where
\mathcal{M}_{5,\rm cl}^{(0)}(q_{1},q_{2},k^{h})
denotes the classical tree-level scattering amplitude.

It is now useful to perform the following manipulation to set
b_{1}=b
and
b_{2}=0
. We are interested in the strain at future null infinity so we will eventually consider the waveform in the time domain; notice now that

\displaystyle\int\hat{\mathrm{d}}\omega\,e^{-i\omega u}e^{i(q_{1}\cdot b_{1}+q_{2}\cdot b_{2})}\,\hat{\delta}^{D}(q_{1}+q_{2}-k)=\int\hat{\mathrm{d}}\omega\,e^{-i\omega u}e^{i(q_{1}\cdot b_{1}+k\cdot b_{2}-q_{1}\cdot b_{2})}\,\hat{\delta}^{D}(q_{1}+q_{2}-k)=

(8.5)

\displaystyle=\int\hat{\mathrm{d}}\omega\,e^{-i\omega u}e^{iq_{1}\cdot(b_{1}-b_{2})}e^{i\omega n\cdot b_{2}}\,\hat{\delta}^{D}(q_{1}+q_{2}-k)=\int\hat{\mathrm{d}}\omega\,e^{-i\omega(u-n\cdot b_{2})}e^{iq_{1}\cdot(b_{1}-b_{2})}\,\hat{\delta}^{D}(q_{1}+q_{2}-k)\,,

We can now perform the translation
u\to u+n\cdot b_{2}
and define
b=b_{1}-b_{2}
to obtain

\int\hat{\mathrm{d}}\omega\,e^{-i\omega u}e^{iq_{1}\cdot b}\,\hat{\delta}^{D}(q_{1}+q_{2}-k)\,,

(8.6)

From this discussion, we conclude that for the purpose of the computation, we can set
b_{1}=b
and
b_{2}=0
; the symmetric result can be recovered later by applying the appropriate translation at the level of the time-domain waveform.

We are now ready to compute Eq. (
8.4
)
D=4
dimensions.

Let us start by integrating out
q_{2}
using the momentum-conserving delta function.
Recalling that in the classical limit, at leading order, we can neglect the shift
q_{i}^{2}
in the delta functions of Eq. (
8.3b
), we have

\displaystyle W^{(0)}(b,k^{h})

\displaystyle=\int\hat{\mathrm{d}}^{4}q_{1}\hat{\mathrm{d}}^{4}q_{2}\,\hat{\delta}(-2{p}_{1}\cdot q_{1})\hat{\delta}(-2{p}_{2}\cdot q_{2})\hat{\delta}^{4}(q_{1}+q_{2}-k)\,e^{i(q_{1}\cdot b_{1}+q_{2}\cdot b_{2})}\,\mathcal{M}_{5,\rm cl}^{(0)}(q_{1},q_{2},k^{h})=

(8.7)

\displaystyle=\int\hat{\mathrm{d}}^{4}q_{1}\,\hat{\delta}(-2{p}_{1}\cdot q_{1})\hat{\delta}\big(-2{p}_{2}\cdot(k-q_{1})\big)e^{iq_{1}\cdot b}\,\mathcal{M}_{5,\rm cl}^{(0)}(q_{1},q_{2},k^{h})

It is now useful to parameterize the remaining integration variable
q_{1}
as

\displaystyle q_{1}=z_{1}v_{1}+z_{2}v_{2}+z_{v}\tilde{v}+z_{b}\tilde{b}\,,

(8.8)

with

\displaystyle v_{1}^{\mu}=\frac{p_{1}^{\mu}}{m_{1}}\,,\quad v_{2}^{\mu}=\frac{p_{2}^{\mu}}{m_{2}}\,,\quad\tilde{v}^{\mu}=\frac{v^{\mu}}{\sqrt{-v^{2}}}\,,\quad\tilde{b}^{\mu}=\frac{b^{\mu}}{\sqrt{-b^{2}}}\,,

(8.9)

where
p_{i}
are the incoming momenta of the scattered objects,
and

\displaystyle v^{\mu}=\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}v_{1\nu}v_{2\rho}\tilde{b}_{\sigma}\,,\quad v^{2}=-(\gamma^{2}-1)\,,\quad\gamma=v_{1}\cdot v_{2}\,.

(8.10)

Furthermore, if we identify
b
to be the asymptotic impact parameter, we also have
b\cdot v_{1}=b\cdot v_{2}=0
.

Let us now focus on the measure
d\mu
and see how it transforms under this manipulation.
Under the change of variables Eq. (
8.8
), the Jacobian of the transformation is

\det J=|\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\tilde{v}^{\mu}v_{1}^{\nu}v_{2}^{\rho}\tilde{b}^{\sigma}|=\sqrt{\gamma^{2}-1}\,,

(8.11)

thus

\mathrm{d}^{4}q_{1}=\sqrt{\gamma^{2}-1}\,\mathrm{d}z_{1}\mathrm{d}z_{2}\mathrm{d}z_{v}\mathrm{d}z_{b}\,.

(8.12)

Let us now see how the
\delta
-s transform after the change of variables of Eq. (
8.8
):

\displaystyle-2p_{1}\cdot q_{1}=-2z_{1}(p_{1}\cdot v_{1})-2z_{2}(p_{1}\cdot v_{2})-2z_{v}(p_{1}\cdot\tilde{v})-2z_{b}(p_{1}\cdot\tilde{b})=

\displaystyle\hskip 49.79231pt=-2m_{1}(z_{1}+z_{2}\gamma)

(8.13a)

\displaystyle-2p_{2}\cdot(k-q_{1})=-2m_{2}(v_{2}\cdot k)+2m_{2}(\gamma z_{1}+z_{2})=-2m_{2}\omega_{2}+2m_{2}(\gamma z_{1}+z_{2})\,,

(8.13b)

where we have defined

\displaystyle w_{1}=v_{1}\cdot k\,,\qquad w_{2}=v_{2}\cdot k\,.

(8.14)

Using now the property of the delta function:
\delta(ax)=\frac{1}{|a|}\delta(x)
; the measure becomes

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\mu

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{\gamma^{2}-1}\,\mathrm{d}z_{1}\mathrm{d}z_{2}\mathrm{d}z_{v}\mathrm{d}z_{b}}{(4\pi)^{2}m_{1}m_{2}}\,\delta\left(z_{1}+\gamma z_{2}\right)\delta\left(\omega_{2}-\gamma z_{1}-z_{2}\right)=

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{\gamma^{2}-1}\,\mathrm{d}z_{1}\mathrm{d}z_{2}\mathrm{d}z_{v}\mathrm{d}z_{b}}{(4\pi)^{2}m_{1}m_{2}}\,\delta\left(z_{1}+\gamma z_{2}\right)\delta\left((\gamma^{2}-1)z_{2}+\omega_{2}\right)\,,

(8.15)

Altogether, Eq. (
8.4
) becomes

\displaystyle W^{(0)}(b,k^{h})

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}z_{1}\mathrm{d}z_{2}\mathrm{d}z_{v}\mathrm{d}z_{b}\,e^{-iz_{b}\sqrt{-b^{2}}}\delta\left(z_{1}+\gamma z_{2}\right)\delta\left((\gamma^{2}-1)z_{2}+\omega_{2}\right)\times

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\sqrt{\gamma^{2}-1}\,\frac{{\mathcal{M}}_{5,\rm cl}^{(0)}(q_{1},q_{2}.k^{h})}{(4\pi)^{2}m_{1}m_{2}}\,.

(8.16)

We may now use the two delta functions to integrate out
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
, and obtain

\displaystyle W^{(0)}(b,k^{h})

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}z_{v}\mathrm{d}z_{b}\,e^{-iz_{b}\sqrt{-b^{2}}}\frac{\hat{\mathcal{M}}_{5,\rm cl}^{(0)}}{(4\pi)^{2}m_{1}m_{2}\sqrt{\gamma^{2}-1}}\ ,

(8.17)

where

\hat{\mathcal{M}}_{5,\rm cl}^{(0)}=\left.\mathcal{M}_{5,\rm cl}^{(0)}(q_{1},q_{2},k^{h})\right|_{z_{1}=\frac{\gamma}{\gamma^{2}-1}w_{2}\,,\ z_{2}=-\frac{1}{\gamma^{2}-1}w_{2}}\,.

(8.18)

We are now left to evaluate this integral.
Let us start by evaluating the
z_{v}
integral.
To accomplish this we deform the contour of integration from the real axis to infinity through the upper half-plane (UHP) (or equivalently the LHP) as in Fig. (
8
).

Figure 8
:
The deformation of the contour
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathcal{C}^{(+)}}
in the complex
z_{v}
plane allows to evaluate the integral of the five-point tree-level amplitude directly in terms of the factorization channels. Figure reproduced from Ref.
[
114
]
.

Thanks to the Riemann-Lebesgue’s lemma it is possible to show that the principal value

15
15

15

Notice that we can consider the principal value of the integral without loss of generality, as, by the Sokhotski–Plemelj theorem, the integral and its principal value differ by a term proportional to a delta function. This delta function is proportional to non-negative powers of
z_{b}
, which, when substituted into Eq. (
8.1
), result in terms that integrate to short-range interactions,
\delta^{(n)}(\sqrt{-b^{2}})
, and are thus irrelevant in the classical approximation.

of the integral over the dotted half-circumference vanishes in the limit of infinite radius;
hence, using the Cauchy theorem, we have

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\mathrm{d}z_{v}\,\hat{\mathcal{M}}_{5,\rm cl}^{(0)}(q_{1},q_{2},k^{\lambda})=\int_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\mathcal{C}^{(+)}}}\!\mathrm{d}z_{v}\,\hat{\mathcal{M}}_{5,\rm cl}^{(0)}(q_{1},q_{2},k^{\lambda})\equiv I^{\lambda}_{\text{UHP}}

(8.19)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow I^{\lambda}_{\text{UHP}}=2\pi i\sum_{i}\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{z=z_{i}}\hat{\mathcal{M}}_{5,\rm cl}^{(0)}(q_{1},q_{2},k^{\lambda})

Let us now study the poles of this integrand.
